Limit law for root separation in random polynomials
Marcus Michelen, Oren Yakir

Abstract
Let f n f_{n} be a random polynomial of degree n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 whose coefficients are independent and identically distributed random variables. We study the separation distances between roots of f n f_{n} and prove that the set of these distances, normalized by n − 5 / 4 n^{-5/4} , converges in distribution as n → ∞ n\to\infty to a non-homogeneous Poisson point process. As a corollary, we deduce that the minimal separation distance between roots of f n f_{n} , normalized by n − 5 / 4 n^{-5/4} has a non-trivial limit law. In the course of the proof, we establish a related result which may be of independent interest: a Taylor series with random i.i.d. coefficients almost-surely does not have a double zero anywhere other than the origin.



1
Introduction

Consider the random polynomial

f_{n}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\xi_{k}z^{k}\,,

(1)

where
\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{n}
are i.i.d. random variables. This model, usually referred to as the

Kac polynomial
after Mark Kac
[
Kac43
]
, was studied in classical works by Bloch-Pólya
[
BP31
]
, Littlewood-Offord
[
LO38
]
, Salem-Zygmund
[
SZ54
]
, Erdős-Offord
[
EO56
]
, Konyagin-Schlag
[
KS99
]
, Tao-Vu
[
TV15
]
, and many more; this list is by no means complete. As the coefficients of (
1
) are random, it is natural to ask about the typical spatial distribution of the roots as the degree
n
grows large. The case when the coefficients follow the Gaussian distribution often leads to the most tractable analysis, with exact formulas for correlations of roots. It is widely believed (and only sometimes proved) that many properties of the random roots do not depend on the specific choice of coefficients distribution, as
n\to\infty
. This type of meta-conjecture is commonly referred to as the
universality
phenomena. In the special case of when
\xi_{0}
takes the values
\{-1,+1\}
with equal probability,
f_{n}
is called a random Littlewood polynomial in part due to the works
[
LO38
,
Lit66
]
. Although we expect similar asymptotic phenomena, studying the roots of a random Littlewood polynomial is, generally speaking, a more challenging task than its Gaussian counterpart.

A striking and now-classical fact
is that under mild integrability conditions on
\xi_{0}
, most of the roots of
f_{n}
tend to cluster uniformly around the unit circle as
n\to\infty
. Special cases of this phenomenon were shown by
Erdős-Turán
[
ET50
]
, Šparo-Šur
[
SS62
]
, Hammersley
[
Ham56
]
, and in great generality by the work of Ibragimov–Zaporozhets
[
IZ13
]
. On a finer scale, this clustering was shown by Shepp-Vanderbei
[
SV95
]
and Ibragimov-Zeitouni
[
IZ97
]
to hold on the scale of
n^{-1}
, meaning that most of the roots are present in the annulus of radius
\{1-K/n\leq|z|\leq 1+K/n\}
as soon as
K
is large but fixed and
n\to\infty
(see
[
MS20
,
Coo+23
]
for finer points on the distance of roots to the unit circle).

Additionally, the roots of random polynomials experience
repulsion
, although a precise quantitative statement of this is rather technical (see Figure
1
for a visual demonstration). In the case of Gaussian coefficients, this can be understood by looking at pair correlations. This amounts to asking for the probability density of having roots at points
z
and
w
and demonstrating decay as
z
and
w
become close. The work of Shiffman-Zelditch
[
SZ03
, Theorem 4]

demonstrated repulsion for the case of Gaussian coefficients and local universality results for random polynomials show that repulsion occurs for a wide range of coefficients (see, e.g.,
[
TV15
]
). Repulsion of roots of random polynomials has also been studied in the physics literature, see for example
[
BBL96
]
.

In this paper we address the following question:
What is the typical separation distance between the roots of
f_{n}
?
Throughout the paper, we will assume that
\xi_{0}
is a mean-zero, sub-Gaussian
1
1

1

That is,
\mathbb{P}\big[|\xi_{0}|\geq t\big]\leq 2\exp(-ct^{2})
for some
c>0
;
random variable.
Denote by
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
the (random) roots of
f_{n}
, taken with multiplicities and in an arbitrary order. Our main result is the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
f_{n}
be given by (
1
), and suppose
\xi_{0}
is a mean-zero, sub-Gaussian random variable satisfying
\mathbb{P}[\xi_{0}=0]=0
. Then the point process

\Big\{n^{5/4}\,|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}|\,:\,1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n\Big\}

converges in distribution (with respect to the vague topology) as
n\to\infty
to a non-homogeneous Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with intensity
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}t^{3}
, for some
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}>0
.

The value of
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}>0
from Theorem
1.1
is somewhat explicit and is given by (
69
) below. We remark that we may drop the assumption
\mathbb{P}[\xi_{0}=0]=0
, with the cost of adding more wording to our main result. Indeed, in the latter case the probability of having a double root at the origin is exactly
\mathbb{P}[\xi_{0}=0]^{2}
, so the limiting Poisson process is super-positioned by an independent unit delta-mass at the origin, which occurs independently of the Poisson process with probability
\mathbb{P}[\xi_{0}=0]^{2}
. To avoid this technicality, throughout the paper we adopt the assumption

\mathbb{P}\big[\xi_{0}=0\big]=0\,.

(2)

We also remark that with minor modifications of the proof, Theorem
1.1
continues to hold even if
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{0}]\not=0
, but to simplify the presentation we adopt the mean-zero assumption throughout the paper.

1.1
Heuristic explanation for the scaling exponent

The scaling exponent
5/4
in Theorem
1.1
reflects the repulsion between the random roots. To better explain this point, we consider a simple toy model where we pretend the roots of
f_{n}
do not interact. Suppose that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are i.i.d. random variables, all uniformly distributed in the annulus

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{K}=\Big\{1-\frac{K}{n}\leq|z|\leq 1+\frac{K}{n}\Big\}

for some
K\geq 1
large but fixed. We recall that if
K
is a large constant, then the vast majority of roots of
f_{n}
lie in this annulus
[
SV95
,
IZ97
]
, and so this is indeed a toy model for the roots of
f_{n}
without interaction between roots.
Note that for any
1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n
we have

\mathbb{P}\big[|X_{j}-X_{j^{\prime}}|\leq{\varepsilon}\big]\approx n{\varepsilon}^{2}\,,

since the disk of radius
{\varepsilon}
around
X_{j}
is of area
\approx{\varepsilon}^{2}
, and
\mathcal{A}
has area
\approx n^{-1}
. Linearity of expectation then shows

\mathbb{E}\Big[\#\big\{\text{pairs }j\not=j^{\prime}\ \text{such that }X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}\ \text{are }{\varepsilon}\text{-close}\big\}\Big]\approx n^{3}{\varepsilon}^{2}\,,

and hence, we expect some non-trivial scaling limit when
{\varepsilon}=n^{-3/2}
. Let us now contrast this with the roots
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
of the random polynomial
f_{n}
given by (
1
). Letting
\mathbb{D}(x,{\varepsilon})
denote the disk of radius
{\varepsilon}>0
centered at
x\in\mathcal{A}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\#\big\{j:X_{j}\in\mathbb{D}(x,{\varepsilon})\big\}\Big]\approx(n{\varepsilon})^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{E}\Big[\#\big\{j:\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{D}(x,{\varepsilon})\big\}\Big]\approx(n{\varepsilon})^{2}\,.

In other words, the first intensity of the point process
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
is comparable to that of random roots. The key difference between the i.i.d. points
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and the random roots
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
comes in at the
2
-point intensity (sometimes referred to in the literature as the pair correlations). By independence, for
{\varepsilon}\ll n^{-1}
, one can compute

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists j\not=j^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ X_{j},\,X_{j^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{D}(x,{\varepsilon})\Big]\approx n^{2}\times\big(n{\varepsilon}^{2}\big)^{2}\,.

(3)

In contrast, for random roots one has

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\ \text{two distinct roots of }f_{n}\in\mathbb{D}(x,{\varepsilon})\Big]\approx n^{2}\times\big(n{\varepsilon}^{2}\big)^{2}\times(n{\varepsilon})^{2}\,,

(4)

instead of (
3
). The extra
(n{\varepsilon})^{2}
term in (
4
) can be thought of as a repulsion term between the roots, see Figure
1
for a related simulation.

Figure 1:
Left: roots on the unit circle of
\text{Re}(f_{n})
for random Littlewood
f_{n}
(red points); Right: the same number of i.i.d. uniform points on the unit circle (blue points).

When the coefficients are Gaussian, one can formally derive (
4
) via the Kac-Rice formula for the 2-point function of the random roots, see for example
[
Hou+09
, Chapter 3.4]
or
[
SZ03
, Theorem 4]
. One of the main achievements of this paper is obtaining the asymptotic (
4
) for a large class of coefficients distribution
for
{\varepsilon}
which is polynomially small in
n
, including in the Littlewood case (see Section
1.4
below, and (
8
) in particular). Once (
4
) is established, union bounding over an
{\varepsilon}
-net of
\mathcal{A}
of size
n^{-1}{\varepsilon}^{-2}
yields

\mathbb{E}\Big[\#\big\{\text{pairs of roots of }f_{n}\ \text{which are }{\varepsilon}\text{-close}\big\}\Big]\approx n^{5}{\varepsilon}^{4}\,,

and hence the scaling
{\varepsilon}=n^{-5/4}
is a natural one for our problem.

1.2
Double roots of random polynomials

Recalling that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
are the roots of
f_{n}
given by (
1
), we denote by

m_{n}=\min\big\{|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}|\,:\,1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n\big\}

the distance between the closest distinct roots of
f_{n}
. Clearly,
m_{n}=0
if and only if
f_{n}
has a double root. An immediate consequence of Theorem
1.1
is the following limit law for the minimal separation between the roots.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
f_{n}
be given as in (
1
) with mean-zero and sub-Gaussian coefficients, and assume that (
2
) holds. Then for all
s\geq 0
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[n^{5/4}m_{n}\geq s\Big]=\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}}{4}\,s^{4}\Big)\,.

In particular, the probability that
f_{n}
has a double root tends to zero as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

For a Poisson process with intensity
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}t^{3}
, the number of points in
[0,s]
has a Poisson distribution with parameter

\int_{0}^{s}\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t=\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}}{4}s^{4}\,.

Hence, the probability there are no points present in this interval is
\exp\big(-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}}{4}\,s^{4}\big)
, and the corollary follows from Theorem
1.1
.
∎

For polynomials of the form (
1
) with random integer coefficients, the probability of having a double root has been studied in prior works. For random Littlewood polynomials, Peled, Sen and Zeitouni
[
PSZ16
]
computed the asymptotic of this probability as
n\to\infty
. In particular, they showed that the rate of decay depends on divisibility properties of the degree
n
. Later on, Feldheim and Sen
[
FS17
]
extended this result to more general integer-valued distributions
\xi_{0}
, and obtained similar findings. We remark that the problem of investigating the minimal gap between the roots was also suggested in
[
FS17
, Section 7]
. It is worth noting that the approach in both
[
PSZ16
,
FS17
]
are highly algebraic, with heavy use of the fact that the coefficients are integers. Additionally, a key assumption in both works is that the maximal atom of
\xi_{0}
has probability at most
1/\sqrt{3}
and that the
\xi_{0}
has bounded support. To the best of our knowledge, Corollary
1.2
appears to be the first result in the literature that establishes the absence of double roots with high probability for Kac polynomials for a wide class of non-degenerate coefficient distributions.

1.3
Double zeros of random analytic functions in the disk

En route of proving our main result Theorem
1.1
, we must address the following natural question:
Can a random analytic function in the disk
have a double zero?
Consider an infinite sequence of i.i.d. (possibly complex) random variables
\{a_{k}\}
, and assume that

\mathbb{E}\big[\log(1+|a_{0}|)\big]<\infty\,.

(5)

By applying the Borel-Cantelli lemmas, one can verify that the condition (
5
) is both necessary and sufficient for the random power series

F(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}z^{k}

(6)

to almost surely define a random analytic function in the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
.

Random analytic functions of the form (
6
) naturally emerge as distributional limits of random Kac polynomials on compact subsets of
\mathbb{D}
. In fact, Ibragimov and Zaporozhets
[
IZ13
]
showed that (
5
) is also necessary and sufficient for the clustering to the unit circle of the random roots of the corresponding Kac polynomial as the degree tends to infinity. The study of the behavior of the zeros of
F
has a rich history. Rather than providing a comprehensive overview, we refer the interested reader to the classical book of Kahane
[
Kah85
]
to learn more about this subject. Our focus here is on the question of double zeros of
F
, which leads to the following result, proved in Section
2
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
F
be a random Taylor series defined via (
6
),
assuming that (
5
) holds. Then

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{D}\ \text{such that }\,F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=0\Big]=\mathbb{P}\Big[F(0)=F^{\prime}(0)=0\Big]=\big(\mathbb{P}[a_{0}=0]\big)^{2}\,.

In the special case when
a_{0}
is Gaussian, Theorem
1.3
was proven in
[
PV05
, Lemma 28]
(see also
[
Hou+09
, Lemma 2.4.1]
). Among the main challenges of proving Theorem
1.3
is that the behavior of zeros of
F
away from the unit circle is
not
universal. As a basic example, if
a_{j}\in\{-1,1\}
for all
j
, then
F
has no roots of modulus less than
1/2
; conversely, in the Gaussian case
F
can have a root arbitrarily close to the origin. The proof of Theorem
1.3
is self-contained and we outline the argument more extensively in Section
2
. We also point out that Section
2
can be read independently from all other parts of the paper, yet is necessary for the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

1.4
Outline of the proof

We already mentioned that most of the roots of
f_{n}
are present in the annulus

\mathcal{A}_{K}=\Big\{1-\frac{K}{n}\leq|z|\leq 1+\frac{K}{n}\Big\}

as soon as
K
is large but fixed and
n\to\infty
. Indeed, the main contribution to the limiting Poisson process described in Theorem
1.1
is from pairs of roots from
\mathcal{A}_{K}
. Broadly speaking, the proof is split into three main steps:

(A)

Proving that the distances between the roots in
\mathcal{A}_{K}
converge to a Poisson point process at scale
n^{-5/4}
with intensity
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)t^{3}{\rm d}t
on non-negative reals
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as
n\to\infty
. We then also verify that
\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}=\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)
exists and is non-zero. See Theorem
3.2
for a precise formulation of this step.

(B)

Proving that roots which are present in
\big\{r_{0}\leq|z|\leq 1-\frac{K}{n}\big\}
do not contribute to the limiting Poisson point process described above, with high probability as
n\to\infty
followed by
K\to\infty
and then
r_{0}\to 1
. See Proposition
3.1
for a precise formulation of this step.

(C)

Finally, apply Theorem
1.3
to show there are no close roots in the disk
\{|z|\leq r_{0}\}
which contribute to the limiting process as
n\to\infty
, for all
r_{0}<1
fixed. This step follows from a simple monotonicity argument, while the proof of Theorem
1.3
is given in Section
2
.

We remark that since
f_{n}(z)
has the same law as the polynomial
z^{n}f_{n}(z^{-1})
, then steps (B) and (C) in the above program automatically take care of roots present in
\{|z|\geq 1+K/n\}
.

The main step in the above program is (A), and to explain it the reader can assume for the moment that
f_{n}
is in fact a Gaussian polynomial. The covariance kernel of
f_{n}
is given by

\mathbb{E}\big[f_{n}(z)\overline{f_{n}(w)}\big]=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(z\overline{w})^{k}=\frac{1-(z\overline{w})^{n+1}}{1-z\overline{w}}\,.

Since
\mathbb{E}[|f_{n}(z)|^{2}]\asymp n
for
z\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
, this implies in particular that for
z,w\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
for which
|z-w|=\omega(n^{-1})
, the Gaussian variables
f_{n}(z)
and
f_{n}(w)
are roughly independent. As we are after a “local statistic” (specifically, finding roots which are very close), a natural idea is to consider a sufficiently dense net of points, with the hope that the random vector
\big(f_{n}(z),f_{n}^{\prime}(z),f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)\big)
for a net point
z\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
will dictate whether or not there exist close roots nearby. The reason for considering the polynomial and its first two derivatives (and nothing more) is that under a typical event for third derivative, the polynomial is close to its quadratic approximation on scales
\ll n^{-1}
, which in principle can predict the existence of a pair of close roots. Although the above reasoning is formally correct, to get the asymptotic (
4
) we need to use the randomness to show that typically, solving the quadratic approximation is similar to solving two
linear approximations
. The latter is much better suited for computing small-ball probabilities.

To be more precise, we will consider a
\delta
-net of points in
\mathcal{A}_{K}
, where
\delta=n^{-5/4-\beta}
and
\beta>0
is small but fixed. Let
{\varepsilon}=n^{-5/4}
. To capture roots which are
{\varepsilon}
-separated, we will first use
(f_{n}(z),f_{n}^{\prime}(z))
to capture roots which are
\delta
-close to a given net point. By applying a standard linearization, we predict a root should occur near

\alpha=z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\,,

and hence

\Big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=0\Big\}\approx\Big\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\in\mathbb{D}(0,\delta)\Big\}

where by
\approx
we just mean that with high-probability both event happen (or don’t happen) simultaneously. Once we know there is a root
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)
, we will Taylor expand
at the root
, which gives the prediction for another root at

\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}\,.

On a typical event for close roots to occur, we have the asymptotic

\alpha^{\prime}\approx z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}

(see Lemma
5.7
) and by neglecting multiplicative constants, we conclude that

\bigg\{\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)\,,\exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(z,{\varepsilon})\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\bigg\}\approx\bigg\{\Big|\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\Big|\leq\delta\,,\,\Big|\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\Big|\leq{\varepsilon}\bigg\}\,.

(7)

For Gaussian polynomials, one can compute to first order the probability of the event on the right-hand side of (
7
) (see Section
10
) and get that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\,\Big|\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\Big|\leq\delta\,,\,\Big|\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\Big|\leq{\varepsilon}\bigg]\approx{\varepsilon}^{4}n^{6}\delta^{2}=n^{-3/2-2\beta}\,.

(8)

Since the number of net points is
n^{-1}\delta^{-2}=n^{3/2+2\beta}
, we get that the number of
{\varepsilon}
-separated roots is of constant order. The full Poisson limit follows from the method of moments, so we need to compute the probability of the event (
7
) intersected over finite collections of net points in
\mathcal{A}_{K}
. We also remark that if a net point predicts two close roots nearby then all other net points of distance
o(n^{-1})
are likely not to predict two close roots (see Lemma
5.8
). Therefore, the contribution from the net sum to the set of close roots are asymptotically independent, which is crucial for a Poisson limit to hold.

For non-Gaussian polynomials, the asymptotic (
8
) is not true for certain points
z\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
with bad arithmetic properties. As a toy example, consider the (much simpler) event that
\{|f_{n}(z)|\leq\sqrt{n}\gamma\}
for some small
\gamma>0
. When the coefficients are Gaussian and
z\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
, then one has
f_{n}(z)/\sqrt{n}
is a (uniformly non-degenerate) two-dimensional Gaussian and so one anticipates

\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq\sqrt{n}\gamma\big]\asymp\gamma^{2}

(9)

for all
z\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
(away from the real axis) and all
\gamma>0
. On the other hand, in the case the coefficients are, say, uniformly distributed in
\{-1,0,1\}
, one can show that

\mathbb{P}\big[f_{n}(e^{i\pi/2})=0\big]\asymp\frac{1}{n}\,.

That is, at the point
e^{i\pi/2}\in\mathcal{A}_{K}
the Gaussian heuristic (
9
) fails for small
\gamma
. If we have any hope of proving (
8
), which handles a much more complicated event than (
9
), we need to remove poorly-behaved points such as
e^{i\pi/2}
. By now, it is well-understood in the random polynomial literature that the key property of
e^{i\pi/2}
that causes the failure of (
9
) is that its argument is close to a rational multiple of
\pi
with small denominator (of course,
\frac{\pi}{2}
is genuinely equal to such a multiple).

The relationship between arithmetic structure and small-ball probabilities is a recurring theme in the study of both random polynomials and random matrices. Investigation into this relationship goes back at least to Halász
[
Hal77
]
and is now often referred to as the
inverse Littlewood-Offord problem
(see, e.g.,
[
TV10
,
NV13
]
). We will need to remove these points with bad arithmetic properties from our net and prove (
8
) for
smooth points
(see Definition
3.8
). In turn, this follows from proving a quantitative local Gaussian comparison for those smooth points (and for tuples of well-separated smooth points), see Theorem
3.12
. The analysis here is based on an idea by Konyagin and Schlag
[
KS99
]
which was later generalized substantially by Cook-Nguyen in
[
CN21
]
. For our application, we need to push the method a bit further, and prove the local Gaussian comparison also for points which are
not
on the unit circle (both
[
KS99
,
CN21
]
work only on the unit circle). Proving different versions of this local Gaussian comparison spans through Sections
7
,
8
and
9
of this paper.

To prove item (B) of our program, we follow similar steps as in item (A). The key observation is that in the annulus

\big\{r\leq|z|\leq r+\frac{1-r}{2}\big\}

the original polynomial
f_{n}
“behaves” effectively like a random Kac polynomial with degree
(1-r)^{-1}
. Therefore, to show there are no close roots in the annulus
\{r_{0}\leq|z|\leq 1-K/n\}
, we sum dyadically over concentric annuli, and show that the sum of contributions is arbitrary small as
r_{0}\to 1
and
K\to\infty
. En route of proving item (B), we will need to prove small ball probability bounds for the polynomial at arbitrary points
z\in\mathbb{D}
which takes into account the effective degree of the polynomial at the point
d_{n}(z)=\min\{n,(1-|z|)^{-1}\}
. These bounds are developed in Section
7
and Section
8
. As we want to “push” our results for discrete coefficients as well, we will again need to remove from consideration non-smooth points and obtain the desired small-ball bounds (of the form (
8
)) for smooth points, see Lemma
3.9
.

Finally, we need to complete item (C), that is, we need to deal with those roots which stay strictly inside the unit disk as
n\to\infty
. As there is no Gaussian-like behavior strictly inside the unit disk (unless, of course, the coefficients
\xi_{k}
are Gaussian) we need to come up with a different strategy. The idea is to consider the “full” Taylor series instead of the finite polynomial (that is,
n=\infty
) and apply a perturbation argument to show that this Taylor series (of the form (
6
)) does not have a double zero almost surely. This is precisely Theorem
1.3
, which we prove in Section
2
. We also refer the reader to the beginning of Section
2
for a more elaborate sketch of proof for Theorem
1.3
. Once Theorem
1.3
is established, we can argue that with high probability, all roots of
f_{n}
in the disk will be uniformly separated as
n\to\infty
, which concludes item (C).

1.5
Organization of the paper and notation

To ease the readability, we outline a bird’s eye view for each section below:

\circ

In Section
2
we give the proof of Theorem
1.3
concerning double roots of random power series. As we already mentioned, this section can be read independently of all other parts of the paper.

\circ

In Section
3
we state our program more precisely and formulate Proposition
3.1
and Theorem
3.2
described above. This is where we describe how to reduce the proof of Theorem
1.1
to a Poisson limit for a sum over a net. In this section, we state the relevant small-ball probability bounds/comparisons that will be used throughout the paper.

\circ

Section
4
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
3.1
which allows us to reduce our focus to the annulus
\mathcal{A}_{K}
for large but fixed
K
. This amounts to eliminating close pairs of roots that are more than distance
1/n
from the unit circle.

\circ

Section
5
is the starting point for our net argument described above. Here, we reduce the question of existence of close roots to a question about local events in
(f_{n},f_{n}^{\prime},f_{n}^{\prime\prime})
on an appropriately chosen net of points in the annulus
\mathcal{A}_{K}
.

\circ

In Section
6
we show that the sum over the net points indeed has a Poisson scaling limit, assuming that the small-ball probability results from Section
3
hold. From this point onward, the rest of the paper is devoted to proving these small-ball bounds.

\circ

In Section
7
we derive small-ball bounds which are valid to all sample points in
\mathbb{D}
. As such, these small-balls are relatively weak, and do not allow us to compute to first order events of the form (
7
).

\circ

In Section
8
we improve the small-ball bound from the previous section, in the case where the sample point
z\in\mathbb{D}
is
smooth
(i.e. has some favorable arithmetic property, see Definition
3.8
for a precise formulation). This improvement allows us to estimate the probability of (
8
) for the remaining sample points accurately.

\circ

In Section
9
we take one step further, and prove that small-ball probabilities for tuples of smooth, separated points in
\mathcal{A}_{K}
, scale as in the case of Gaussian coefficients. This type of Gaussian-comparison is crucial when computing moments of the sum of indicators over the net, and allow us to compute the actual intensity (and, in particular, pin-down the non-homogeneous effect) for the limiting Poisson point process.

\circ

Finally, in Section
10
we explicitly compute the limiting intensity for the Poisson point process. By the program described above, this task is reduced to computing the probability of the event (
8
) to first asymptotic order in the case of Gaussian coefficients.

We end the introduction by listing some notations that we will use freely across the paper:

•

\mathbb{C},\mathbb{R}
the complex plane and the real line. We will always identify
\mathbb{C}^{d}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
m
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(the dimension should be clear from the context);

•

\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the non-negative reals;
\mathbb{D}
the unit disk;
\mathbb{H}
the upper-half plane;

•

\mathbb{D}(z,r)
a disk centered at
z
with radius
r
;
\mathcal{A}(a,b)=\mathbb{D}(0,b)\setminus\mathbb{D}(0,a)
;

•

\xi_{0}
the coefficient distribution of the random polynomial
f_{n}
given by (
1
);
\varphi(t)=\mathbb{E}\big[\exp(it\xi_{0})\big]
the characteristic function of
\xi_{0}
;

•

For
z\in\mathbb{D}
, the
effective degree
of
f_{n}
at
z

d_{n}(z)=\min\big\{n,(1-|z|)^{-1}\big\};

(10)

•

\tau>\beta>0
are absolute, sufficiently small constants (taking
\tau=10^{-4}
and
\beta=\tau/20
is fine).

We will use freely the Landau notations
O(\cdot),o(\cdot),\omega(\cdot),\Theta(\cdot)
to denote inequalities up to non-asymptotic constants. We will also write
a\lesssim b
if
a=O(b)
.

2
Almost sure result in the disk: Proof of Theorem
1.3

Throughout this section we are concerned with the zeros of the random power series
F
defined at (
6
), whose i.i.d. coefficients satisfy (
5
).
To prove Theorem
1.3
, it is enough to show that for each
\delta>0
, almost surely there is no double zero of
F
in the annulus
\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)=\{\delta<|z|<1-\delta\}
.

The main idea is that if
F
has a double zero at some
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)
for which
|F^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})|>0
, then we decompose

F(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}a_{k}z^{k}+\sum_{k>m}a_{k}z^{k}=:P_{m}(z)+T_{m}(z)

and show that
P_{m}^{\prime}
has a root close to
z_{0}
on which
F
is atypically small (provided
m
is large enough). To omit the possibility of a double zero, we first reveal (i.e. condition on) the polynomial
P_{m}
. On the event that
F
has a double zero, it must be the case that among the finitely many roots of
P_{m}^{\prime}
, we have that
T_{m}
nearly cancels
P_{m}
. We show that this latter event is rare by using a basic anti-concentration fact due to Lévy-Kolmogorov-Rogozin (see Lemma
2.4
), which is a variant on Erdős solution to the Littlewood-Offord problem.

A wrinkle to this story is that for this argument to work, we need
|F^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})|>0
. To get around this, we observe that with a basic quasirandomness condition on the coefficients, there does not exists a zero with extremely high multiplicity, i.e. for a large
k
it is not likely for there to exist a point
z_{0}
for which

F(z_{0})=F^{\prime}(z_{0})=F^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})=\cdots=F^{(k)}(z_{0})=0\,.

See Claim
2.3
for a precise statement. We then let
k_{\ast}
be the smallest
k
for which there is positive probability that there is some zero of multiplicity
k_{\ast}
. If
k_{\ast}\geq 2
, then we run the above argument with
G=F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}
, and by the minimality of
k_{\ast}
we have that each root of
F
of multiplicity
k_{\ast}
must be a double root of
G
and have non-zero second derivative.

2.1
Preparations

In what follows, it will be convenient to assume bounds on the growth of the random coefficients, which will be shown to hold with high probability. For fixed constants
K,\beta>0
define the set of analytic functions in the disk

\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}=\bigg\{F(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}z^{k}:\,|a_{k}|\leq Ke^{\beta k}\ \text{for all }k\geq 0\bigg\}\,.

(11)

Claim 2.1
.

Let
\{a_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
be an i.i.d. sequence which satisfies (
5
). Then for each
\beta>0
and for all
{\varepsilon}>0
there exists
K\geq 1
large enough so that
\mathbb{P}\big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}\big]\geq 1-{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

By the moment assumption (
5
) on the random coefficients we have

\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}\big[|a_{k}|\geq e^{\beta k}\big]\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}\big[\log\big(1+|a_{0}|\big)\geq\beta k\big]\lesssim\beta^{-1}\mathbb{E}\log\big(1+|a_{0}|\big)<\infty\,.

Therefore, by the Borel-Cantelli lemma, the random variable
\max_{k\geq 0}|a_{k}|e^{-\beta k}
is finite almost surely. Taking
K
sufficiently large now gives

\mathbb{P}\big[\max_{k\geq 0}|a_{k}|e^{-\beta k}\geq K\big]\leq{\varepsilon}

as desired.
∎

For what follows, we would also like to omit functions with deep zeros at the origin. Let
c_{0}>0
be chosen so that
\tau=\mathbb{P}\big[|a_{0}|\geq c_{0}\big]>0
(this is possible as we assume that
a_{0}
is non-degenerate). For
\ell\geq 0
we define another set of analytic functions

\mathcal{F}_{\ell}=\bigg\{F(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}z^{k}:\,\exists j\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}\ \text{such that }|a_{j}|\geq c_{0}\bigg\}\,.

(12)

Claim 2.2
.

With the notations described above, for all
k\geq 0
and
\ell\geq 0
we have

\mathbb{P}\big[F^{(k)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}\big]\geq 1-e^{-\tau\ell}\,.

Proof.

Simply note that

\mathbb{P}\big[F^{(k)}\not\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}\big]\leq\mathbb{P}\big[|a_{k+j}|<c_{0}\ \text{for all }j=0,\ldots,\ell\,\big]=\Big(1-\mathbb{P}\big[|a_{0}|\geq c_{0}\big]\Big)^{\ell+1}

and the bound follows from the definition of
\tau=\mathbb{P}\big[|a_{0}|\geq c_{0}\big]
.
∎

We will make use of the Cauchy estimates which we now recall. For
0<s<r
and
F
an analytic function in the disk
r\mathbb{D}
, we have

\max_{z\in s\mathbb{D}}|F^{(k)}(z)|\leq\frac{k!}{(r-s)^{k}}\max_{w\in r\mathbb{D}}|F(w)|\,.

(13)

In the next claim, we upper bound the number of zeros in
(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}
of analytic functions from the set
\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}=\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
.

Claim 2.3
.

For each
K,\ell,\beta,\delta>0
such that
1-\delta/2<e^{-\beta}
there is a constant
M=M(K,\ell,\beta,\delta)
such that each function
h\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}
has at most
M
zeros (counting multiplicity) in
(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}
.

Proof.

Fix some analytic function in the disk
h
, and denote by
N
the number of zeros it has in
(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}
with multiplicities. The classical Jensen-type bound gives

N\leq C_{\delta}\log\frac{\max_{(1-\delta/2)\mathbb{D}}|h|}{\max_{(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}}|h|}\,.

(14)

For the reader’s convenience, we recall the proof of (
14
). Let
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{N}
be the multiple zeros of
h
and recall that the Blaschke factor is given by

B_{\zeta}(z)=\frac{\rho(z-\zeta)}{\rho^{2}-z\overline{\zeta}}\,,\qquad\rho=1-\delta/2\,.

Note that
|B_{\zeta}(z)|=1
for
|z|=\rho
and that
\max_{(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}}|B_{\zeta}|\leq\frac{2-2\delta}{4-3\delta}=:C_{\delta}^{\prime}.
Now put
h=B_{\zeta_{1}}\cdots B_{\zeta_{N}}g
and observe that by the maximum principle

\max_{(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}}|h|\leq C_{\delta}^{N}\max_{(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}}|g|\leq C_{\delta}^{N}\max_{(1-\delta/2)\mathbb{D}}|g|=(C_{\delta}^{\prime})^{N}\max_{(1-\delta/2)\mathbb{D}}|h|\,,

which yields (
14
). To conclude the proof, we note that for each
h\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
the Cauchy estimates (
13
) imply that

c_{0}\leq\max_{j\leq\ell}|a_{j}|=\max_{j\leq\ell}\frac{|F^{(j)}(0)|}{j!}\leq\frac{\max_{(1-\delta)\mathbb{D}}|h|}{2\pi}\,,

whereas for each
h\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}
we have

\max_{(1-\delta/2)\mathbb{D}}|h|\leq K\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(e^{\beta}(1-\delta/2))^{j}\,.

Plugging both these inequalities into (
14
) yields the claim.
∎

2.2
The perturbative argument

In the proof of Theorem
1.3
, we will make use of a standard anti-concentration inequality, which may be viewed as a generalization of Erdős’s solution to the Littlewood-Offord problem to arbitrary non-degenerate random variables. A version in this generality follows from the Lévy-Kolmogorov-Rogozin inequality, see for example
[
Ess68
]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

(
[
Ess68
, Corollary 1]
)

Let
a_{j}
be an i.i.d. sequence of non-degenerate (complex) random variables and let
c_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
|c_{j}|\geq 1
. There is a constant
C>0
depending only on the distribution of
a_{0}
so that for all
R\geq 1
we have

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\bigg[\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}c_{j}-x\Big|\leq R\bigg]\leq\frac{CR}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

For
m\geq 1
we will decompose
F(z)=P_{m}(z)+T_{m}(z)
, where

P_{m}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}a_{k}z^{k}

(15)

is the Taylor polynomial of degree
m
of
F
around the origin. We first deduce uniform anti-concentration for the first
m
derivatives of
T_{m}
using Lemma
2.4
:

Claim 2.5
.

There exists
C>0
depending only on the distribution of
a_{0}
so that for all
z\in\mathbb{D}
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq k\leq m
we have

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|T_{m}^{(k)}(z)-x\big|\leq{\varepsilon}|z|^{m}\Big]\leq C\big(\log(1/|z|)/\log(1/{\varepsilon})\big)^{-1/2}\,.

Proof.

Let
N=\lfloor\log({\varepsilon}^{-1})/2\log(|z|^{-1})\rfloor
, and note that with this choice of
N
for each
j\in\{m+1,\ldots,m+N\}
we have

{\varepsilon}^{-1}|z|^{j-k-m}\frac{j!}{(j-k)!}\geq{\varepsilon}^{-1}|z|^{j-m}\geq 1\,.

We thus have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|T_{m}^{(k)}(z)-x\big|\leq{\varepsilon}|z|^{m}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\sum_{j=m+1}^{\infty}a_{j}\frac{j!}{(j-k)!}z^{j-k}-x\right|\leq{\varepsilon}|z|^{m}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\sum_{j=m+1}^{m+N}a_{j}\frac{j!}{(j-k)!}{\varepsilon}^{-1}|z|^{-m}z^{j-k}-x\right|\leq 1\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma}~\ref{lemma:Levy-Kolmogorov-Rogozin}}}{{\leq}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{N}}

which, by our choice of
N
, completes the proof.
∎

We want to show that if
F
has a double zero, then for large
m
the polynomial
P_{m}^{\prime}
has a root nearby on which
F
is small. This takes the form of the following lemma, which provides the engine behind the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
h(z)=\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{k}z^{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}
for some
K>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
, and for
m\geq 1
let
p_{m}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}h_{k}z^{k}
denote
its Taylor polynomial around the origin. For each
\gamma>0
there exists
C=C(\gamma,K,\beta)
so that the following holds. If

h(z_{0})=h^{\prime}(z_{0})=0\quad\text{and}\quad|h^{\prime\prime}(z_{0})|\geq\gamma

for some
z_{0}\in\mathcal{A}(\beta,e^{-8\beta})
, then for each
m\geq C
there exists
w\in\mathcal{A}(\beta e^{-\beta},e^{-7\beta})
such that
p_{m}^{\prime}(w)=0
and
|h(w)|\leq Ce^{-\beta m}|w|^{m}
.

Proof.

As
h\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}
, we have that

\max_{0\leq j\leq 3}\max_{z\in e^{-2\beta}\mathbb{D}}|h^{(j)}(z)|\leq K\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}k(k-1)(k-2)e^{-\beta k}\leq 6K\frac{e^{3\beta}}{(1-e^{-\beta})^{4}}=:C_{0}\,,

(16)

and similarly, for all
z\in e^{-2\beta}\mathbb{D}

|(h-p_{m})^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{Ke^{2\beta}}{1-e^{-\beta}}m\,|ze^{\beta}|^{m}\leq C_{0}|ze^{\beta}|^{m}\,.

By setting
\eta=4C_{0}\gamma^{-1}m\,|z_{0}e^{2\beta}|^{m}
, we see that

\max_{|y|=\eta}\big|(h-p_{m})^{\prime}(z_{0}+y)\big|\leq C_{0}(|z_{0}|e^{\beta}+\eta)^{m}\leq C_{0}|z_{0}e^{2\beta}|^{m}

(17)

for all
m
large enough. Since
h^{\prime}(z_{0})=0
, for each
y
on the circle
\{|y|=\eta\}
, we can bound

|h^{\prime}(z_{0}+y)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bound_on_derivatives_of_h}}}{{\geq}}\gamma|y|-C_{0}|y|^{2}\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}\eta=2C_{0}m\,|z_{0}e^{2\beta}|^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bound_on_derivative_of_tail_on_circle}}}{{>}}\max_{|y|=\eta}|(h-p_{m})^{\prime}(z_{0}+y)|

where the second inequality is for all
m
large enough.
Rouché’s theorem now implies that
p_{m}^{\prime}
has a root
w
in the disk of radius
\eta
centered at
z_{0}
. Furthermore, note that by Taylor’s theorem

|h(w)|\leq\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}\max_{e^{-3\beta}\mathbb{D}}|h^{(2)}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bound_on_derivatives_of_h}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}C_{0}=8C_{0}^{3}\gamma^{-2}m^{2}|z_{0}e^{2\beta}|^{2m}\,.

We can take
m
sufficiently large (depending on
\beta
) so that we can guarantee
w\in\mathcal{A}\big(e^{-\beta}|z_{0}|,e^{\beta}|z_{0}|\big)
, which in turn shows

|h(w)|\leq C_{0}|we^{3\beta}|^{2m}\leq C_{0}e^{-\beta m}|w|^{m}\,,

which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

As we already mentioned, it suffices to show that for each
\delta>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{such that }\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=0\Big]=0\,.

Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}=\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
where the events
\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
are defined by (
11
) and (
12
), respectively. By combining Claim
2.1
and Claim
2.2
, the above equality will follow once we show that for all
K,\ell\geq 1
and
\beta>0
small enough we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and there exists}\ \alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{such that }\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=0\Big]=0\,.

To be slightly more general, let us define for
k\geq 0
the event
\mathcal{G}_{k}=\mathcal{G}_{k}(K,\beta,\ell,\delta)
by

\mathcal{G}_{k}=\Big\{F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and there exists}\ \alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=\ldots=F^{(k)}(\alpha)=0\Big\}\,.

Denoting by

k_{\ast}=\min\big\{k\geq 0\,:\ \mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{k}\big]=0\big\}\,,

(18)

we see that the proof of Theorem
1.3
will follow once we show that
k_{\ast}\leq 1
. We note that by Claim
2.3
we have
k_{\ast}\leq M
, as the total number of zeros for
F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}
is at most
M
. Seeking a contradiction, assume that
k_{\ast}\geq 2
. We have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{k_{\ast}-1}\big]

(19)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=\ldots=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=\ldots=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|>0\big]

where the last equality is by the definition (
18
) of
k_{\ast}
. As the sequence of events

\Big\{F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=\ldots=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|\geq\gamma\Big\}

increase as
\gamma\downarrow 0
, we conclude from (
19
) there exists some
\gamma>0
for which

\mathbb{P}\Big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=\ldots=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|\geq\gamma\Big]>0\,.

In particular, we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(\alpha)=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|\geq\gamma\Big]>0

(20)

which, as we will now show, is a contradiction for
k_{\ast}\geq 2
.

We start by noting that for
F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}
, we may decrease
\beta
to some
\beta^{\prime}
and increase
K,\ell
to some
K^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}
and get that
F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}\in\mathcal{E}_{K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}
. Furthermore as the event in (
20
) increases as
\beta
decreases, we may also assume that
\beta^{\prime}<\delta<1-\delta<e^{-8\beta^{\prime}}
. Recalling the decomposition
F(z)=P_{m}(z)+T_{m}(z)
given by (
15
), we can apply Lemma
2.6
with
h=F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}
and deduce that

\displaystyle\Big\{

\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(\alpha)=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|\geq\gamma\Big\}

(21)

\displaystyle\subset\Big\{F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}\in\mathcal{E}_{K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\exists w\in\mathcal{A}(\beta^{\prime}e^{-\beta^{\prime}},e^{-7\beta^{\prime}})\ :\ P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(w)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)|\leq Ce^{-\beta^{\prime}m}|w|^{m}\Big\}

for all
m\geq C(K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
large enough. For
F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}\in\mathcal{E}_{K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}
, we set

\mathcal{W}=\big\{w\in\mathcal{A}(\beta^{\prime}e^{-\beta^{\prime}},e^{-7\beta^{\prime}})\,:\,P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(w)=0\big\}

and note that by Claim
2.3
we have
|\mathcal{W}|\leq M
. Since
P_{m}
and
T_{m}
are independent, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[

\displaystyle\Big\{F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}\in\mathcal{E}_{K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\exists w\in\mathcal{A}(\beta^{\prime}e^{-\beta^{\prime}},e^{-7\beta^{\prime}})\ :\ P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(w)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)|\leq Ce^{-\beta^{\prime}m}|w|^{m}\Big\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P_{m}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\{P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}\in\mathcal{E}_{K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\}}\mathbb{P}_{T_{m}}\Big[\exists w\in\mathcal{W}\,:\ \big|T_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)+P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)\big|\leq Ce^{-\beta^{\prime}m}|w|^{m}\Big]\,\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P_{m}}\bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\{P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}\in\mathcal{E}_{K^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\}}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbb{P}_{T_{m}}\Big[\big|T_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)+P_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)\big|\leq Ce^{-\beta^{\prime}m}|w|^{m}\Big]\,\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq M\max_{w\in\mathcal{A}(\beta^{\prime}e^{-\beta^{\prime}},e^{-7\beta^{\prime}})}\max_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\,\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|T_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)-x\big|\leq Ce^{-\beta^{\prime}m}|w|^{m}\Big]\,.

By Claim
2.5
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
so that

\max_{w\in\mathcal{A}(\beta^{\prime}e^{-\beta^{\prime}},e^{-7\beta^{\prime}})}\max_{x\in\mathbb{C}}\,\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|T_{m}^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(w)-x\big|\leq Ce^{-\beta^{\prime}m}|w|^{m}\Big]\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\sqrt{m}}\,,

which, by combining with (
21
) implies that

\mathbb{P}\Big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(\alpha)=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|\geq\gamma\Big]\lesssim m^{-1/2}

for arbitrarily large
m
. This implies that

\mathbb{P}\Big[F\in\mathcal{E}_{K,\beta,\ell}\ \text{and}\ \exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\delta,1-\delta)\ \text{s.t.}\ F^{(k_{\ast}-2)}(\alpha)=F^{(k_{\ast}-1)}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |F^{(k_{\ast})}(\alpha)|\geq\gamma\Big]=0

which is a contradiction to (
20
). This shows that
k_{\ast}\leq 1
which completes the proof.
∎

3
Reducing Theorem
1.1
to a net argument

Most of the roots of our random polynomial
f_{n}
lie inside the annulus
\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1+K/n)
with
K\geq 1
large (see for instance
[
IZ97
, Theorem 1]
) in the sense that one typically has
1-{\varepsilon}
proportion of the roots in
\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1+K/n)
for
K
large enough as a function of
{\varepsilon}
. In view of that observation, the proof of our main result Theorem
1.1
will naturally be split into two main steps (recall also the outline of the proof from Section
1.4
). First, we will show that roots of
f_{n}
that are present outside of the annulus do not contribute to the limiting Poisson process. This is seen by the following proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

For each
{\varepsilon}>0
and
L\geq 1
, there is a
K\geq 1
so that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{D}\big(0,1-K/n\big)\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,Ln^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ {\text{such that }}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\leq{\varepsilon}\,.

Next, we will show that the close roots from inside the annulus
\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1+K/n)
, scaled appropriately, converge in law to a Poisson point process as
n\to\infty
. Before that, we get a technical issue out of the way. Since
f_{n}
has real coefficients, we have
f_{n}(\overline{z})=\overline{f_{n}(z)}
, which in turn implies that the set of roots is symmetric with respect to complex conjugate. To avoid this redundancy, we will only consider the roots of
f_{n}
which lie in the upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}
. For
K\geq 1
fixed and
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
a finite union of intervals, we set

\Omega_{K}=\mathbb{H}\cap\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1+K/n)

(22)

and

X_{n}(U)=\#\Big\{\alpha\in\Omega_{K}\,:\,\exists\alpha^{\prime}\ \,\text{with}\ \,|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\ \,\text{and }\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big\}\,.

(23)

Theorem 3.2
.

For all
K\geq 1
fixed, we have
X_{n}(U)\xrightarrow{\ d\ }\text{Poisson}({\lambda}_{K,U})
as
n\to\infty
, with

{\lambda}_{K,U}=\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\int_{U}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t

(24)

and
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)>0
is given by (
68
) below.

Before we dive into the actual details, we first show how Proposition
3.1
combined with Theorem
3.2
yields the proof of our main result of the paper.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Denoting by
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
the roots of
f_{n}
in
\mathbb{H}
,
m\leq n
, we consider the random measure

\mu_{n}=\sum_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m}\delta_{n^{5/4}\,|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}|}\,.

Furthermore, for
K\geq 1
, we set

\mu_{n}^{K}=\sum_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m}\delta_{n^{5/4}\,|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j^{\prime}}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\in\Omega_{K}\}}\,.

Note that the polynomial
g_{n}(z)=z^{n}f_{n}(z^{-1})
has the same distribution of
f_{n}
and has roots
\{\alpha_{j}^{-1}\}
. Further, for
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{D}
we have that
|\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}|<|\alpha_{j}^{-1}-\alpha_{i}^{-1}|
. As such, Proposition
3.1
also eliminates pairs of close roots in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\Omega_{K}\cap\mathbb{D}\}
. Thus, for any
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
a finite union of intervals, Proposition
3.1
implies that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\mu_{n}(U)-\mu_{n}^{K}(U)\big|\geq 1\Big]\leq{\varepsilon}

for all
K\geq 1
large. Furthermore, Theorem
3.2
implies that
\mu_{n}^{K}(U)=X_{n}(U)
converges in distribution to a Poisson random variable with parameter
{\lambda}_{K,U}
. Since

\lim_{K\to\infty}{\lambda}_{K,U}=\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\int_{U}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t=\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}\int_{U}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t

(see Claim
6.5
) where
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}>0
is given by (
69
) below, we observe that for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mu_{n}(U)=\ell\big]

\displaystyle=\lim_{K\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mu_{n}^{K}(U)=\ell\big]

\displaystyle=\lim_{K\to\infty}e^{-{\lambda}_{K,U}}\frac{{\lambda}_{K,U}^{\ell}}{\ell!}=\exp\Big(-\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}\int_{U}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t\Big)\frac{1}{\ell!}\Big(\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}\int_{U}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t\Big)^{\ell}\,.

That is,
\mu_{n}(U)
converge in distribution to a Poisson random variable with intensity
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}\int_{U}t^{3}\,{\rm d}t
. Since this is true for all
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
a finite collection of intervals, a standard compactness argument (see, for instance, Kallenberg
[
Kal17
, Theorem 4.7]
) implies that the sequence of point processes
\{\mu_{n}\}
converge in the vague topology to a Poisson point process with intensity
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}t^{3}{\rm d}t
, and we are done.
∎

3.1
Making use of the sub-Gaussian assumption

In this section we show how the sub-Gaussian assumption on the coefficients is used in our argument. First, to ensure that the net argument indeed captures roots which are relatively close, we will also need a control on the maximum of
f_{n}
along with its first few derivatives inside the disk. This is made possible by a variant of the classical Salem-Zygmund inequality, see for example
[
Kah85
, Chapter 6]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
r\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1)
we write
d=\min\{n,(1-r)^{-1}\}
. There exists
d_{0}\geq 1
so that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, the probability that

\max_{z\in r\mathbb{D}}\big|f_{n}^{(\ell)}(z)\big|\leq d^{1/2+\ell}\log^{2}d\,,\qquad\text{for}\ \ell\in\{0,1,2,3\}

is at least
1-2\exp\left(-\log^{2}d\right)
.

Proof.

For
\ell\geq 0
, the Salem-Zygmund inequality
[
Kah85
, Chapter 6, Theorem 1]
applied with
\kappa=e^{\log^{2}d}
implies that

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\max_{|z|=r}\big|f_{n}^{(\ell)}(z)\big|\geq C_{\ell}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{n}k^{2\ell}r^{2k}\Big)^{1/2}\log d\bigg]\leq 2\exp\big(-\log^{2}d\big)\,,

for some constant
C_{\ell}>0
. Now, by taking
d_{0}
to be large enough so that

C_{\ell}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{n}k^{2\ell}r^{2k}\Big)^{1/2}\leq d^{1/2+\ell}\log d

for all
d\geq d_{0}
we get what we wanted.
∎

The proofs of both Proposition
3.1
and of Theorem
3.2
are based on net arguments in the respective annulus. To make those net arguments work, we will need to prove effective small-ball probability bounds for samples of
(f_{n}(z),f_{n}^{\prime}(z),f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z))
along net points
z
.
The standard tool for bounding small-ball probabilities via the corresponding characteristic function is through the Esseen inequality
[
Ess66
]
: for any random vector
X
with characteristic function
\varphi_{X}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}[e^{i\langle X,\xi\rangle}]
we have

\sup_{x\in X}\mathbb{P}\big[|X-x|\leq\delta\big]\lesssim m\big(B(0,\delta)\big)\int_{B(0,\delta^{-1})}|\varphi_{X}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,.

(25)

In what follows, a typical application of (
25
) would be with
X=(f_{n}(z),d^{-1}f_{n}^{\prime}(z),d^{-2}f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z))
, where
d=d_{n}(z)=\min\{n,(1-|z|)^{-1}\}
is the effective degree of the polynomial
f_{n}
at
z\in\mathbb{D}
. In fact, for our specific application we will also need an ‘exponentially-tilted’ version of the Esseen inequality, which takes the form of Proposition
3.4
below and is inspired by
[
Cam+24
, Lemma 5.2]
.

Recall that the random coefficients of
f_{n}
are given by
\{\xi_{k}\}
, and let
\{\tilde{\xi}_{k}\}
and independent copy of
\{\xi_{k}\}
. Let
\eta_{k}
be the random variable which is equal to
0
with probability
1/2
and to
\xi_{k}-\tilde{\xi}_{k}
with probability
1/2
. With that, we can define a new random polynomial

g_{n}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\eta_{k}z^{k}

(26)

which is also a Kac polynomial. Let
\varphi_{\eta_{0}}
denote the characteristic function of
\eta_{0}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

There exists a constants
C,c>0
such that for all
z\in\mathcal{A}(\tfrac{1}{2},2)
we have

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq\delta d^{1/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-\beta|\leq\delta d^{3/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq td^{5/2}\Big]\\
\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}}\delta^{4}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,,

for all
\delta>0
and
t\geq 0
, where
d=d_{n}(z)
is given by (
10
).

Proof.

For the proof, we will denote by

X=d^{-1/2}\big(f_{n}(z),d^{-1}f_{n}^{\prime}(z)\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{4}

and
Y=f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)/d^{5/2}\in\mathbb{C}
. We will also denote by

\Phi(t)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\cos^{2}\big((\xi_{0}-\xi_{0}^{\prime})t\big)\Big]\,,\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(27)

Then the proof will follow once we prove the bound

\mathbb{P}\Big[|X-x|\leq\delta\,,|Y|^{2}\geq t^{2}\Big]\lesssim e^{-ct^{2}}\delta^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\Phi\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}/2}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)

(28)

uniformly for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Indeed, by the elementary inequality
\cos^{2}(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cos(2x)
we have that

\Phi(t)\leq\varphi_{\eta_{0}}(2t)\,,

and by plugging this into (
28
) and changing variables
2\xi\mapsto\xi
we get the inequality we are after.

To prove (
28
), we first recall that the random coefficients
\{\xi_{k}\}
are assumed to be sub-Gaussian, and hence
Y
is a sum of independent sub-Gaussian random variables. By
[
Ver18
, Propsotion 2.6.1]
there exists a constant
c>0
so that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\big(c|Y|^{2}\big)\Big]\leq 4

(29)

for all
z\in\mathcal{A}(\tfrac{1}{2},2)
. Hence, for all
{\lambda}>0
we can apply Markov’s inequality and get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[|X-x|\leq\delta\,,|Y|^{2}\geq t^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big[e^{-{\lambda}^{2}|X-x|^{2}/2}\geq e^{-{\lambda}^{2}\delta^{2}/2}\,,\ e^{\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2}}\geq e^{ct^{2}/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{{\lambda}^{2}\delta^{2}/2-ct^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-{\lambda}^{2}|X-x|^{2}/2}e^{\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2}}\Big]\,.

(30)

Fourier inversion implies that for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
we have

e^{-|z|^{2}/2}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{-|y|^{2}/2}e\big(\langle y,z\rangle\big)\,{\rm d}m(y)\,,

and by plugging in
z={\lambda}(X-x)
and applying Fubini we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-{\lambda}^{2}|X-x|^{2}/2}e^{\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=C\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{-|y|^{2}/2}e\big(\langle{\lambda}y,X-x\rangle\big){\rm d}m(y)\bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2})\,e\big({\lambda}\langle y,X\rangle\big)\Big]\Big|\,e^{-|y|^{2}/2}\,{\rm d}m(y)\,.

Plugging this into (
3.1
), we can apply the change of variables
\xi={\lambda}y
along with the choice
{\lambda}=\delta^{-1}
and get that

\mathbb{P}\Big[|X-x|\leq\delta\,,|Y|^{2}\geq t^{2}\Big]\lesssim\delta^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2})\,e\big(\langle\xi,X\rangle\big)\Big]\Big|\,e^{-\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}/2}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,.

(31)

It remains to bound the expectation inside the integral. Recall that
\{\tilde{\xi}_{k}\}
are independent copies of
\{\xi_{k}\}
, and let
\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}
be the corresponding independent copies of the random vectors
X,Y
. We have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2})\,e\big(\langle\xi,X\rangle\big)\Big]\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2})\,e\big(\langle\xi,X\rangle\big)\Big]\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|\tilde{Y}|^{2})\,e\big(-\langle\xi,\tilde{X}\rangle\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2}+\tfrac{c}{2}|\tilde{Y}|^{2})\,e\big(\langle\xi,X-\tilde{X}\rangle\big)\Big]

\displaystyle(\text{definition of $X$})\qquad

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2}+\tfrac{c}{2}|\tilde{Y}|^{2})\,\prod_{k=0}^{n}e\bigg(d^{-1/2}(\xi_{k}-\tilde{\xi}_{k})\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle(\text{symmetry of $\xi_{k}-\tilde{\xi}_{k}$})\qquad

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2}+\tfrac{c}{2}|\tilde{Y}|^{2})\,\prod_{k=0}^{n}\cos\bigg(d^{-1/2}(\xi_{k}-\tilde{\xi}_{k})\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\bigg)\Bigg]\,.

Now, combining the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality with (
29
), we see that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp(\tfrac{c}{2}|Y|^{2})\,e\big(\langle\xi,X\rangle\big)\Big]\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{k=0}^{n}\cos^{2}\bigg(d^{-1/2}(\xi_{k}-\tilde{\xi}_{k})\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\bigg)\Big]\Bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\Phi\bigg(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\bigg)\Bigg)^{1/2}

where the equality is due to the independence of the sequence
\{\xi_{k}-\tilde{\xi}_{k}\}
. Plugging this into (
31
) yields (
28
), and we are done.
∎

3.2
“Worst case” small-ball probability bounds

We will require three small-ball probability bounds for the the tuple
(f_{n},f_{n}^{\prime})
evaluated at arbitrary points in
\mathbb{D}
. We think of these as “worst-case” bounds since they require no arithmetic structure on
z
. The three results stated in this subsection vary depending on how close
z
is to the real axis.

Recall that
d=d_{n}(z)=\min\{n,(1-|z|)^{-1}\}
is the effective degree of the polynomial
f_{n}
at a point
z\in\mathbb{D}
. We first state a rather weak small-ball probability bound, which is valid for all points
z\in\mathbb{D}
which are separated from the real axis.

Claim 3.5
.

There exists
C,c>0
such that for all
z=re^{i\theta}
with
\theta\in[d^{-1},\pi-d^{-1}]
we have

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq ad^{1/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-\beta|\leq bd^{3/2}\Big]\leq C(ab)^{2}

for all
a,b\geq(\log d)/d^{1/2}
.

The proof of Claim
3.5
is not difficult, and is based on a Gaussian approximation on the Fourier-side. We will also need a quantitative variant of the Claim
3.5
as the evaluation point
z
approaches the real axis, which is provided by the next claim.

Claim 3.6
.

There exists
C,c>0
such that for all
z=re^{i\theta}
with
\theta\in[d^{-1-\tau/4},d^{-1}]
we have

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq ad^{1/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-\beta|\leq bd^{3/2}\,\Big]\leq Cd^{2\tau}(ab)^{2}

for all
a,b\geq d^{-1/2+3\tau}
.

The proofs of Claim
3.5
and Claim
3.6

are given in Section
7
. Finally, we will also need a small-ball bound for the polynomial evaluated on points very close to the real axis. For this, the classical Littlewood-Offord bound (Lemma
2.4
) is sufficient:

Claim 3.7
.

There exists
C>0
so that for all
z=re^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{D}
with
\theta\in[0,d^{-1-\tau/4}]
we have

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq ad^{1/2}\Big]\leq C\big(a+d^{-1/2}\big)

for all
a>0
.

3.3
A small-ball probability bound for smooth points

The small-ball probability bounds stated above are relatively weak, in the sense that the parameters
a,b
must be taken to be rather large (specifically, larger than
d^{-1/2+o(1)}
) for the bounds to apply. For the proof of Proposition
3.1
this will not suffice as we shall need to deal with probabilities of much smaller order. To improve our bounds, we lean on a method pioneered by Konyagin and Schlag
[
Kon94
,
KS99
]
in their study of the minimum modulus of the polynomial on the unit circle.

Definition 3.8
.

For
A\geq 1
, we say that
z=re^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}
is
A
-smooth if

\|p\theta/\pi\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\geq A/d_{n}(z)\qquad\forall p\in[1,A+1]\cap\mathbb{Z}\,,

where as before
d_{n}(z)=\min\{n,(1-r)^{-1}\}
. We say that
z\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathbb{D}
is
A
-smooth if
z^{-1}
is
A
-smooth.

To carry out the net argument for the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we will separate the net to those points where the random vector
\big(f_{n}(z),f_{n}^{\prime}(z)\big)
is relatively well-spread (which happens exactly when
z
is smooth) and the rest of the points, where we shall apply Claim
3.5
, Claim
3.6
and Claim
3.7
instead. Concretely, we will use the following small-ball probability bound, which we prove in Section
8
.

Lemma 3.9
.

There exists universal constants
r_{0}\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1)
and
c,C>0
so that for all
r\in[r_{0},1]
and for all
t\geq 0
the following holds. For each
z=re^{i\theta}
which is
d^{7\tau}
-smooth we have

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq ad^{1/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-\beta|\leq bd^{3/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq td^{5/2}\Big]\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}}(ab)^{2}\,,

where
a,b\geq d^{-3/2+11\tau}
and
d=d_{n}(z)=\min\{n,(1-r)^{-1}\}
.

3.4
Gaussian comparison near the unit circle

While for the proof of Proposition
3.1
we will only need upper bounds on the small-ball probabilities, to prove the limit law as stated in Theorem
3.2
we will need to compute the first-order approximation of small-ball probabilities along the net. Recall the definition of
\Omega_{K}
given by

\Omega_{K}=\mathbb{H}\cap\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1+K/n)\,.

Definition 3.10
.

For
m\geq 2
and
\gamma>0
we say that
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
is
\gamma
-spread if

|z_{j}-z_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{\gamma}{n}\qquad\forall 1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m\,.

Furthermore, we say that a tuple
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
is
A
-smooth if
z_{j}
is
A
-smooth (in the sense of Definition
3.8
) for each
1\leq j\leq m
.

Remark 3.11
.

In a moment, we will state our small-ball bounds/comparisons for tuples of smooth, separated points in
\Omega_{K}
. Even though Definition
3.8
depends on the effective degree
d_{n}(z)
, in the annulus
\Omega_{K}
all effective degrees have

d_{n}(z)\gtrsim_{K}n.

Therefore, any
A
-smooth tuple in
\Omega_{K}
would be
A^{\prime}
-smooth if
d_{n}(z)
is replaced by
n
in Definition
3.8
, for some
A^{\prime}\gtrsim_{K}A
. For our application, the degree of “smoothness”
A
will always tend to infinity as
n\to\infty
, so we will safely ignore this point and not keep track of the effective degree
d_{n}(\cdot)
when considering points in
\Omega_{K}
.

For a tuple
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
we denote by

S_{n}(\mathbf{z})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(f_{n}(z_{1}),\ldots,f_{n}(z_{m}),\frac{z_{1}^{-1}}{n}f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{1}),\ldots,\frac{z_{m}^{-1}}{n}f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{m}),\frac{z_{1}^{-2}}{n^{2}}f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z_{1}),\ldots,\frac{z_{m}^{-2}}{n^{2}}f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z_{m})\Big)\,.

(32)

To prove Theorem
3.2
, we will need to compare small ball probabilities of
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6m}
with that of the corresponding Gaussian. Let
\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
be a Gaussian vector with the same covariance matrix as
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
(which is spelled out in Section
9
below, see (
113
)). Note that in the case where the random coefficients
(\xi_{k})
are standard Gaussian, then
\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})
has the same distribution of
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
be
n^{\kappa}
-smooth and
1
-spread for some
\kappa\in(0,1)
, and let
Q\subset\mathbb{C}^{3m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6m}
be a rectangle (that is, a Cartesian product of intervals) lying in some fixed compact set, and with side-lengths at least
n^{-M}
for some
M>0
. Then

\sup_{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q+\zeta\,\big]-\mathbb{P}\big[\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q+\zeta\,\big]\bigg|\lesssim n^{-1/2}\,m(Q)\,,

where the constant depends only on
m,\kappa,K,M
and the distribution of
\xi
.

We will also need a near optimal small-ball probability bound for tuples which are almost
1
-spread.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
be
n^{\kappa}
-smooth and
\gamma
-spread for some
\kappa\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\geq n^{-{1/30m}}
. Then for any
K>0
and for any
\delta>n^{-M}
we have

\sup_{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}}\mathbb{P}\Big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in B(\zeta,\delta)\Big]\lesssim\gamma^{-O(m^{2})}\delta^{6m}\,,

where
B(\zeta,\delta)\subset\mathbb{C}^{3m}
is a ball of radius
\delta
around
\zeta
, and the explicit constant depends only on
m,\kappa,K,M
and the distribution of
\xi
.

Both Theorem
3.12
and Proposition
3.13
are proved in Section
9
.

4
Reducing to an annulus: Proof of Proposition
3.1

The main goal of this section is to prove Proposition
3.1
, namely that with high probability there is no pair of roots at distance
\omega(1/n)
from the unit circle which contributes to the limiting point process stated in Theorem
1.1
. In what follows we will need different net arguments at different scales, so we start by giving a rather general definition of our net in an annulus.

Definition 4.1
.

For
r_{1}<r_{2}
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
we define

\mathcal{N}=\Big\{(r_{1}+a/M)e\big(\pi b/M\big)\,:\,a\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lceil M(r_{2}-r_{1})\rceil\}\,,\ b\in\{1,\ldots,2M\}\Big\}\,,

and note that
\mathcal{N}
is a
(2r_{2}/M)
-net of
\mathcal{A}(r_{1},r_{2})
. For each point
z=(r_{1}+a/M)e\big(\pi b/M\big)\in\mathcal{N}
, define the (polar) rectangle

\mathcal{R}_{z}=\Big\{(r_{1}+(a+s)/M)e\big(\pi(b+t)/M\big)\,:\,t,s\in[-1/2,1/2]\Big\}\,.

Then the collection
\{\mathcal{R}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathcal{N}}
consists of rectangles with disjoint interiors which cover
\mathcal{A}(r_{1},r_{2})
.

Let
K\geq 1
be the constant from the statement of Proposition
3.1
, which we will take sufficiently large (as a function of
{\varepsilon},L
) as the proof goes. As we will only consider the disk
(1-K/n)\mathbb{D}
in this section, for
r_{0}<1
we define the sequence of decreasing radii

\rho_{k}=1-\frac{K+(2^{k}-1)}{n}\,,

(33)

where
k=0,1,\ldots,N
with
N=\lceil\log_{2}\big(n(1-r_{0})-K+1\big)\rceil
. With this notation, we have
\rho_{0}=1-K/n
and
r_{0}-2(1-r_{0})\leq\rho_{N}\leq r_{0}
. We will also denote by
\mathcal{A}_{k}=\mathcal{A}(\rho_{k+1},\rho_{k})
and
d_{k}=(1-\rho_{k})^{-1}
. For reasons that will be evident later, we will also denote by
\rho_{-1}=1
, and with that
\mathcal{A}_{-1}=\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1)
. We always set
d_{-1}=n
to be the maximal possible degree.

4.1
The derivative cannot be too small on roots

We start our argument by showing that it is unlikely to have a root on which the derivative is very small. The key for that is the following deterministic fact.

Claim 4.2
.

For any
\beta>0
and for any
d\geq d_{0}(\beta)
large enough the following holds. For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
{\varepsilon}\geq d^{-\beta/2}
and
\delta\in(0,d^{-5/4-\beta})
, suppose that
f
is an analytic function for which

\max_{\zeta\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)}|f^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)|\leq d^{5/2}\log^{2}d\,.

If there exists
w\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)
so that
f(w)=0
and
|f^{\prime}(w)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}
, then

|f(z)|\leq\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}(1+d^{-\beta/3})\qquad\text{and}\qquad|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}(1+d^{-\beta/2})\,.

Proof.

Applying Taylor’s theorem around the point
z
gives

|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f^{\prime}(w)|+\delta\max_{\zeta\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)}|f^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}+d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}(1+d^{-\beta/2})\,,

where in the last inequality we used the bound
{\varepsilon}\geq d^{-\beta/2}
. To get the corresponding bound for
|f^{\prime}(z)|
, we use Taylor expansion again to get

\displaystyle|f(z)|

\displaystyle=|f(z)-f(w)|\leq\delta|f^{\prime}(z)|+\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\max_{\zeta\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)}|f^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq\delta\Big({\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\big(1+d^{-\beta/2}\big)+\frac{1}{2}d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d\Big)\leq\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\big(1+d^{-\beta/3}\big)\,,

which is what we wanted.
∎

Recall the definition of
\rho_{k}
for
k=0,\ldots,N
as given by (
33
). For each
k\in\{0,\ldots,N\}
fixed, we will consider the net
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{k}
of
\mathcal{A}(\rho_{k+1},\rho_{k})
which is given by Definition
4.1
with the parameters
\delta=d_{k}^{-5/4-\beta}
and
M=\lfloor 10/\delta\rfloor
. Recall Definition
3.8
of smooth points
z\in\mathbb{C}
, and let
\tau>0
be a sufficiently small absolute constant. We will also need further decomposition of the net
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{k}
. We denote by

\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}=\big\{z\in\mathcal{N}\,:\,z\ \text{is}\ d_{k}^{7\tau}\text{-smooth}\big\}

(34)

and by
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}=\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}
. We will further decompose the non-smooth part into

\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}=\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\big\{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}\,:\,\arg(z)\in[d_{k}^{-1},\pi-d_{k}^{-1}]\big\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\big\{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}\,:\,\arg(z)\in[d_{k}^{-1-\tau/4},d_{k}^{-1}]\cup[\pi-d_{k}^{-1},\pi-d_{k}^{-1-\tau/4}]\big\}\,,

(35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\big\{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}\,:\,\arg(z)\in[0,d_{k}^{-1-\tau/4}]\cup[\pi-d_{k}^{-1-\tau/4},\pi]\big\}\,.

We note that we have the following bounds:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}|

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{2}(\rho_{k}-\rho_{k+1})\lesssim\delta^{-2}d_{k}\,,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{-2}d_{k}^{7\tau}\,,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{-2}\,,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{-2}d_{k}^{-\tau/4}\,.

Lemma 4.3
.

There exist absolute constants
C,\beta>0
,
r_{0}<1
so that the following holds. For each
k\in\{-1,0,\ldots,N\}
and
{\varepsilon}\in(d_{k}^{-\beta},1)
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\,:\ f_{n}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\leq{\varepsilon}d_{k}^{5/4}\Big]\leq C{\varepsilon}\,.

Proof.

As
k\in\{-1,0,1,\ldots,N\}
will remain fixed throughout the proof, we will sometimes omit the dependence of the parameters on it to lighten the notation. Define the event
\mathcal{E}
via

\mathcal{E}=\Big\{\max_{\zeta\in\rho_{k}\mathbb{D}}|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)|\leq d^{5/2}\log^{2}d\Big\}

and note that by Lemma
3.3
we have
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}^{c}\big]\leq\exp(-c\log^{2}d)
. Using the fact that the set
\mathcal{N}
is a
\delta
-net of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, we can use Claim
4.2
and the union bound to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\,:\

\displaystyle f_{n}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\,:\ f_{n}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\leq{\varepsilon}d_{k}^{5/4}\,,\mathcal{E}\Big]+e^{-c\log^{2}d}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]+e^{-c\log^{2}d}\,.

(36)

Here we used the fact that
\min_{k}\rho_{k}=\rho_{N}
can be made arbitrarily close to
1
by increasing
r_{0}
. We will bound the sum in (
4.1
) by splitting the net into
\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)},\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)},\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
, as described in (
34
) and (
4.1
).

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}
:
For points
z\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
that are
d^{7\tau}
-smooth, Lemma
3.9
implies that

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{1/2}d^{-\beta-1/2}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{3/2-1/4}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{4}d^{-3/2-2\beta}\,.

Since
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}|\lesssim\delta^{-2}d=d^{3/2+2\beta}
, we get that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{4}\,.

(37)

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
:
Here we argue similarly, but apply Claim
3.5
instead of Lemma
3.9
(note that
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
are separated from the real axis) and we get a slightly worst bound

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq\log d\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{3/2-1/4}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{2}\,d^{-3/2}\log^{2}d\,.

Since
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}|\lesssim d^{1/2+\beta+7\tau}
, we get that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}^{(1)}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\lesssim d^{-1+2\beta+7\tau}{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}\,.

(38)

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}
:

Here we also argue similarly, but apply Claim
3.6
instead of Claim
3.5
:

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq d^{3\tau}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{3/2-1/4}\Big]\lesssim d^{-3/2+6\tau}{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}\,.

As
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}|\lesssim d^{1/2+2\beta}
, this yields that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}^{(2)}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\lesssim d^{-1+2\beta+6\tau}{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}\,.

(39)

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
:
Since
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}|\lesssim d^{1/2+2\beta-\tau/4}
, it suffices to use the very crude Berry-Esseen bound. Note that for
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
, we have that
\min\{(1-\text{Re}(z))^{-1},n\}\geq d/2
, and so Claim
3.7
gives that

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 1\Big]\lesssim d^{-1/2}

for all
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
. We get that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}^{(3)}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq 2{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\lesssim d^{-\tau/4+2\beta}\,.

(40)

To conclude the proof, we plug in the bounds (
37
), (
38
), (
39
) and (
40
) into the inequality (
4.1
) to get that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\,:\ f_{n}(\alpha)=0\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{-5/4}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{2}+d^{-\tau/4+2\beta}+e^{-c\log^{2}d}\,,

which, by taking
d
to be large enough (which amounts to taking
r_{0}
sufficiently close to
1
), concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

4.2
From close roots to quadratic approximations

Looking ahead to the proof of Proposition
3.1
, the main idea is to show that under the quasi-random condition that the derivatives of
f_{n}
are controlled (Lemma
3.3
) and that the derivative evaluated at a root cannot be too small (Lemma
4.3
), two close roots implies some small-ball relation for the random vector
\big(f_{n}(z),f_{n}^{\prime}(z),f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)\big)
, evaluated at a net point
z\in\mathcal{N}
nearby. The precise statement takes form in the next two claims.

Claim 4.4
.

For all
\beta\in(0,10^{-2})
and for all
d\geq d_{0}(\beta)
large enough the following holds. For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
{\varepsilon}>d^{-\beta}
and
t\in(0,d^{-5/4+\beta})
, suppose that
f
is an analytic function for which we have

\max_{\zeta\in\mathbb{D}(z,d^{-5/4+\beta})}|f^{(j)}(\zeta)|\leq d^{j+1/2}\log^{2}d\,,\qquad\text{for }j\in\{2,3\}.

If there exists
w\in\mathbb{D}(z,t)
for which
f(z)=f(w)=0
and
|f^{\prime}(z)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}
, then

\frac{2|f^{\prime}(z)|}{|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\leq t\cdot\big(1+d^{-\beta}\big)\,.

Proof.

Taylor’s theorem implies that

\Big|f(w)-f(z)-(w-z)f^{\prime}(z)-\frac{(w-z)^{2}}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(z)\Big|\leq|w-z|^{3}d^{7/2}\log^{2}d\,,

which shows that for
d
large enough,

|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{t}{2}|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|+d^{1+3\beta}\,.

Recalling the lower bound on
|f^{\prime}(z)|
, we see that

|f^{\prime}(z)|\big(1+d^{-\beta}\big)^{-1}\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|-d^{1+3\beta}\leq\frac{t}{2}|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\,.\qed

Claim 4.5
.

For all
\beta\in(0,10^{-2})
and for all
d\geq d_{0}(\beta)
large enough the following holds. For all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
{\varepsilon}\geq d^{-\beta/2}
,
t\in(0,d^{-5/4+\beta})
and
\delta\in(0,d^{-5/4-\beta})
, suppose that
f
is an analytic function for which

\max_{\zeta\in\mathbb{D}(z,d^{-5/4+\beta})}|f^{(j)}(\zeta)|\leq d^{j+1/2}\log^{2}d\,,\qquad\text{for }j\in\{2,3\}.

If there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}(z,\delta)
for which
f(\alpha)=0
,
|f^{\prime}(\alpha)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}
and
\tfrac{2|f^{\prime}(\alpha)|}{|f^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)|}\leq t
, then

|f(z)|\leq\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\cdot\big(1+d^{-\beta}\big)\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{2|f^{\prime}(z)|}{|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\leq t\cdot\big(1+d^{-\beta/2}\big)\,.

Proof.

We start by noting that

\big|f^{\prime}(z)-f^{\prime}(\alpha)\big|\leq\delta\max_{\zeta\in\mathbb{D}(z,d^{-5/4+\beta})}|f^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)|\leq d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d\,,

and so, by the lower bound on
|f^{\prime}(\alpha)|
, we have

|f^{\prime}(z)|\geq|f^{\prime}(\alpha)|-|f^{\prime}(z)-f^{\prime}(\alpha)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4-\beta/2}

for all
d
large enough. Combining this with the inequality

|f(z)|=|f(z)-f(\alpha)|\leq\delta|f^{\prime}(z)|+\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}d^{5/2}\log^{2}d\,,

gives

|f(z)|\leq\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\cdot\big(1+d^{-\beta}\big)\,.

To obtain the second conclusion of the claim, we use a similar argument to bound

\displaystyle|f^{\prime}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq|f^{\prime}(\alpha)|+d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}t|f^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)|+d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}t|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|+\frac{1}{2}t\delta d^{7/2}\log^{2}d+d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d\leq\frac{1}{2}t|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|+O(d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d)\,.

Since
|f^{\prime}(z)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4-\beta/2}
, we get that

|f^{\prime}(z)|-O(d^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}d)\geq|f^{\prime}(z)|\big(1+d^{-\beta/2}\big)^{-1}

which gives what we wanted.
∎

4.3
Bounding the probability of close roots on each annulus

We will now apply a net argument once more to show that with high probability there are no close roots in each of the annuli
\mathcal{A}_{k}
for
k\in\{0,\ldots,N\}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

There exists absolute constants
C,\beta>0
and
r_{0}<1
so that the following holds. For each
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}
,
{\varepsilon}\in(d_{k}^{-\beta/2},1)
and
\gamma\in(d_{k}^{-\beta},{\varepsilon})
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\gamma d_{k}^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{such that }\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\leq C{\varepsilon}\,.

Proof.

As
k\in\{0,\ldots,N\}
is kept fixed throughout the proof we will write
d=d_{k}=(1-\rho_{k})^{-1}
to lighten the notation. Denote the event

\mathcal{E}_{1}=\Big\{\max_{\zeta\in\rho_{k}\mathbb{D}}|f_{n}^{(j)}(\zeta)|\leq d^{j+1/2}\log^{2}d\,,\ j\in\{2,3\}\Big\}\,,

and note that
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\big]\leq\exp(-c\log^{2}d)
by Lemma
3.3
. We will also consider the event

\mathcal{E}_{2}=\Big\{\forall\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{such that}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=0\ \text{we have}\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big\}

and note that, by Lemma
4.3
we have
