\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}\big]=\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{such that}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=0\ \text{and}\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\Big]\leq C{\varepsilon}\,.

Recall the definition of the net
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{k}
from the proof of Lemma
4.3
, that is, the net as in Definition
4.1
with
\delta=d^{-5/4-\beta}
and
M=\lfloor 10/\delta\rfloor
. By combining Claim
4.4
and Claim
4.5
with the bounds above, we see that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\gamma d_{k}^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{such that }\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\\
\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]+C{\varepsilon}+e^{-c\log^{2}d}\,.

(41)

Therefore, it remains to show that the sum (
41
) is
O({\varepsilon}^{-1}\gamma^{2})
and the proof will be finished. Indeed, we recall that notation
\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)},\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)},\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
as given by (
34
) and (
4.1
) and show the desired bound holds for each sum separately.

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}
:

We break down the probability in (
41
) into a dyadic sum, according to the possible values of
|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor 4\log\log d\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\in[{\varepsilon}2^{k-1}d^{5/4},{\varepsilon}2^{k}d^{5/4}]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor 4\log\log d\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2^{k+1}{\varepsilon}d^{-\beta}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq{\varepsilon}2^{k}d^{5/4}\,,|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/2}2^{k-1}/\gamma\Big]\,.

(42)

Since the points
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}
are
d^{7\tau}
-smooth, Lemma
3.9
implies that for each
k=0,\ldots\lfloor 4\log\log d\rfloor
we have the bound

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2^{k+1}{\varepsilon}d^{-\beta}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq{\varepsilon}2^{k}d^{5/4}\,,|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq{\varepsilon}d^{5/2}2^{k-1}/\gamma\Big]\\
\lesssim 16^{k}{\varepsilon}^{4}d^{-3/2-2\beta}\exp\big(-c{\varepsilon}^{2}4^{k}/\gamma^{2}\big)\,.

Plugging into (
4.3
) gives that

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]\\
\lesssim d^{-3/2-2\beta}{\varepsilon}^{4}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor 4\log\log d\rfloor}16^{k}\exp\big(-c{\varepsilon}^{2}4^{k}/\gamma^{2}\big)\lesssim d^{-3/2-2\beta}\,{\varepsilon}^{4}\exp\big(-c{\varepsilon}^{2}/\gamma^{2}\big)\,.

Since
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}|\lesssim d^{3/2+2\beta}
, we get that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt s}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{4}\exp\big(-c{\varepsilon}^{2}/\gamma^{2}\big)\lesssim{\varepsilon}\,.

(43)

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
:

Here we do not need to break down the probability dyadically. We first simply bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq

\displaystyle|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\gamma d^{-\beta}\log^{2}d\,,|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq\gamma d^{5/4}\log^{2}d\Big]

(44)

and subsequently bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\gamma d^{-\beta}\log^{2}d\,,|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq\gamma d^{5/4}\log^{2}d\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq{\varepsilon}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\log^{2}d\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\log^{4}d}{d^{3/2}}\,{\varepsilon}^{4}\,.

Since
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}|\lesssim d^{1/2+\beta+7\tau}
, we bound

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{4}\frac{d^{\beta+8\tau}}{d}\lesssim{\varepsilon}\,.

(45)

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}
:

The argument here is identical to the argument above for
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}
. The only difference is that we need to apply Claim
3.6
instead of Claim
3.5
(as points
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}
are at distance at least
d^{-\tau/4}
from the real axis), which in turn adds an extra
d^{2\tau}
factor on the right-hand side of (
45
), which does no harm. We get that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{4}\frac{d^{\beta+10\tau}}{d}\lesssim{\varepsilon}\,.

(46)

The sum over
\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
:
Recall that for all
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
we have that
\min\{(1-\text{Re}(z))^{-1},n\}\geq d/2
. Using the definition of the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we can bound the probability from (
41
) by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot

\displaystyle|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2\gamma d^{-\beta}\log^{2}d\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq\gamma d^{5/4}\log^{2}d\Big]\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 1\Big]\lesssim d^{-1/2}

where the last inequality follows Claim
3.7
. Since
|\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}|\lesssim d^{1/2+2\beta-\tau/4}
we get that

\sum_{z\in\mathcal{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f(z)|\leq 2\delta\cdot|f^{\prime}(z)|\,,\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}d^{5/4}\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|\cdot\gamma d^{-5/4}\,,\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\Big]\leq d^{2\beta-\tau/4}\leq{\varepsilon}\,.

(47)

Plugging the bounds (
43
), (
45
), (
46
) and (
47
) into the inequality (
41
) finally gives that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\gamma d_{k}^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{such that }\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\lesssim{\varepsilon}\,.\qed

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We start by proving that for each
{\varepsilon}>0
and
L\geq 1
there is an
r_{0}<1
and
K>0
so that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{D}\big(r_{0},1-K/n\big)\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,Ln^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\leq{\varepsilon}/2\,,

(48)

for all
n\geq 1
large enough. Recall that
d_{k}=(1-\rho_{k})^{-1}
where
\rho_{k}
is given by (
33
). We apply Lemma
4.6
with
\gamma_{k}=\max\{d_{k}^{-\beta},L(d_{k}/n)^{5/4}\}
and
{\varepsilon}_{k}=\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}
and see that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\gamma d_{k}^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{such that }\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\lesssim d_{k}^{-\beta/2}+L^{4}\Big(\frac{d_{k}}{n}\Big)^{5}\,.

Hence, we can apply the union bound and sum over the sequence of dyadic annuli
\{\mathcal{A}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}
to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}\big(r_{0},1-K/n\big)\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(z,Ln^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\gamma d_{k}^{-5/4})\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{such that }\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{N}d_{k}^{-\beta/2}+L^{4}\Big(\frac{d_{k}}{n}\Big)^{5}\lesssim d_{N}^{-\beta/2}+L^{4}\Big(\frac{d_{0}}{n}\Big)^{5}\lesssim(1-r_{0})^{\beta/2}+\frac{L^{4}}{K^{5}}\,.

Taking now
r_{0}
close to
1
and
K\geq 1
large enough yields (
48
). It remains to handle the close roots from the disk
r_{0}\mathbb{D}
, for some
r_{0}<1
fixed. Indeed, we will prove the slightly stronger statement: for each
r_{0}<1
and
{\varepsilon}>0
there exists
\delta>0
so that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in r_{0}\mathbb{D}\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\delta)\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\leq{\varepsilon}/2\,,

(49)

Since
L/n^{5/4}\leq\delta
for large enough
n
, we can combine (
48
) and (
49
) together with the union bound to conclude the proof of Proposition
3.1
, so it only remains to prove (
49
).

Recall the definition (
6
) of the random Taylor series
F
. By Theorem
1.3
, we see that

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in r_{0}\mathbb{D}\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\delta)\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]=\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in r_{0}\mathbb{D}\,:F(\alpha)=F^{\prime}(\alpha)=0\Big]=0\,.

Therefore, for some
\delta>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in r_{0}\mathbb{D}\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\delta)\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\leq{\varepsilon}/2\,.

By Hurwitz’s theorem, as
n\to\infty
the roots of
f_{n}
inside
r_{0}\mathbb{D}
converge almost surely to those of the analogous Taylor series
F
. In particular, we have the convergence

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in r_{0}\mathbb{D}\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\delta)\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\\
=\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in r_{0}\mathbb{D}\,,\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}(\alpha,\delta)\setminus\{\alpha\}\ \text{s.t.}\ F(\alpha)=F(\alpha^{\prime})=0\Big]\leq{\varepsilon}/2

which proves (
49
), and we are done.
∎

5
Reducing Theorem
3.2
to a count over a net

Let
K\geq 1
be a fixed large constant, and recall the notation
\Omega_{K}=\mathbb{H}\cap\mathcal{A}(1-K/n,1+K/n)
given by (
22
). The main goal of this section will be to prove Theorem
3.2
, which states that the random variable
X_{n}(U)
given by (
23
) converges in distribution to a Poisson random variable as
n\to\infty
, for all fixed
K
. Below we will reduce the count of close roots to a count over a net of smooth points in
\Omega_{K}
whose quadratic approximation suggests there will be two close roots present nearby. After that, we will prove the Poisson limit holds for the count over the net via a moment computation (see Proposition
5.4
below), which will be performed in Section
6
below. We start by defining the following net in the annulus
\Omega_{K}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Set
\delta=n^{-5/4-\beta}
and
M_{1}=\lceil 4K/(\delta n)\rceil
,
M_{2}=\lceil 4/\delta\rceil
. The
\delta
-net
\mathsf{N}
of
\Omega_{K}
by

\mathsf{N}=\bigg\{\Big(1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a}{M_{1}}\Big)e\big(\pi b/M_{2}\big)\,:\,a\in\{0,1,\ldots,M_{1}\}\,,\,b\in\{1,\ldots,M_{2}\}\bigg\}\,.

For a point
z=\big(1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a}{M_{1}}\big)e\big(\pi b/M_{2}\big)\in\mathsf{N}
we further denote by

\mathsf{R}_{z}=\bigg\{\Big(1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a+s}{M_{1}}\Big)e\big(\pi(b+t)/M_{2}\big)\,:\,s,t\in[-1/2,1/2]\bigg\}\cap\mathbb{H}\,.

Note that the collection
\{\mathsf{R}_{z}\}_{z\in\mathsf{N}}
of (polar) rectangles cover the annulus
\Omega_{K}
. We will also use the notation
\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\subset\mathsf{R}_{z}
, to denote the smaller rectangles

\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}=\bigg\{\Big(1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a+s}{M_{1}}\Big)e\big(\pi(b+t)/M_{2}\big)\,:\,s,t\in[-1/2+n^{-\beta/2},1/2-n^{-\beta/2}]\bigg\}\cap\mathbb{H}\,.

We further split the net into the smooth points and non-smooth points, analogous to what we did in (
34
). Denote by

\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}=\Big\{z\in\mathsf{N}\,:\,z\ \text{is }n^{7\tau}\text{-smooth}\Big\}\,,

and set
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}=\mathsf{N}\setminus\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
.

For
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
and an finite union of intervals
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we define the event

A_{z}(U)=\left\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-z\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\,,\ \frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in n^{-5/4}U\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\right\}

(50)

The basic idea is that the event (
50
) implies that the quadratic approximation of
f_{n}
at the point
z
will predict a root in the rectangle
\mathsf{R}_{z}
, and also a second root at distance
n^{-5/4}U
from
z
. Heuristically, we want to show that with high probability as
n\to\infty
,

X_{n}(U)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}(U)}\,.

(51)

Furthermore, via a moment computation, we will show that the right-hand side of (
51
) has a Poisson scaling limit. The reason for the prefactor
\frac{1}{2}
on the right-hand side of (
51
) is that each pair of close roots makes two net points “ring”, while it is only counted once in
X_{n}(U)
. In turn, the goal of this section is to prove (
51
) in its more technical form, given by Proposition
5.3
below as a two-sided inequality. In Section
6
we will perform the moment computation which shows that both random variables converges in law to the same Poisson variable as
n\to\infty
.

5.1
Setting up the two-sided inequality

We will consider the following “bad” events

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\exists\alpha\in\Omega_{K}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=0,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|\leq\frac{2n^{5/4}}{\log n}\bigg\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\exists\alpha\in\bigcup_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}}\mathsf{R}_{z}\,,\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\Big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\Big)\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\bigg\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{3}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\exists\alpha\in\bigcup_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}\mathsf{R}_{z}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\,,\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\Big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\Big)\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\bigg\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{2}\cup\mathcal{B}_{3}\,.

(52)

We will also consider the “good” event

\mathcal{G}=\bigg\{\max_{0\leq j\leq 3}\max_{z\in\Omega_{2K}}\frac{|f_{n}^{(j)}(z)|}{n^{j+1/2}}\leq\log^{2}n\bigg\}\,,

(53)

and note that by Lemma
3.3
we have
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]\leq\exp(-c\log^{2}n)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

With the above notations, we have
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}\big]=1
.

On the likely event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
, we can formulate (
51
) more precisely. To do this we will need to introduce some further notation. For
z\in\mathsf{N}
define

\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp}=\bigg\{\Big(1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a+s}{M_{1}}\Big)e\big(\pi(b+t)/M_{2}\big)\,:\,s,t\in[-1/2+n^{-3\beta/4},1/2-n^{-3\beta/4}]\bigg\}\cap\mathbb{H}\,,

and note that
\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\subset\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp}\subset\mathsf{R}_{z}
. Furthermore, for a finite union of intervals
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we set

U^{+}=\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\,:\,\text{dist}(x,U)\leq\frac{1}{\log n}\Big\}\,,\quad U^{-}=\Big\{x\in U\,:\,\text{dist}(x,U^{c})\geq\frac{1}{\log n}\Big\}\,.

(54)

Finally, we set

A_{z}^{+}(U)=\bigg\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-z\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp}\,,\ \frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in n^{-5/4}U^{+}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\bigg\}\,,

(55)

and

A_{z}^{-}(U)=\bigg\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-z\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\,,\ \frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in n^{-5/4}U^{-}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\bigg\}\,.

(56)

Note that
A_{z}^{-}(U)\subset A_{z}^{+}(U)
. This leads us to the definition

X_{n}^{\pm}(U)=\sum_{\{z,w\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}^{\pm}(U)\cap A_{w}^{\pm}(U)}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z-w|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}\,,

(57)

where we denote by
\binom{S}{2}
the set of all unordered pairs from a finite set
S
.

Proposition 5.3
.

On the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
we have

X_{n}^{-}(U)\leq X_{n}(U)\leq X_{n}^{+}(U)\,.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\geq 1
recall that the falling factorial is given by

(x)_{m}=x(x-1)\cdots(x-m+1)\,.

We will show that both
X_{n}^{+}(U)
and
X_{n}^{-}(U)
have limiting moments matching those of a Poisson random variable, with the intensity
{\lambda}_{K,U}
given by (
24
). This is stated in the next proposition, the proof of which is postponed to Section
6
below.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
X_{n}^{+}(U)
and
X_{n}^{-}(U)
be given by (
57
). Then for all
m\geq 1
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(X_{n}^{+}(U)\big)_{m}\Big]\leq\big({\lambda}_{K,U}\big)^{m}\,,

and

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(X_{n}^{-}(U)\big)_{m}\Big]\geq\big({\lambda}_{K,U}\big)^{m}\,.

Assuming for the moment Propositions
5.3
and
5.4
, we can provide the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Since
X_{n}^{-}(U)\leq X_{n}^{+}(U)
deterministically, Proposition
5.4
implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(X_{n}^{+}(U)\big)_{m}\Big]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(X_{n}^{-}(U)\big)_{m}\Big]=\big({\lambda}_{K,U}\big)^{m}\,.

In particular, by the method of moments, we conclude that both
X_{n}^{+}(U)
and
X_{n}^{-}(U)
converge in distribution, as
n\to\infty
, to a Poisson random variable with parameter
{\lambda}_{K,U}
. By combing Proposition
5.3
with Lemma
5.2
, we conclude that for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathbb{P}\big[X_{n}^{+}(U)\geq s\big]-o(1)\leq\mathbb{P}\big[X_{n}(U)\geq s\big]\leq\mathbb{P}\big[X_{n}^{-}(U)\geq s\big]+o(1)

as
n\to\infty
, which implies the desired convergence for
X_{n}(U)
as well.
∎

The remaining of this section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
5.2
and Proposition
5.3
.

5.2
Upper bound

Before proving the upper bound in Proposition
5.3
, we require the following application of Rouché’s theorem.

Claim 5.5
.

For all
\beta>0
and for each
K\geq 1
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the following holds for all large enough
n
. On the event
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{G}
, if
\alpha\in R_{z}^{\circ}
with
f_{n}(\alpha)=0
then

z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\in R_{z}^{\sharp}\,.

Furthermore, if there exists some
\alpha^{\prime}
such that
f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0
and
|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U
, then

\frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in U^{+}\,.

Proof.

On the event
\mathcal{G}
, we can Taylor expand around
\alpha
and observe that

\displaystyle\alpha-\Big(z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\alpha-z+\frac{f_{n}(\alpha)+(z-\alpha)f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+O(n^{-2\beta}\log^{2}n)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+O(n^{-\beta}\log^{2}n)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{on }\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}}}{{=}}(\alpha-z)-(\alpha-z)+O(n^{-5/4-2\beta}\log^{3}n)=O(n^{-5/4-2\beta}\log^{3}n)\,,

which proves the first assertion. Another Taylor expansion shows also that

f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=f_{n}(\alpha)+(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)^{2}\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}{2}+O(|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|^{3}n^{7/2}\log^{2}n)\,.

Since
f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=f_{n}(\alpha)=0
, we can rearrange the above and get that

0=f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}{2}+O(|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|^{2}n^{7/2}\log^{2}n)\,.

(58)

Now, since
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}
holds and since
U
lies in a compact subset of
(0,\infty)
, (
58
) implies that
|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)|\gtrsim n^{5/2}/\log n
, which by (
58
) shows that

\Big|\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}\Big|=|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|+O(n^{-3/2}\log^{3}n)\,.

On the event
\mathcal{G}
we also have

\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}=\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}+O(n^{-5/4-\beta}\log^{4}n)

which, together with the above, implies that
\frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in U^{+}
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
: the upper bound.

We want to show here that on the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
we have
X_{n}(U)\leq X_{n}^{+}(U)
. Indeed, denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
the set of roots of
f_{n}
, and note that

X_{n}(U)=\sum_{\{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\}\in\binom{\mathcal{Z}\cap\Omega_{K}}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\}}\,.

Set
\Omega_{\tt s}^{\circ}=\Omega_{K}\cap\big(\bigcup_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\big)
. On the event
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{3}^{c}
, we have

X_{n}(U)=\sum_{\{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\}\in\binom{\mathcal{Z}\cap\Omega_{\tt s}^{\circ}}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\}}\,.

Next, we argue that on the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}
, there is no pair of roots
\{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\}\in\binom{\mathcal{Z}\cap\Omega_{\tt s}^{\circ}}{2}
so what
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}
for some
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
. Indeed, if there was such a pair, then Taylor’s theorem would imply that

0=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})-f_{n}(\alpha)=(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+O(|\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha|^{2}\,n^{5/2}\log^{2}n)\,,

which in turn shows that
|f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|=O(n^{5/4-\beta}\log^{2}n)
, which cannot hold on
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}
. Combining the above, we see that on the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
we can write

X_{n}(U)=\sum_{\{z,w\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ},\,\exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\circ}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\ \text{and}\ |\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\big\}}\,.

Claim
5.5
shows that

\mathbf{1}_{\big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ},\,\exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\circ}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\ \text{and}\ |\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\big\}}\leq\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}^{+}(U)\cap A_{w}^{+}(U)}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z-w|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}

on the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
, which in view of (
57
) implies the upper bound.
∎

5.3
Lower bound

We start working towards the lower bound in Proposition
5.3
. First, we will show that the event
A_{z}(U)
given by (
50
) indicates there is a root of
f_{n}
nearby due to a linear approximation around
z
.

Claim 5.6
.

For all
\beta>0
and for each
K\geq 1
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the following holds for all large enough
n
. If
z\in\mathsf{N}
is such that

|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\,,\qquad z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\,,

and the event
\mathcal{G}
holds, then there exists some
\alpha
with
f_{n}(\alpha)=0
and

\Big|\alpha-\Big(z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\Big)\Big|\leq n^{-5/4-2\beta}\log^{4}n\,.

Proof.

Consider the linear approximation of
f_{n}
around
z
, that is the linear function

L_{z}(w)=f_{n}(z)+(w-z)f_{n}^{\prime}(z)

and denote by
\zeta=z-f_{n}(z)/f_{n}^{\prime}(z)
the unique root of
L_{z}
. Note that by the definition of
\mathsf{R}_{z}
we have
|z-\zeta|\leq 2n^{-5/4-\beta}
. We will apply Rouché’s theorem to show that
f_{n}
and
L_{z}
have the same number of roots inside a disk of radius
r=n^{-5/4-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}
around
\zeta
. First note that

\min_{|w|=r}|L_{z}(w+\zeta)|=r|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq n^{{-2\beta}}(\log n)^{3}\,.

On the other hand, on the event
\mathcal{G}
we have

\displaystyle\max_{|w|=r}|f_{n}(w+\zeta)-L_{z}(w+\zeta)|

\displaystyle\lesssim|w+\zeta-z|^{2}n^{5/2}\log^{2}n

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(|w-z|^{2}+r^{2}\big)n^{5/2}\log^{2}n

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(n^{-5/2-4\beta}(\log n)^{8}+n^{-5/2-2\beta}\big)n^{5/2}\log^{2}n\lesssim n^{-2\beta}(\log n)^{2}\,.

and by Rouché we know that
f_{n}
has a root
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}(\zeta,r)
, as desired.
∎

Next, we show that the quadratic approximation provides us with the second root.

Lemma 5.7
.

For all
\beta>0
and for each
K\geq 1
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the following holds for all large enough
n
. If
\mathcal{G}
holds and
z\in\mathsf{N}
is such that

|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\,,\qquad z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\,,\qquad\frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\leq\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\,,

then there exists some
\alpha^{\prime}
with
f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0
and

\Big|\alpha^{\prime}-\Big(z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\Big)\Big|\leq n^{-5/4-2\beta}\log^{5}n\,.

Proof.

As before, we denote by
r=n^{-5/4-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}
. By Claim
5.6
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}\Big(z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)},r\Big)
so that
f_{n}(\alpha)=0
. Set

T_{\alpha}(w)=(w-\alpha)f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+\frac{(w-\alpha)^{2}}{2}f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)

and note that
T_{\alpha}
has exactly two roots; one at
\alpha
and another at

\zeta=\alpha-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}\,.

On the event
\mathcal{G}
, the assumptions of the lemma also implies that

|\zeta-\alpha|=2\frac{|f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)|}\geq 2\frac{|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|+O\big(|z-\alpha|n^{5/2}\log^{2}n\big)}{n^{5/2}\log^{2}n}\gtrsim\frac{n^{-5/4}}{(\log n)^{3}}\,.

Since
z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\in\mathsf{R}_{z}
, we also have
|z-\alpha|\leq 2n^{-5/4-\beta}
. As
|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq n^{5/2}/\log^{2}n
, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}+(\alpha-z)\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}+O\big(n^{-3/2-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+O\big(n^{-1/4-\beta}(\log n)^{4}\big)}\bigg(\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}+\alpha-z\bigg)+O\big(n^{-3/2-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}+\alpha-z+O\big(n^{-3/2-\beta}(\log n)^{5}\big)\,.

(59)

Therefore

|\zeta-\alpha|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:proof_of_lemma:quadratic_approximation_predicts_second_root}}}{{\lesssim}}\Big|\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}+\alpha-z\Big|\leq\Big|\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}\Big|+\Big|\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\Big|+r\lesssim n^{-5/4}\log n\,.

We will apply Rouché’s theorem to show that
T_{\alpha}
and
f_{n}
both have exactly one root in the disk
\mathbb{D}(\zeta,r)
. Indeed, first note that

\displaystyle\min_{|w|=r}\big|T_{\alpha}(\zeta+w)\big|

\displaystyle=\min_{|w|=r}\Big|\Big(w-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}\Big)f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+\Big(w-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}\Big)^{2}\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}{2}\Big|

\displaystyle=\min_{|w|=r}\Big|-wf_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)+w^{2}\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\geq r\frac{n^{5/4}}{2\log n}-r^{2}n^{5/2}\log^{2}n\gtrsim n^{-2\beta}(\log n)^{3}\,.

On the other hand, we have the upper bound

\displaystyle\max_{|w|=r}\big|T_{\alpha}(\zeta+w)-f_{n}(\zeta+w)\big|

\displaystyle\leq|\zeta+w-\alpha|^{3}n^{7/2}\log^{2}n

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(n^{-15/4}(\log n)^{3}+r^{3}\Big)n^{7/2}\log^{2}n\lesssim n^{-1/4}(\log n)^{5}\,.

Therefore, we conclude that for all
n
large enough Rouché applies, and hence
f_{n}
has a root
\alpha^{\prime}
in the disk
\mathbb{D}(\zeta,r)
. It remains to observe that

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\alpha-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(\alpha)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)}+O\big(n^{-5/4-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:proof_of_lemma:quadratic_approximation_predicts_second_root}}}{{=}}z-\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-2\Big(\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}+\alpha-z\Big)+O\big(n^{-5/4-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}\big)

\displaystyle=z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}+O\big(n^{-5/4-2\beta}(\log n)^{4}\big)

and hence

\Big|\alpha^{\prime}-\Big(z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\Big)\Big|\lesssim n^{-5/4-2\beta}\log^{4}n\,,

and we are done.
∎

Finally, the next lemma shows that on the typical event of having two close roots, all other roots are separated by an almost macroscopic amount. This fact will be useful in Section
6
, when we actually need to compute the relevant moments.

Lemma 5.8
.

For all
\beta>0
and for each
K\geq 1
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the following holds for all large enough
n
. If
A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{G}
holds for some
z\in\Omega_{K}
, then
f_{n}
has exactly two roots in a ball of radius
\frac{1}{n\log^{4}n}
centered at
z
. The same statement continues to hold if
A_{z}(U)
is replaced by
A_{z}^{+}(U)
or by
A_{z}^{-}(U)
.

Proof.

We will provide the proof only for the event
A_{z}(U)
, as the cases
A_{z}^{+}(U),A_{z}^{-}(U)
are similar. Define the polynomial

P_{z}(w)=f_{n}(z)+(w-z)f_{n}^{\prime}(z)+\frac{(w-z)^{2}}{2}f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)

and set
t=\frac{1}{n(\log n)^{4}}
. We will apply Rouché’s theorem to show that
f_{n}
and
P_{z}
have the same number of roots in the disk
\mathbb{D}(z,t)
. By Claim
5.6
and Lemma
5.7
, we already know that
f_{n}
has at least two roots inside
\mathbb{D}(z,t)
, and since
P_{z}
has at most two roots, the proof will follow. On the event
A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{G}
, we have

|f_{n}(z)|=O(n^{-\beta}\log^{2}n)\,,\qquad|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|=O(n^{-5/4}\log^{2}n)\,,\qquad|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}|\gtrsim n^{5/2}/\log n\,.

From the above, the lower bound follows since

\min_{|w|=t}|P_{z}(w+z)|\geq\frac{t^{2}}{2}|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|-|f_{n}(z)|-t|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\gtrsim\frac{n^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{9}}\,.

The event
\mathcal{G}
also gives an upper bound, namely

\max_{|w|=t}|P_{z}(w+z)-f_{n}(w+z)|\lesssim t^{3}n^{7/2}\log^{2}n\lesssim\frac{n^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{10}}\,.

By Rouché, the two displayed equations above imply that
P_{z}
and
f_{n}
have the same number of roots in
\mathbb{D}(z,t)
, completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are finally ready to provide the lower bound in Proposition
5.3
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
: the lower bound.

As in the proof of the upper bound, on the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
we can write

X_{n}(U)=\sum_{\{z,w\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ},\,\exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\circ}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\ \text{and}\ |\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\big\}}\,.

On the event
\mathcal{G}
, we can apply Claim
5.6
and Lemma
5.7
to conclude that for each
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
for which
A_{z}^{-}(U)
holds, there exist two distinct roots
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}^{\prime}
with
\alpha_{1}\in\mathsf{R}_{z}
and
|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U
. Similarly, if
w\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\setminus\{z\}
is another point for which
A_{w}^{-}(U)
holds and
|z-w|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}
, then
f_{n}
has two distinct roots
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}^{\prime}
where
\alpha_{2}\in\mathsf{R}_{w}
and
|\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U
. We must have
\alpha_{1}\not=\alpha_{2}
since
\mathsf{R}_{z}\cap\mathsf{R}_{w}=\emptyset
. Furthermore, by Lemma
5.8
we have that the set
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\}
contains exactly two elements, and we conclude that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}^{\prime}
and
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}^{\prime}
. This shows that for any pair
\{z,w\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}
we have

\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}^{-}(U)\cap A_{w}^{-}(U)}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z-w|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ},\,\exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\circ}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\ \text{and}\ |\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|\in n^{-5/4}U\big\}}

on the event
\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}^{c}
, and the desired lower bound follows.
∎

5.4
The “bad” event
\mathcal{B}
is rare

Now that we concluded the proof of Proposition
5.3
, we use the remainder of the section to prove Lemma
5.2
. Recall that
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}=\mathsf{N}\setminus\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
and further decompose as

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\big\{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}\,:\,\arg(z)\in[n^{-1},\pi-n^{-1}]\big\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\big\{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}\,:\,\arg(z)\in[n^{-1-\tau/4},n^{-1}]\cup[\pi-n^{-1},\pi-n^{-1-\tau/4}]\big\}\,,

(60)

\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\big\{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}\,:\,\arg(z)\in[0,n^{-1-\tau/4}]\cup[\pi-n^{-1-\tau/4},\pi]\big\}\,.

We note that as
n\to\infty
, we have

|\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}|=M_{1}M_{2}\big(1+o(1)\big)=16Kn^{3/2+2\beta}\big(1+o(1)\big)\,,

(61)

and furthermore

\displaystyle|\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim M_{1}M_{2}n^{7\tau-1}\lesssim Kn^{1/2+2\beta+7\tau}\,,

\displaystyle|\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim M_{1}M_{2}n^{-1}\lesssim Kn^{1/2+2\beta}\,,

(62)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim M_{1}M_{2}n^{-1-\tau/4}\lesssim Kn^{1/2+2\beta-\tau/4}\,.

Towards the proof of Lemma
5.2
, we start by showing that with high probability there is no pair of close roots in the non-smooth part of the annulus
\bigcup_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}}\mathsf{R}_{z}
. This is shown via a net argument similar to the one presented in Section
4
.

Claim 5.9
.

We have
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}\big]=0
.

Proof.

By the union bound, we have

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}\big]\leq\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}}\mathbb{P}\Big[\big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\,,\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\Big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\Big)\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\big\}\cap\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\Big]\,.

Furthermore, by combining Claim
4.4
with Claim
4.5
we get the inclusion

\big\{\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\,,\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\Big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\Big)\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\big\}\cap\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\\
\subset\big\{|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}(\log n)^{3}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}(\log n)^{3}\big\}\,,

and altogether

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{B}_{2}\big]\leq\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}}\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}(\log n)^{3}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}(\log n)^{3}\big]\,.

(63)

To bound the sum on the right-hand side of (
63
), we split the sum into
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
,
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}
and
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
as given by (
5.4
) and bound separately on each part.

The sum over
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
:

By Claim
3.5
, for each
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
we have the bound

\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{3}n\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{3}n\big]\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq\log n\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{3}n\big]\lesssim n^{-3/2}\log^{8}n\,.

Since
|\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}|\lesssim Kn^{1/2+2\beta+7\tau}
we conclude that

\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}}\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{3}n\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{3}n\big]\lesssim n^{-1+2\beta+7\tau}\log^{8}n\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,.

(64)

The sum over
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}
:

By Claim
3.6
we see that for all
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(1)}
we have

\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{3}n\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{3}n\big]\lesssim n^{-3/2+8\tau}\log^{6}n\,.

Combining with the fact that
|\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}|\lesssim Kn^{1/2+2\beta}
we get

\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(2)}}\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{3}n\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{3}n\big]\lesssim n^{-1+2\beta+8\tau}\log^{6}n\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,.

(65)

The sum over
\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
:

For the range
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}
of point very close to the real axis, we apply Claim
3.7
to conclude that

\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}}\mathbb{P}\big[|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{3}n\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{3}n\big]\lesssim|\mathsf{N}_{\tt ns}^{(3)}|n^{-1/2}\lesssim n^{-\tau/4+2\beta}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,,

(66)

for all
\beta>0
small enough.
Combining (
64
), (
65
), and (
66
) we see that the sum on the right-hand side of (
63
) tends to zero as
n\to\infty
, which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

Next, we show how that we can eliminate roots which are near the boundaries of smooth rectangles.

Claim 5.10
.

We have
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{3}\big]=0
.

Proof.

By the union bound we have

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{3}\big]\leq\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\,,\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\Big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\Big)\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\,,\mathcal{G}\Big]\,.

For
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
we cover the set
\mathsf{R}_{z}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}
with disks of radius
\widetilde{\delta}=\delta n^{-\beta/2}
so that the number of disks is
\lesssim n^{\beta/2}
(this is possible, as the area of
\mathsf{R}_{z}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}
is
\simeq\delta^{2}n^{-\beta/2}
). Denote for a moment by
\widetilde{z}
the center of an arbitrary disk
\mathbb{D}(\widetilde{z},\widetilde{\delta})
in the covering. By combining Claim
4.4
with Claim
4.5
, we conclude that on the event
\mathcal{G}
, if there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}(\widetilde{z},\widetilde{\delta})
and
\alpha\prime\in\mathbb{D}\big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\big)\setminus\{\alpha\}
such that
f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0
then we must have

|f_{n}(\widetilde{z})|\leq 2\widetilde{\delta}|f_{n}^{\prime}(\widetilde{z})|\,\,\qquad\text{and }\qquad|f_{n}^{\prime}(\widetilde{z})|\leq 2n^{5/4}(\log n)^{3}\,.

Furthermore, as
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
we know that any
\widetilde{z}
in the new
\widetilde{\delta}
-net is
n^{5\tau}
-smooth, in the sense of Definition
3.8
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.9
we have

\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(\widetilde{z})|\leq 2\widetilde{\delta}|f_{n}^{\prime}(\widetilde{z})|\,,|f_{n}^{\prime}(\widetilde{z})|\leq 2n^{5/4}(\log n)^{3}\Big]\\
\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(\widetilde{z})|\leq 4n^{-3\beta/2}(\log n)^{3}\,,|f_{n}^{\prime}(\widetilde{z})|\leq 2n^{5/4}(\log n)^{3}\Big]\lesssim n^{-3/2-3\beta}(\log n)^{8}\,.

Hence, a union bound implies that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\,,\ \text{and }\ \exists\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\Big(\alpha,\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\Big)\setminus\{\alpha\}\,:\,f_{n}(\alpha)=f_{n}(\alpha^{\prime})=0\,,\mathcal{G}\Big]\\
\lesssim n^{\beta/2}n^{-3/2-3\beta}(\log n)^{8}=n^{-3/2-2\beta-\beta/2}(\log n)^{8}\,.

Since
|\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}|\lesssim Kn^{3/2+2\beta}
, we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{3}\big]\lesssim n^{-\beta/2}(\log n)^{8}

and we are done.
∎

Putting everything together, we finally give the proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

By Lemma
3.3
we know that
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]=o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, and by Lemma
4.3
we also know that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}\big]=0\,.

Combining the above facts with Claim
5.9
and Claim
5.10
completes the lemma.
∎

6
Poisson limit for the sum over the net

In this section we prove Proposition
5.4
. We first recall some relevant notations from the previous section and give a general outline. Recall the “good” event

\mathcal{G}=\bigg\{\max_{0\leq j\leq 3}\max_{z\in\Omega_{2K}}\frac{|f_{n}^{(j)}(z)|}{n^{j+1/2}}\leq\log^{2}n\bigg\}\,,

By Lemma
3.3
, we have
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]\leq\exp\big(-c\log^{2}n\big)
. Furthermore, for
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
and
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we recall the events

\displaystyle A_{z}(U)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-z\in\mathsf{R}_{z}\,,\ \frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in n^{-5/4}U\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\bigg\}\,,

\displaystyle A_{z}^{+}(U)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-z\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp}\,,\ \frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in n^{-5/4}U^{+}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\bigg\}\,,

(67)

\displaystyle A_{z}^{-}(U)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-z\in\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\,,\ \frac{2|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|}{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|}\in n^{-5/4}U^{-}\,,\ |f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\geq\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log n}\bigg\}\,,

where
U^{\pm}
are given by (
54
). For a reminder on what the polar rectangles
\mathsf{R}_{z},\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp},\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}
are, we refer the reader to Definition
5.1
. The events above led us to consider

X_{n}^{\pm}(U)=\sum_{\{z,w\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}^{\pm}(U)\cap A_{w}^{\pm}(U)}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z-w|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}\,.

The goal of this section is to prove matching upper and lower bounds for the factorial moments of
X_{n}^{+}(U)
and
X_{n}^{-}(U)
, respectively. Below we will show that the limiting moments as
n\to\infty
match those of a Poisson random variable with parameter
{\lambda}_{K,U}=\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t
, where

\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{-K}^{K}\mathfrak{F}(x)\,{\rm d}x\,,

(68)

and
\mathfrak{F}
is given by (
78
) below. We will also use this opportunity to identify the limiting intensity
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}t^{3}{\rm d}t
for the Poisson process described in our main result Theorem
1.1
. Indeed, since
X_{n}(U)
given by (
23
) is asymptotically a Poisson random variable with parameter
{\lambda}_{K,U}
, and since
X_{n}(U)
and the set of roots at distance
n^{-5/4}U
do not differ with high probability for large values of
K>0
(see the proof of Theorem
1.1
in Section
3
), we set

\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}=\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathfrak{F}(x)\,{\rm d}x\,.

(69)

Indeed, by Claim
6.5
below we know in particular that the limit in (
69
) exists.

6.1
Factoring probabilities over spread tuples

We denote by
\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}
the probability distribution of the random polynomial
f_{n}
, in the case where the random coefficients are i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables. Recall Definition
3.10
of
\gamma
-spread tuples. We will need to know that for macroscopically spread tuples, the probabilities of seeing close roots approximately factors, and is the same as in the case of Gaussian coefficients. The exact formulation is given by the following lemma, the proof of which we postpone to Section
9
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For
m\geq 1
fixed let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}
be a
n^{\beta}
-spread tuple. Then, as
n\to\infty
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}(U)\big]=\big(1+o(1)\big)\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z_{j}}(U)\big]\,,

uniformly in
K>0
and
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in a compact set. Furthermore, the above remains true if the events on the left-hand side are replaced by
A_{z}^{+}(U)
or by
A_{z}^{-}(U)
.

We will also need the following bound, which shows that the probabilities for almost-macroscopically spread tuples is comparable to those obtained from a Gaussian computations, up to a tolerable poly-logarithmic lost.

Lemma 6.2
.

For
m\geq 1
fixed let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}
be a
\frac{1}{(\log n)^{5}}
-spread tuple. We have

\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{+}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}(U)\big]\lesssim_{m,K,U}\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\log^{O_{m}(1)}n\,.

Proof.

Recall that on the event
A_{z}^{+}(U)\cap\mathcal{G}
we have that

|f_{n}(z)|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{2}n\,,\qquad|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{2}n\,.

Therefore, we have the bound

\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{+}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}(U)\big]\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\big\{|f_{n}(z_{j})|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{2}n\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{j})|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{2}n\big\}\Big]+\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]\,.

(70)

Clearly, the 4-dimensional Lebesgue measure of the set

\mathbb{D}\big(0,n^{-1/2-\beta}\log^{2}n\big)\times\mathbb{D}\big(0,n^{-1/4}\log^{2}n\big)

is
\lesssim n^{-3/2-2\beta}\log^{O(1)}n
. Since we can cover the above set by disks of radius
n^{-2}
so that the sum of their measure is also
\lesssim n^{-3/2-2\beta}\log^{O(1)}n
, and since the tuple
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
is
\frac{1}{(\log n)^{5}}
-spread and
n^{7\tau}
-smooth, we can apply Proposition
3.13
and get that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\big\{|f_{n}(z_{j})|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{2}n\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{j})|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{2}n\big\}\Big]\lesssim\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\log^{O(1)}n\,.

Since
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]\leq\exp\big(-c\log^{2}n\big)
, in view of (
70
) we are done.
∎

We will also need another small-ball estimate, showing that on the event
A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{G}
, it is likely that at least one of
A_{w}(U)
occur, where
|z-w|\in n^{-5/4}U
. The following lemma will be used in the proof of the lower bound of Proposition
5.4
. For
z\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
consider the event

\Upsilon_{z}=A_{z}^{+}(U)\cap\Bigg\{z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\in\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
0<|z-w|\leq\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\end{subarray}}(\mathsf{R}_{w}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\circ})\bigg\}\,,

(71)

where we recall that
z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}
is the “quadratic prediction” for the location of the close root, see Lemma
5.7
for the precise statement.

Lemma 6.3
.

For
m\geq 1
fixed let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}
be a
n^{\beta}
-spread tuple. We have

\mathbb{P}\big[\Upsilon_{z_{1}}\cap A_{z_{2}}^{+}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}(U)\big]\lesssim_{m,K,U}\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\cdot n^{-\beta/10}\,.

Proof.

We start by bounding the Lebesgue measure of the set

\Xi_{n}=\Big\{(X,X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\,:\,|X|\leq\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/2+\beta}}\,,\\
\frac{1}{n^{1/4}\log n}\leq|X^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/4}}\,,\ |X^{\prime\prime}|\leq\log^{2}n\,,\ \frac{X}{nX^{\prime}}-\frac{2X^{\prime}}{nX^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
|z-w|\in n^{-5/4}U\end{subarray}}(\mathsf{R}_{w}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\circ}-z)\Big\}\,.

Indeed, we will show that

m(\Xi_{n})\lesssim n^{-3/2-2\beta-\beta/10}\,.

(72)

The set
\Xi_{n}
is relevant to our computation since for all
z\in\mathsf{N}
we have the

\Upsilon_{z}\cap\mathcal{G}\subset\Big\{\Big(\frac{f_{n}(z)}{n^{1/2}},\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{n^{3/2}},\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{n^{5/2}}\Big)\in\Xi_{n}\Big\}\,,

(73)

where
\Upsilon_{z}
is given by (
71
). After the bound (
72
) is established, we will apply Proposition
3.13
and argue as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
.
We first note that on
\Xi_{n}
we have

\Big|\frac{X}{nX^{\prime}}\Big|\leq\frac{(\log n)^{3}}{n^{5/4+\beta}}\,,

and since
\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\subset\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp}
with
m\big(\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\sharp}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{z}^{\circ}\big)\lesssim n^{-5/4-3\beta/2}
we get by the triangle inequality that

\Xi_{n}\subset\Big\{|X|\leq\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/2+\beta}}\,,\ \frac{1}{n^{1/4}\log n}\leq|X^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/4}}\,,\ |X^{\prime\prime}|\leq\log^{2}n\,,\ \frac{2X^{\prime}}{nX^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
|z-w|\in n^{-5/4}U\end{subarray}}(\mathsf{R}_{w}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\sharp}-z)\Big\}\,.

Furthermore, we have that

\frac{n^{5/4}}{\log^{2}n}\leq\frac{2n}{|X^{\prime}|}\leq 2n^{5/4}\log n\,.

Together with the simple bound

m(\mathsf{R}_{w}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\sharp})\lesssim n^{-5/4-5\beta/2}\,,

we get the following constraint on the variable
X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
: it must lie in the set

\Bigg(\bigg[(2n^{5/4}\log^{2}n)\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
|z-w|\in n^{-5/4}U\end{subarray}}(\mathsf{R}_{w}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\sharp}-z)\bigg]\setminus\mathbb{D}\Big(0,\frac{1}{\log^{2}n}\Big)\Bigg)^{-1}\,.

(74)

Here, by the inverse of a set
A\subset\mathbb{C}
we just mean
A^{-1}=\{z^{-1}\,:\,z\in A\}
. The set (
74
) might look complicated, but we really just need to estimate its measure, which is fairly straightforward. By the previous estimates we have

m\bigg((2n^{5/4}\log^{2}n)\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
|z-w|\in n^{-5/4}U\end{subarray}}(\mathsf{R}_{w}\setminus\mathsf{R}_{w}^{\sharp}-z)\bigg)\lesssim n^{-\beta/4}\log^{O(1)}n\,.

Moreover, for a bounded closet set
A\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
0\not\in A
we can change variables and observe that

m(A^{-1})=\int_{A^{-1}}\,{\rm d}m\lesssim\int_{A}\frac{{\rm d}m(z)}{|z|^{2}}\lesssim\frac{m(A)}{\text{dist}(0,A)^{2}}\,.

We conclude that the measure of the set (
74
) is
\lesssim n^{-\beta/4}\log^{O(1)}n
, and by bounding the constraints on
X,X^{\prime}
trivially we obtain

m(\Xi_{n})\lesssim m\Big(\mathbb{D}\Big(0,\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/2+\beta}}\Big)\Big)\times m\Big(\mathbb{D}\Big(0,\frac{\log^{2}n}{n^{1/4}}\Big)\Big)\times n^{-\beta/5}\lesssim n^{-1-2\beta}n^{-1/2}n^{-\beta/6}\,,

which yields (
72
) for
n
large enough. Since
\Xi_{n}
is a Lipschitz domain, we can cover the set
\Xi_{n}
with balls of radius
n^{-2}
so that the sum of their measure is
\lesssim n^{-3/2-2\beta-\beta/10}
. By combining Proposition
3.13
with the observation (
73
) yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big[\Upsilon_{z_{1}}\cap A_{z_{2}}^{+}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}(U)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg[\Big\{\Big(\frac{f_{n}(z_{1})}{n^{1/2}},\frac{f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{1})}{n^{3/2}},\frac{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z_{1})}{n^{5/2}}\Big)\in\Xi_{n}\Big\}\cap\bigcap_{j=2}^{m}\big\{|f_{n}(z_{j})|\leq 2n^{-\beta}\log^{2}n\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{j})|\leq n^{5/4}\log^{2}n\big\}\bigg]+\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\cdot n^{-\beta/10}+\exp\big(-c\log^{2}n\big)

and we are done.
∎

6.2
Convergence of Riemann sums

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\{0,\ldots,4\}
we set

a_{j}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}t^{j}e^{2xt}{\rm d}t=\begin{cases}\frac{e^{2x}-1}{2x}&j=0\,,\\
\frac{e^{2x}(2x-1)+1}{4x^{2}}&j=1\,,\\
\frac{e^{2x}(1+2x(x-1))-1}{4x^{3}}&j=2\,,\\
\frac{e^{2x}(4x^{3}-6x^{2}+6x-3)+3}{8x^{4}}&j=3\,,\\
\frac{e^{2x}(2x^{4}-4x^{3}+6x^{2}-6x+3)-3}{4x^{5}}&j=4\,.\end{cases}

(75)

We will further denote by

\Delta_{1}(x)=a_{1}(x)a_{3}(x)-a_{2}(x)^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{2}(x)=a_{2}(x)a_{4}(x)-a_{3}(x)^{2}\,,

(76)

and note that both functions as non-negative on the real line, as they can be written as a determinant of a Gram matrix. Finally, we set

\eta(x)=a_{0}(x)a_{2}(x)a_{4}(x)-a_{0}(x)a_{3}(x)^{2}-a_{1}(x)^{2}a_{4}(x)+2a_{1}(x)a_{2}(x)a_{3}(x)-a_{2}(x)^{3}\,,

(77)

and apply some algebra with (
75
) to write

\eta(x)=\frac{e^{3x}\big(2x^{3}\cosh(x)-x^{2}(3+x^{2})\sinh(x)+\sinh(x)^{3}\big)}{16x^{9}}\,.

Finally, we set

\mathfrak{F}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{a_{4}(x)^{5}}{\eta(x)}\cdot\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\Delta_{2}(x)}\Big(\frac{a_{4}(x)^{2}\Delta_{1}(x)^{2}}{\eta(x)}+a_{4}(x)a_{3}(x)^{2}\Big)\bigg)^{-3}

(78)

and recall that
M_{1}=\lceil 4K/(\delta n)\rceil
,
M_{2}=\lceil 4/\delta\rceil
are the parameters in Definition
5.1
that determine the size of the
\delta
-net
\mathsf{N}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
z\in\Omega_{K}
be such that
\arg(z)\in[n^{-1+4\tau},\pi-n^{-1+4\tau}]
, and let
A_{z}(U)
be given by (
50
). Then, as
n\to\infty
,

\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z}(U)\big]=\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\mathfrak{F}(x)\Big(\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)\big(1+o(1)\big)

where
x=n\log|z|
, and the error term is uniform in
K>0
and
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in a compact set.

The proof of Lemma
6.4
is merely a Gaussian computation, and we postpone it to Section
10
. The next claim lists some basic properties of the limiting intensity function
\mathfrak{F}
.

Claim 6.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}
be given by (
78
). Then
\mathfrak{F}
is a non-negative smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
with

\mathfrak{F}(0)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\times\frac{27}{10^{4}}

(79)

Furthermore, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
so that

\sup_{x\geq 1}\big(e^{x}\,\mathfrak{F}(x)\big)\leq C\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{x<-1}\big(x^{16}\,\mathfrak{F}(x)\big)\leq C\,.

Proof.

We already pointed out that
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
are non-negative functions, while the non-negativity of
a_{0},\ldots,a_{4}
is obvious from the definition (
75
). Hence, to prove that
\mathfrak{F}
is non-negative, we only need to show that
\eta
given by (
77
) is a positive function. Abbreviating the variable
x
for the moment, this comes from the fact that

\eta=\Delta_{2}\bigg(a_{0}-\frac{a_{1}^{2}a_{4}-2a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}+a_{2}^{3}}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}\bigg)=\Delta_{2}\bigg(a_{0}-\frac{1}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{4}&-a_{3}\\
-a_{3}&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\bigg)

(80)

as can be seen from a direct inspection. Hence,
\eta/\Delta_{2}
is the Schur complement of the positive-definite matrix given by

\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&a_{1}&a_{2}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{4}\end{pmatrix}\,,

and hence,
\eta
is also pointwise positive. To find the value at
x=0
, a direct computation shows that

a_{j}(0)=\frac{1}{j+1}\,,\quad\Delta_{1}(0)=\frac{1}{72}\,,\quad\Delta_{2}(0)=\frac{1}{240}\,,\quad\eta(0)=\frac{1}{2160}\,,

and plugging the above into (
78
) and some simplifications gives (
79
). Finally, to get the asymptotic bounds as
|x|\to\infty
, we first note that

\displaystyle a_{j}(x)\sim\frac{e^{2x}}{2x}\,,\qquad\text{for }\ j\in\{0,\ldots,4\}\,

and

\Delta_{1}(x)\sim\frac{e^{4x}}{16x^{4}}\,,\quad\Delta_{2}(x)\sim\frac{e^{4x}}{16x^{4}}\,,\quad\eta(x)\sim\frac{e^{6x}}{128x^{13}}

as
x\to+\infty
, which in turn implies that, as
x\to\infty
,

\log\Delta_{1}(x)\sim 4x\,,\quad\log\Delta_{2}(x)\sim 4x\,,\quad\log\eta(x)\sim 6x\,.

Plugging this asymptotic into (
78
) gives that

\log\mathfrak{F}(x)\sim-2x

as
x\to+\infty
,
and so the right-tail bound follows. As for the left-tail bound, we note from (
75
) that

a_{j}(x)\simeq|x|^{-j-1}\,,\qquad\text{as }\ x\to-\infty\,,

and furthermore

\Delta_{1}(x)\simeq x^{-6}\,,\quad\Delta_{2}(x)\simeq x^{-8}\,,\quad\eta(x)\simeq(-x)^{-9}\,.

Plugging once more into (
78
) and keeping track of the exponents shows that

\mathfrak{F}(x)\simeq x^{-16}\,,\quad\text{as }\ x\to-\infty\,,

which proves the desired bound for the left-tail as well.
∎

In view of the moment computation ahead, we show that summing the success probabilities over the net points give us the limiting intensity.

Claim 6.6
.

We have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}}\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z}(U)\big]=2\,\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\,.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.4
the claim will follow once we show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}}\mathfrak{F}(n\log|z|)=2\,\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\,,

(81)

where
\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)
is given by (
68
).
Recall that the
\delta
-net
\mathsf{N}
is consists of points

z=\Big(1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a}{M_{1}}\Big)e\big(\pi b/M_{2}\big)\,,

with
a\in\{0,\ldots,M_{1}\}
and
b\in\{1,\ldots,M_{2}\}
. Hence, we see that
\log|z|
does not depend on the variable
b
, and we get that

\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}}\mathfrak{F}(n\log|z|)=\frac{K}{M_{1}}\sum_{a=0}^{M_{1}}\mathfrak{F}\Big(n\log\Big|1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a}{M_{1}}\Big|\Big)\,.

(82)

Next, we claim that the sum on the right-hand side of the above display is a Riemann sum. Indeed, the point set

n\log\Big|1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a}{M_{1}}\Big|\,,\quad 0\leq a\leq M_{1}\,,

is a partition of the interval
[-K,K]
, with the spacing between two points given by

n\Big(\frac{2K}{nM_{1}}+o\big(\frac{1}{nM_{1}}\big)\Big)=\frac{2K}{M_{1}}\Big(1+o(1)\Big)

with the error term uniformly bounded for
K>0
in a compact set. As
M_{1}
tends to infinity as
n\to\infty
, we conclude that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{K}{M_{1}}\sum_{a=0}^{M_{1}}\mathfrak{F}\Big(n\log\Big|1-\frac{K}{n}+\frac{2K}{n}\cdot\frac{a}{M_{1}}\Big|\Big)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-K}^{K}\mathfrak{F}(x)\,{\rm d}x=2\,\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\,.

This proves (
81
) and we are done.
∎

6.3
Extracting the Poisson limit: proof of Proposition
5.4

We are finally ready to show the moments on
X_{n}^{\pm}(U)
are Poissonian. For pedagogical reasons, we split the proof into two, showing the desired upper bound and the desired lower bound separately.

Proof of Proposition
5.4
: the upper bound.

To keep notations simple, we will suppress the dependence in
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
until the final calculation. We start with the upper bound, and note that, since
X_{n}^{+}
is a sum of indicators we have

(X_{n}^{+})_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{z_{1},w_{1}\},\ldots\{z_{m},w_{m}\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}\\
\{z_{j},w_{j}\}\not=\{z_{j^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\}\ \forall j\not=j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{+}}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}\,.

(83)

From Lemma
3.3
, we see that

\mathbb{E}\big[(X_{n}^{+})_{m}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\big]\lesssim|\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}|^{2m}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}^{c}\big]\lesssim n^{3m+4\beta m}e^{-c\log^{2}n}=o(1)\,,

so we can assume that
\mathcal{G}
holds throughout the computation. By Claim
5.6
, on the event
A_{z_{j}}^{+}\cap\mathcal{G}
, there is a root of
f_{n}
in
\mathsf{R}_{z_{j}}
. We thus conclude from Lemma
5.8
, that if the events
A_{z_{j}}^{+}\cap A_{w_{j}}^{+}\cap\mathcal{G}
occur for all
1\leq j\leq m
(where
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}
are some fixed tuples as in (
83
)), then we must have
|z_{j}-z_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{1}{n(\log n)^{5}}
for all
j\not=j^{\prime}
. From the above reasoning, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\big[(X_{n}^{+})_{m}\big]\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{z_{1},w_{1}\},\ldots\{z_{m},w_{m}\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}\\
\mathbf{z}\text{ is }(\log n)^{-5}\text{-spread}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{+}}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}+o(1)\\
=2^{-m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}\\
\mathbf{z}\text{ is }(\log n)^{-5}\text{-spread}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{+}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{j}\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
0<|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{+}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}}\bigg]+o(1)\,,

where the above equality follows from summing over ordered pairs in
\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}
instead of unordered pairs as defined in
\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}
. Combining once more Claim
5.6
and Lemma
5.8
, we see that at most one of the
w_{j}
’s in the sum above can occur, and we conclude that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[(X_{n}^{+})_{m}\big]\leq 2^{-m}\,\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}\\
\mathbf{z}\text{ is }(\log n)^{-5}\text{-spread}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{+}\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}\big]\,.

(84)

Denote by

E_{1}=\Big\{\mathbf{z}\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}\,:\,\mathbf{z}\text{ is }n^{\beta}\text{-spread}\Big\}

(85)

and by

E_{2}=\Big\{\mathbf{z}\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}\,:\,\mathbf{z}\text{ is }(\log n)^{-5}\text{-spread and }\exists j\not=j^{\prime}\text{ such that }|z_{j}-z_{j^{\prime}}|\leq n^{\beta-1}\Big\}\,.

Clearly, the sum on the right-hand side of (
84
) breaks into two separate sums over
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively. We first show that the sum over
E_{2}
is negligible. Indeed, we note that
|E_{2}|\lesssim|\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}|^{m}n^{\beta-1}
, since there are
|\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}|n^{\beta-1}
possible choices for the close points with all other kept fixed. By Lemma
6.2
we see that

\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{2}}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{+}\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}\big]\lesssim|E_{2}|\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\log^{O(1)}n\lesssim n^{\beta-1}\log^{O(1)}n

and since
\beta>0
is small, we see that this sum does not contribute to (
84
). On the other hand, we have by Lemma
6.1
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{+}\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}\big]

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z_{j}}\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in|\mathsf{N}|^{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z_{j}}\big]\bigg)=\big(1+o(1)\big)\bigg(\sum_{z\in\mathsf{N}}\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z}(U)\big]\bigg)^{m}\,.

In the above, we used the simple fact that
|E_{1}|\sim|\mathsf{N}|^{m}
. Claim
6.6
now implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{+}\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{+}\big]=\Big(2\,\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)^{m}

(86)

which, in view of (
84
), completes the proof for the upper bound.
∎

We conclude the section with the proof of the lower bound.

Proof of Proposition
5.4
: the lower bound.

As in the proof of the upper bound, our starting point is the formula

(X_{n}^{-})_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{z_{1},w_{1}\},\ldots\{z_{m},w_{m}\}\in\binom{\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}}{2}\\
\{z_{j},w_{j}\}\not=\{z_{j^{\prime}},w_{j^{\prime}}\}\ \forall j\not=j^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{-}}\,\mathbf{1}_{|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\log n/n^{5/4}}\,.

Recall the set of
n^{\beta}
-spread tuples
E_{1}
given by (
85
). We clearly have the lower bound

\displaystyle(X_{n}^{-})_{m}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-m}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in(\mathsf{N}_{\tt s})^{m}\\
0<|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{-}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{j}\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
0<|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}}\Big)\bigg)\bigg]\,.

(87)

By Claim
5.6
and Lemma
5.7
, on the event
A_{z_{j}}^{-}\cap\mathcal{G}
there are two distinct roots
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
of
f_{n}
present in
\mathbb{D}(z_{j},\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}})
, with
\alpha\in\mathsf{R}_{z_{j}}^{\sharp}
and

\Big|\alpha^{\prime}-\Big(z+\frac{f_{n}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}-\frac{2f_{n}^{\prime}(z)}{f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)}\Big)\Big|\leq n^{-5/4-2\beta}\log^{5}n\,.

Since the same it true with
z_{j}
replaced by
w_{j}
, and since we know from Lemma
5.8
there are only two roots, we conclude the inequality

\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{-}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{j}\in\mathsf{N}_{\tt s}\\
0<|z_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\frac{\log n}{n^{5/4}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{w_{j}}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}}\Big)\geq\mathbf{1}_{A_{z_{j}}^{-}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}}-\mathbf{1}_{\Upsilon_{z_{j}}}

where
\Upsilon_{z_{j}}
is given by (
71
). Plugging this observation into (
6.3
), we see that

\mathbb{E}\big[(X_{n}^{-})_{m}\big]\geq 2^{-m}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{-}\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{-}\cap\mathcal{G}\big]-O\bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big[\Upsilon_{z_{1}}\cap A_{z_{2}}^{+}\cap\ldots A_{z_{m}}^{+}\big]\bigg)\,.

(88)

We start by estimating the error terms. Indeed, Lemma
6.3
gives that

\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big[\Upsilon_{z_{1}}\cap A_{z_{2}}^{+}\cap\ldots A_{z_{m}}^{+}\big]\lesssim|E_{1}|\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\cdot n^{-\beta/10}\lesssim n^{-\beta/100}\,.

Furthermore, Lemma
3.3
implies that
|E_{1}|\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{G}^{c}]=o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, and we conclude from (
88
) that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[(X_{n}^{-})_{m}\big]\geq 2^{-m}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in E_{1}}\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}^{-}\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}^{-}\big]=\Big(\mathfrak{c}_{\ast}(K)\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)^{m}

The last equality is derived exactly the same as in the proof of (
86
) in the upper bound part. First, we apply Lemma
6.1
to show that the probabilities asymptotically factor (we can do that since the tuples from
E_{1}
are
n^{\beta}
-spread), and then apply Claim
6.6
to show that the Riemann sums converge to the desired integral. With that we conclude the proof of Proposition
5.4
.
∎

7
Small ball probability bounds for general points

Recall that
d=\min\{n,(1-r)^{-1}\}
is the effective degree of the random polynomial
f_{n}
at the point
z=re^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{D}
. We denote by
V_{n}(z)
the covariance matrix of the random vector

\displaystyle d^{-1/2}\big(

\displaystyle f_{n}(z),d^{-1}zf_{n}^{\prime}(z)\big)

\displaystyle=d^{-1/2}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\xi_{k}r^{k}\cos(k\theta),\sum_{k=0}^{n}\xi_{k}r^{k}\sin(k\theta),\sum_{k=0}^{n}\xi_{k}\tfrac{k}{d}r^{k}\cos(k\theta),\sum_{k=0}^{n}\xi_{k}\tfrac{k}{d}r^{k}\cos(k\theta)\Big)

(89)

As we always identify
\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
V_{n}(z)
is a
4\times 4
real, symmetric, positive-definite matrix given by

V_{n}(z)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=0}^{n}r^{2k}\begin{pmatrix}\cos^{2}(k\theta)&\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)&\tfrac{k}{d}\cos^{2}(k\theta)&\tfrac{k}{d}\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)\\
\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)&\sin^{2}(k\theta)&\tfrac{k}{d}\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)&\tfrac{k}{d}\sin^{2}(k\theta)\\
\tfrac{k}{d}\cos^{2}(k\theta)&\tfrac{k}{d}\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)&\tfrac{k^{2}}{d^{2}}\cos^{2}(k\theta)&\tfrac{k^{2}}{d^{2}}\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)\\
\tfrac{k}{d}\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)&\tfrac{k}{d}\cos^{2}(k\theta)&\tfrac{k^{2}}{d^{2}}\cos(k\theta)\sin(k\theta)&\tfrac{k^{2}}{d^{2}}\sin^{2}(k\theta)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(90)

7.1
Non-singularity of covariance matrix

We start our argument with a simple lemma that shows that if
z
is separated from the real axis then the correlations between the random polynomial and its derivative are non-trivial.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
V=V_{n}(z)
be the covariance matrix (
90
), and denote by
{\lambda}={\lambda}_{n}(z)
its smallest eigenvalue. There exists a constant
c>0
so that for all
d^{-1-\tau}\leq\theta\leq\pi-d^{-1-\tau}
we have

{\lambda}\geq c\min\{1,(d\theta)^{7},(d(\pi-\theta))^{7}\}\,.

Remark 7.2
.

In Section
9
, we will prove a similar non-singularity result for the covariance matrix of tuples of spread points of the form (
32
), see Lemma
9.1
therein. Formally, the case where
|\theta|\geq d^{-1}
and
d=n
in Lemma
7.1
follows from this case. Besides the fact that we need know the non-singularity of the covariance for different degrees
d=d_{n}(z)
, we decided to include a separate proof of Lemma
7.1
for two reasons: (1) we need the qualitative lower bound for
{\lambda}
as
d\theta
becomes small, and (2) it serves as a “warm-up” for the more technical proof of Lemma
9.1
, where a similar (but more complicated) method is applied.

Proof.

Note that the matrix
V
is invariant under the transformation
\theta\mapsto\pi-\theta
, and so we can assume without loss of generality that
\theta\leq\pi/2
. Throughout the proof we will focus on the more difficult case where
\theta\leq d^{-1}
, and will explain briefly in end what to do in the complementary case
\pi/2\geq\theta>d^{-1}
. Let
W=W_{n}(z)
denote the
(n+1)\times 4
matrix with rows

\Big(r^{k}\cos(k\theta),r^{k}\sin(k\theta),\tfrac{k}{d}r^{k}\cos(k\theta),\tfrac{k}{d}r^{k}\cos(k\theta)\Big)\,,\qquad k=0,\ldots,n\,.

Then
V=d^{-1}W^{\sf T}W
and to prove the lemma it will be enough to show

\min_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{3}}\|Wu\|^{2}\geq cd(d\theta)^{7}\,.

(91)

Denote by
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{4})\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and note that for each
k\in[0,n]
we have

\displaystyle(Wu)_{k}

\displaystyle=r^{k}\Big(\cos(k\theta)\big(u_{1}+\tfrac{k}{d}u_{3}\big)+\sin(k\theta)\big(u_{2}+\tfrac{k}{d}u_{4}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{k}}{2}\Big(e(k\theta)\big(u_{1}+\tfrac{k}{d}u_{3}-iu_{2}-i\tfrac{k}{d}u_{4}\big)+e(-k\theta)\big(u_{1}+\tfrac{k}{d}u_{3}+iu_{2}+i\tfrac{k}{d}u_{4}\big)\Big)\,.

We will denote, just for this proof,
x=u_{1}+iu_{2}
and
y=u_{3}+iu_{4}
; the above display then reads

(Wu)_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(z^{k}\big(\overline{x}+\tfrac{k}{d}\overline{y}\big)+\overline{z}^{k}\big(x+\tfrac{k}{d}y)\Big)\,.

(92)

Take some small
{\varepsilon}>0
that we will choose later and set
L=\lfloor{\varepsilon}d\rfloor
. Consider the arithmetic progression
P=\{j\cdot L:j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and denote by
W_{P}
the sub-matrix of
W
with rows in the progression
P
. We will first show that

\min_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{3}}\|W_{P}u\|^{2}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}(d\theta)^{6}.

(93)

For
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
and a sequence
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{C}
we define the (twisted) second order discrete differential by

(D_{\zeta}f)(k)=\sum_{t=0}^{2}\binom{2}{t}(-1)^{t}\zeta^{-tL}f(k+tL).

(94)

Later on, in Section
9
, we will need to consider a similar differential of order three, as we will have to deal with the second derivative of
f_{n}
as well, but for now (
94
) will do. For the sequences

a_{z}(k)=z^{k}\,,\qquad b_{z}(k)=\frac{k}{d}z^{k}\,,

a simple computation shows that

\displaystyle(D_{\zeta}a_{z})(k)=z^{k}\Big(1-(z/\zeta)^{L}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle(D_{\zeta}b_{z})(k)=\frac{z^{k}}{d}\Big(k\big(1-2(z/\zeta)^{L}+(z/\zeta)^{2L}\big)+2L\big((z/\zeta)^{2L}-(z/\zeta)^{L}\big)\Big)\,.

Plugging in
\zeta=z
and denoting by
D
the matrix associated to the linear operator
D_{\zeta}
on
\mathbb{C}^{P}
, we see from (
92
) that

(DW_{P}u)_{k}=\frac{\overline{z}^{k}}{2}\Big(x\big(1-e(-2L\theta)\big)^{2}+y\frac{k\big(1-e(-2L\theta)\big)^{2}+2Le(-2L\theta)\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}{d}\Big)\,.

In particular, for all
k\in P
such that
k+2L\in P
we can take the modulus and sum to get

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in P:k+2L\in P}\|(DW_{P}u)_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\big|1-e(-2L\theta)\big|^{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P:k+2L\in P\\
k\leq d\end{subarray}}\Big|x+y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim(L\theta)^{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P:k+2L\in P\\
k\leq d\end{subarray}}\Big|x+y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|^{2}\,.

(95)

The next claims gives a lower bound for the sum in (
7.1
).

Claim 7.3
.

We have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P:k+2L\in P\\
k\leq d\end{subarray}}\Big|x+y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|^{2}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}1\,.

Proof of Claim
7.3
.

Recalling that
x=u_{1}+iu_{2}
and
y=u_{3}+iu_{4}
are such that
|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}=1
, to further lower bound the right-hand side of (
7.1
) we split into three cases:

\text{(I)}\ |y|\geq 4d\theta;\qquad\text{(II)}\ |y|\leq\tfrac{1}{4}d\theta;\qquad\text{and }\quad\text{(III)}\ \tfrac{1}{4}d\theta<|y|\leq 4d\theta.

First note that the number of summands in (
7.1
) is at least of size
|P\cap[0,d]|\asymp{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. In case (I), we have that

\Big|y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|\geq|y|\frac{1}{d\theta}\geq 4\,,

and since
|x+\tfrac{k}{d}y|\leq 2
we get that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P:k+2L\in P\\
k\leq d\end{subarray}}\Big|x+y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|^{2}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}1\,.

(96)

In case (II), we have
|x|\geq\tfrac{3}{4}
and since

\Big|y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|\leq\frac{d\theta}{4}\big(1+1.5(d\theta)^{-1}\big)<\frac{3}{4}

we get that (
96
) holds in this case as well. It remains to deal with case (III). Denote by

{\sf p}_{k}=\Big|x+y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|\,,

and note that
\max\{{\sf p}_{0},{\sf p}_{d}\}\geq|y|/2\geq d\theta/4
. Furthermore, all consecutive elements from
\{{\sf p}_{k}\}_{k\in P}
have

\big|{\sf p}_{k+L}-{\sf p}_{k}\big|\leq|y|\frac{L}{d}\leq 4{\varepsilon}\,d\theta

where
{\varepsilon}>0
is a sufficiently small constant. Therefore, at least
10^{-2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}
elements
k\in P
must have
{\sf p}_{k}\gtrsim d\theta
, and we conclude that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P:k+2L\in P\\
k\leq d\end{subarray}}\Big|x+y\frac{k}{d}-y\frac{2Le(-2L\theta)}{d\big(e(-2L\theta)-1\big)}\Big|^{2}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}(d\theta)^{2}\,.

∎

Combining Claim
7.3
with (
7.1
) we arrive at

\sum_{k\in P:k+2L\in P}\|(DW_{P}u)_{k}\|^{2}\gtrsim_{{\varepsilon}}(d\theta)^{6}\,.

Since the operator norm of
D:\ell_{2}(P)\to\ell_{2}(P)
is
O(1)
, the display above shows that uniformly in
u\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
we have

\sum_{k\in P:k+2L\in P}\|(W_{P}u)_{k}\|^{2}\gtrsim_{{\varepsilon}}(d\theta)^{6}\,,

which gives (
93
). It remains to prove (
91
). Consider the sub-matrices
W_{P},W_{1+P},\ldots,W_{d_{0}+P}
composed of rows from the shifted progressions
P,1+P,\ldots,d_{0}+P
, respectively. If
d_{0}\leq L
then these sub-matrices are all disjoint. Moreover, denoting by

F=\begin{pmatrix}r^{k}H&0\\
0&r^{k}H\end{pmatrix}\,\quad\text{with}\quad H=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\,,

we see that
W_{k+P}
and
W_{P}H^{k}
differ only by a matrix of norm
O(d_{0}/d)
. Indeed, the matrix
H
corresponds to the shift in the phase
(\cos k\theta,\sin k\theta)\mapsto(\cos(k+1)\theta,\sin(k+1)\theta)
, so the matrices
W_{k+P}
and
W_{P}F^{k}
differ only in the
k/d
terms in the last two variables. Since the matrix
H
is unitary, we have for
k\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
that

\sigma_{4}(W_{P}F^{k})\gtrsim\sigma_{4}(W_{P})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:lower_bound_on_singular_value_of_W_pointwise_case_on_progression}}}{{\gtrsim}}(d\theta)^{6}\,.

By choosing
d_{0}=\lfloor{\varepsilon}d^{2}\theta\rfloor\leq L
with
{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small we have by the triangle inequality that
\sigma_{4}(W_{k+P})\gtrsim(d\theta)^{6}
for all
1\leq k\leq d_{0}
. As those arithmetic progressions are disjoint, we can sum up over all of them and obtain

\|Wu\|^{2}\geq\sum_{k=0}^{d_{0}}\|W_{k+P}u\|^{2}\gtrsim d_{0}(d\theta)^{6}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}d(d\theta)^{7}\,.

which yields (
91
) and concludes the lemma in the case where
d\theta\leq 1
. To deal with the complementary case
\theta>d^{-1}
, one can repeat the argument above with
L=\lfloor\tfrac{1}{100}d\rfloor
(say) and note that the corresponding lower bound that we get from (
7.1
) now reads

\sum_{k\in P:k+2L\in P}\|(DW_{P}u)_{k}\|^{2}\gtrsim 1\,.

Summing this over the
\Omega(d)
-disjoint arithmetic progressions (as we did before) yields the desired lower bound also for this case, and we are done.
∎

7.2
Comparison of characteristic function to a Gaussian

To handle the small-ball bounds we will apply the ‘exponentially-tilted’ Esseen inequality (Proposition
3.4
) and compare the characteristic function of (
7
) with the characteristic function of the limiting Gaussian random vector. The desired comparison is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
{\bf X}_{1},\ldots,{\bf X}_{n}
be independent random vectors taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, having distributions
G_{1},\ldots,G_{n}
respectively. Assume that the
{\bf X}_{j}
’s have mean zero and a uniformly bounded third moment, that is

\max_{1\leq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}\big[|{\bf X}_{j}|^{3}\big]\leq M\,.

(97)

Assume further that the average covariance matrix
V
is non-singular, and denote by
{\lambda}
its smallest eigenvalue. Let
B
be the symmetric positive-definite matrix so that
V^{-1}=B^{2}
. Then there exists constants
C,c>0
depending only on
M
so that

\sup_{|t|\leq cn^{1/2}{\lambda}^{3/2}}\bigg|\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{G}_{j}\Big(\frac{Bt}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)-\exp\Big(-\frac{|t|^{2}}{2}\Big)\bigg|\leq Cn^{-1/2}{\lambda}^{-3/2}|t|^{3}\exp(-c|t|^{2})\,,

where
\widehat{G}_{j}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}[\exp(i\langle\xi,{\bf X}_{j}\rangle)]
is the characteristic functions of the random vector
{\bf X}_{j}
.

Proof.

By (
97
), we know that for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

\Big|\widehat{G}_{j}(\xi)-1+\frac{1}{2}\langle\xi,V_{j}\xi\rangle\Big|\leq\frac{1}{6}\,M|\xi|^{3}

where
V_{j}
is the covariance matrix of
{\bf X}_{j}
, see for instance
[
BR10
, Corollary 8.2]
. Hence, for
\xi
small enough, we have that

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{G}_{j}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log\Big(1+\big(\widehat{G}_{j}(\xi)-1\big)\Big)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle\xi,V_{j}\xi\rangle+O\big(nM|\xi|^{3}\big)\Bigg\}=\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{n}{2}\langle\xi,V\xi\rangle+O\big(nM|\xi|^{3}\big)\Bigg\}\,,

where
\log
is some branch of the complex logarithm defined in the vicinity of
1
. Plugging
\xi=Bt/\sqrt{n}
and using the identity
B^{2}=V^{-1}
we get that

\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{G}_{j}\Big(\frac{Bt}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)=\exp\bigg\{-|t|^{2}/2+O\big(n^{-1/2}{\lambda}^{-3/2}M|t|^{3}\big)\bigg\}\,.

It remains to apply the elementary inequality
|e^{x}-1|\leq|x|e^{x}
to get that

\displaystyle\bigg|\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{G}_{j}\Big(\frac{Bt}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)-\exp\Big(-\frac{|t|^{2}}{2}\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\frac{|t|^{2}}{2}\Big)\bigg|\exp\Big(O(n^{-1/2}{\lambda}^{-3/2}M|t|^{3})\Big)-1\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-1/2}{\lambda}^{-3/2}|t|^{3}\exp\Big(-\frac{|t|^{2}}{2}+O(n^{-1/2}{\lambda}^{-3/2}M|t|^{3})\Big)\,,

and since
|t|\leq c\sqrt{n}{\lambda}^{3/2}
for some small
c>0
the lemma follows.
∎

7.3
Proving the “worst-case” small-ball bounds

We are now ready to prove the small-ball bounds stated in Section
3.2
.

Proof of Claim
3.5
.

We may assume by a simple covering argument that
a=b=\delta\geq(\log d)/d^{1/2}
. By Proposition
3.4
we have that

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq ad^{1/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-\beta|\leq bd^{3/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq td^{5/2}\Big]\\
\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}}(ab)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,.

(98)

Therefore, the claim will follow once we prove uniform boundedness of the integral in (
98
). Indeed, we will split the integral into two domains
{\rm I}=\{|\xi|\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}d^{1/2}\}
and
{\rm II}=\{|\xi|>{\varepsilon}_{0}d^{1/2}\}
for some sufficiently small
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
(depending only on the distribution of
\xi_{0}
). Since
\varphi_{\eta_{0}}(\cdot)\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\rm II}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}/2}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\leq\int_{{\rm II}}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)=O(d^{O(1)}e^{-c(\log d)^{2}})=o(1)\,.

To deal with the integral over
{\rm I}
, we apply Lemma
7.4
with the bounded random vectors

{\bf X}_{k}=\eta_{k}\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\,.

In other words, we deal with the “symmetrized” version of our random polynomial
g_{n}
given by (
26
), instead of the original
f_{n}
. We get that

\int_{{\rm I}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\\
