\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}e^{-c|\xi|^{2}}{\rm d}m(\xi)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}|\xi|^{3}e^{-c|\xi|^{2}}{\rm d}m(\xi)=O(1)\,.

(99)

We note that the uniform non-singularity of
V=V_{n}(z)
in this range is guaranteed by Lemma
7.1
, which is the reason we can use the Gaussian comparison for the characteristic function here all the way to
{\varepsilon}_{0}d^{1/2}
for some
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
small. Combining both estimates gives a uniform upper bound on the integral in (
98
), and the claim follows.
∎

Proof of Claim
3.6
.

The proof here is similar to the proof of Claim
3.5
, only that here we cannot get the Gaussian comparison for the characteristic function all the way up to
c\sqrt{d}
because of the quantitative singularity of
V_{n}(z)
in this range. Still, as this non-singularity is controlled via Lemma
7.1
, we can get some bound. Arguing similarly as in the proof of Claim
3.5
, from the inequality (
98
) we split the integral into two domains:

{\rm I}^{\prime}=\{|\xi|\leq d^{1/2-2\tau}\}\qquad{\rm II}^{\prime}=\{|\xi|>d^{1/2-2\tau}\}\,.

For the domain
{\rm II}^{\prime\prime}
we have the bound

\int_{{\rm II}^{\prime}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}/2}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\leq\int_{{\rm II}^{\prime}}e^{-\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}/2}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)=O(d^{O(1)}e^{-d^{2\tau}})=o(1)\,.

To deal with
{\rm I}^{\prime}
, we note that Lemma
7.1
implies that
{\lambda}\geq d^{-\tau}
for
z
in our range, and therefore Lemma
7.4
gives that

\prod_{k=0}^{d}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\lesssim\exp\Big(-\langle\xi,V\xi\rangle\Big)+\frac{d^{2\tau}}{\sqrt{d}}|\xi|^{3/2}\exp\Big(-c\langle\xi,V\xi\rangle\Big)

for
\xi\in{\rm I}^{\prime}
. This yields that

\int_{{\rm II}^{\prime}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{n}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det V}}\leq{\lambda}^{-2}\leq d^{2\tau}

and we get what we wanted.
∎

8
Small-ball probability bounds for smooth points

Recall that a point in
z=re^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}
is called
A
-smooth if

\|p\theta/\pi\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\geq A/d_{n}(z)\qquad\forall p\in[1,A+1]\cap\mathbb{Z}\,,

where as before
d=d_{n}(z)=\min\{n,(1-r)^{-1}\}
. In this section we shall prove Lemma
3.9
, which states that for a
d^{7\tau}
-smooth point
z
, the optimal small-ball probability bound for the random vector
d^{-1/2}\big(f_{n}(z),d^{-1}f_{n}^{\prime}(z)\big)
is true up to the scale
d^{-3/2+o(1)}
. Our treatment here is largely inspired by
[
KS99
, Section 4]
, but we have adapted it to also cover the case
r<1
.

8.1
Moving to the Fourier side

The starting point for the proof is the “exponentially tilted” Esseen inequality (Proposition
3.4
), which we use exactly as we used in (
98
). To recall, it gives the bound

\max_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}\Big[|f_{n}(z)-\alpha|\leq ad^{1/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime}(z)-\beta|\leq bd^{3/2}\,,\,|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\geq td^{5/2}\Big]\\
\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}}(ab)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{d}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)

where now
\delta\geq d^{-3/2+11\tau}
. Our goal now is to show that the integral on the right-hand side of the above display is uniformly bounded for
d
large. To this end, we fix some small constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
(depending only on the distribution of
\xi_{0}
) and split the integral into three different regimes

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{|\xi|\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}\sqrt{d}\}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{d}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{{\varepsilon}_{0}\sqrt{d}\leq|\xi|\leq d^{3/2-10\tau}\}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{d}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,,

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{|\xi|\geq d^{3/2-10\tau}\}}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{d}\varphi_{\eta_{0}}\Big(d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle\Big)\Bigg)^{1/4}e^{-c\delta^{2}|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,.

We can handle the integral
I_{1}
in the exact same way as we did in the proof of Claim
3.5
(specifically, we use the bound (
99
)), where we note that any smooth point
z
must lie at distance at least
d^{-1}
away from the real axis. Handling
I_{3}
is also easy, and follows simply from our lower bound on
\delta
as

I_{3}\leq\int_{|\xi|\geq d^{3/2-10\tau}}e^{-c|\xi|^{2}\delta^{2}}\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim d^{O(1)}\exp\Big(-cd^{2\tau}\Big)=o(1)\,.

(100)

Therefore, to prove Lemma
3.9
, it remains to deal with the integral
I_{2}
, which is the main technical part of this section. For
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, we denote by

\psi_{k}(u)=\text{Re}\Big(z^{k}\big(\overline{u}_{1}+\tfrac{k}{d}\overline{u}_{2}\big)\Big)\,,

(101)

and note that

d^{-1/2}\Big\langle\xi,\begin{pmatrix}z^{k}\\
\tfrac{k}{d}z^{k}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle=\psi_{k}(u)

with
u=\xi/\sqrt{d}
. The following lemma is proved in Section
8.2
.

Lemma 8.1
.

For all
{\varepsilon}_{0},c_{1},c_{2}>0
and for any
z\in\mathbb{D}\cap\mathbb{H}
which is
d^{7\tau}
-smooth the following holds. For any
u\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
with
{\varepsilon}_{0}\leq|u|\leq d^{1-10\tau}
, we have that

\sum_{k=0}^{d}\|s\psi_{k}(u)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\geq d^{\tau}\,,

for all
s\in[c_{1},c_{2}]
and for all
d\geq d_{0}({\varepsilon}_{0},c_{1},c_{2})
large enough.

Assuming Lemma
8.1
for the moment, we can conclude the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

In view of the discussion above, to conclude the lemma we only need to show that the integral
I_{2}
is uniformly bounded for large
d
. Indeed, as
\eta_{0}
is a mean-zero non-degenerate symmetric random variable, there exists constant
c_{1},c_{2}>0
so that

\varphi_{\eta_{0}}(t)=\mathbb{E}\big[\cos(t\eta_{0})\big]\leq\exp\big(-c\inf_{c_{1}\leq s\leq c_{2}}\|st\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\big)\,,\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(102)

See for instance
[
CN21
, Eq. (9.2)]
. Plugging this into the definition of
I_{2}
, we see that the proof will follow once we show that

\int_{\{{\varepsilon}_{0}\sqrt{d}\leq|\xi|\leq d^{3/2-10\tau}\}}\exp\left(-c\inf_{c_{1}\leq s\leq c_{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{d}\|s\psi_{k}(\xi/\sqrt{d})\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}m(\xi)=O(1)\,.

(103)

As we assume
z
is
d^{7\tau}
-smooth, we conclude from Lemma
8.1
that the integral in (
103
) is bounded from above by

\int_{\{{\varepsilon}_{0}\sqrt{d}\leq|\xi|\leq d^{3/2-10\tau}\}}\exp\Big(-cd^{\tau}\Big)\lesssim d^{O(1)}e^{-cd^{\tau}}=o(1)

which in particular proves (
103
) and with that the lemma.
∎

8.2
Finite differences

As
u
will remain fixed throughout this section, we will sometimes write
\psi_{k}=s\psi_{k}(u)
to lighten the notation. Roughly speaking, to prove Lemma
8.1
we will show that if the sequence
\{\psi_{k}\}
is close to integers, then in fact many of these integers must be equal to 0. This observation, originating in
[
KS99
]
,
relies heavily on the assumption that
z=re^{i\theta}
is smooth, as the desired conclusion is false if
\theta
lies close to rationals with small denominators. Recall that by Dirichlet’s approximation theorem
[
Cas57
, Chapter 1]
, there exists
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that

p\theta/2\pi-t\in\mathbb{Z}\,,\qquad 1\leq p\leq\lfloor d^{\tau}\rfloor\,,\ |t|\leq d^{-\tau}\,.

(104)

Since
z
is assumed to be
d^{7\tau}
-smooth (see Definition
3.8
), this implies that
|t|\geq d^{-1+7\tau}
. We break down the proof of Lemma
8.1
into three claims.

Claim 8.2
.

Assume there exists an interval
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,d]
with
|\mathcal{J}|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}d^{1-5\tau}
such that

\sup_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\|\psi_{k}\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq d^{-\tau}\,.

Then for any arithmetic progression
\mathcal{P}=\{k_{0}+pj\}_{j\geq 0}
with
k_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
and
p\geq 1
given by (
104
) such that

\#\big(\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{J}\big)\geq cd^{1-5\tau},

there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

|\psi_{s}-m|=\|\psi_{s}\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\qquad\text{for all }s\in\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{J}\,.

Proof.

For a sequence
g:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{C}
and an integer
h\geq 0
we set

\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}g\big)(\ell)=\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}g(\ell+sp)\,,

(105)

the discrete differential of order
h
, with step size
p
. We denote by
m_{k}
the closest integer to
\psi_{k}
, then by our assumption

\sup_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|m_{k}-\psi_{k}|\leq d^{-\tau}\,.

(106)

We would like to bound the discrete differential along the arithmetic progression
\mathcal{P}=\{k_{0}+jp\}_{j\geq 0}
. Recall that by writing
u=(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{C}
we have

\psi_{k}=\text{Re}\Big(z^{k}\big(\overline{u}_{1}+\frac{k}{d}\overline{u}_{2}\big)\Big)\,.

(107)

Thus, for all
j\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}\psi\big)(k_{0}+jp)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}\psi_{k_{0}+jp+sp}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\text{Re}\,\left(z^{k_{0}+jp}\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}z^{sp}\left(\overline{u_{1}}+\frac{k_{0}+jp+sp}{d}\overline{u}_{2}\right)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}z^{sp}\Big(\overline{u_{1}}+\frac{k_{0}+jp+sp}{d}\overline{u}_{2}\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|u_{1}|\Big|\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}z^{sp}\Big|+|u_{2}|\Big|\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}z^{sp}\frac{k_{0}+jp+sp}{d}\Big|\,.

Since

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}z^{sp}=(1-z^{p})^{h}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{h}\binom{h}{s}(-1)^{s}z^{sp}sp=z^{-1}\partial_{z}\Big(z(1-z^{p})^{h}\Big)=z^{-1}(1-z^{p})^{h}-hp(1-z^{p})^{h-1}z^{p-1}\,,

we get that

|\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}\psi\big)(k_{0}+jp)|\lesssim|u|hp|1-z^{p}|^{h}\,.

On the other hand, by (
104
) we see that

|1-z^{p}|\leq 1-r^{p}+r^{p}|1-e(2\pi t)|\lesssim|t|+d^{-1+\tau}\lesssim d^{-\tau}

and since
|u|\leq d^{1-10\tau}
, we arrive at

|\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}\psi\big)(k_{0}+jp)|\lesssim h2^{h}d^{1-9\tau-h\tau}\,.

(108)

On the other hand, (
106
) implies the trivial bound

\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}(\psi-m)\big)(k_{0}+jp)\leq 2^{h}d^{-\tau}

for all those
j\geq 0
for which we have
[k_{0}+jp,k_{0}+jp+hp]\subset\mathcal{J}
. Combining with (
108
), we get that

|\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}m\big)(k_{0}+jp)|\leq 2^{h}d^{-\tau}+Ch2^{h}d^{1-9\tau-h\tau}\,.

We can now choose
h=\lfloor\tau(\log d)/2\rfloor
and
d\geq d_{0}
sufficiently large so that the right-hand side of the above is less that
\frac{1}{2}
, which implies that for all admissible
j\geq 0

\big(\Delta_{p}^{h}m\big)(k_{0}+jp)=0\,.

That is, there exist a polynomial
Q
with degree
\leq h-1
such that

Q(j)=m_{k_{0}+jp}\,.

(109)

We will be done once we show that
Q
is the constant polynomial. Suppose it is not, then
Q^{\prime}
has at most
h-2
zeros, and hence
Q
must be strictly monotone on an interval of length
|\mathcal{J}|/(ph)\geq cd^{1-5\tau-o(1)}
. For all
k\in[0,d]
we have

|m_{k}|\leq|\psi_{k}|+1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:psi_k_in_complex_coordinates}}}{{\leq}}|u|+1\leq d^{1-10\tau}+1

and hence we get a contradiction to strict monotonicity of
Q
. That is,
Q
is a constant polynomial and in view of (
109
) we are done.
∎

Claim 8.3
.

Assume that for some
k_{0}\geq 0
we have
|\psi_{k_{0}+jp}(u)|\geq c|u|
for all
0\leq j\leq cd^{1-5\tau}
. Then there exist
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,cd^{1-5\tau}]
such that

\psi_{k_{0}+jp}\cdot\psi_{k_{0}+j^{\prime}p}<0\,.

Proof.

By scaling, we may assume without loss of generality that
|u|=1
. By (
107
), we have that

\psi_{k_{0}+jp}(u)=\text{Re}\big(z^{k_{0}+jp}(\overline{u}_{1}+\tfrac{k_{0}+jp}{d}\overline{u}_{2})\big)=\text{Re}\big(z^{jp}(z^{k_{0}}\overline{u}_{1}+z^{k_{0}}\tfrac{k_{0}}{d}\overline{u}_{2})\big)+O(d^{-5\tau})\,.

Hence, assuming by contradiction there are no sign changes of
\{\psi_{k_{0}+jp}\}
, we get that for some
c>0
and for all
0\leq j\leq cd^{1-5\tau}

\text{Re}\big(z^{jp}(z^{k_{0}}\overline{u}_{1}+z^{k_{0}}\tfrac{k_{0}}{d}\overline{u}_{2})\big)>c\,.

(110)

By (
104
) we have
z^{jp}=r^{jp}e(2\pi jt)
, and because
z
is smooth
|t|\geq d^{-1+6\tau}
. Therefore, the sequence
\{z^{jp}\}_{0\leq j\leq cd^{1-5\tau}}
visits all four quadrants in the plane, and we get a contradiction to (
110
).
∎

Claim 8.4
.

Suppose that
\#\big\{k\in[0,d]\cap\mathbb{Z}\,:\,\|\psi_{k}\|\geq d^{-\tau}\big\}\leq d^{3\tau}.
Then there exists an interval
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,d]
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\geq cd^{1-5\tau}|u|^{2}\,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sup_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|\psi_{k}|\leq d^{-\tau}\,.

Proof.

We decompose the interval
[0,d]
into
\lfloor d^{5\tau}\rfloor
non-overlapping intervals of lengths in
[\tfrac{1}{2}d^{1-5\tau},d^{1-5\tau}]
. Denote by
J_{\nu}
the
\nu
’th interval,
\nu\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor d^{5\tau}\rfloor\}
and by

s_{\nu}=\sum_{k\in J_{\nu}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\,.

By Lemma
7.1
we know that

\sum_{\nu=1}^{\lfloor d^{4\tau}\rfloor}s_{\nu}\geq 10^{-4}\,|u|^{2}d\,\,.

We conclude there exists at least
d^{4\tau}
intervals so that
s_{\nu}\geq 10^{-5}|u^{2}|d^{1-5\tau}
, as otherwise we would have

\sum_{\nu=1}^{\lfloor d^{4\tau}\rfloor}s_{\nu}\leq d^{4\tau}d^{1-5\tau}|u|^{2}+10^{-5}|u|^{2}d^{1-5\tau}(d^{5\tau}-d^{4\tau})<10^{-4}|u|^{2}d

which is a contradiction. By the pigeonhole principle, there exists an interval
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,d]
of length at least
\tfrac{1}{2}d^{1-5\tau}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\geq 10^{-5}d^{1-5\tau}|u|^{2}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sup_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\|\psi_{k}\|\leq d^{-\tau}\,.

(111)

We want to show that
\mathcal{J}
is the desired interval, i.e. that
\|\psi_{k}\|=|\psi_{k}|
for
k\in\mathcal{J}
. To show this, cover
\mathcal{J}
with
p
arithmetic progressions
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{p}
of step size
p\geq 1
given by (
104
). Each such arithmetic progression is of length
\geq cd^{1-5\tau}
, and hence Claim
8.2
implies there exist integers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}
so that

\sup_{k\in\mathcal{P}_{j}}|\psi_{k}-m_{j}|\leq d^{-\tau}\,,\qquad 1\leq j\leq p.

We claim that
m_{j}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq p
. Assume by contradiction this is not the case, and denote by
j^{\prime}
the index for which
|m_{j^{\prime}}|\geq 1
is maximal. By (
111
) we have

d^{1-5\tau}|m_{j^{\prime}}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{P}_{j^{\prime}}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{P}_{j}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}10^{-5}d^{1-5\tau}|u|^{2}

and hence
|m_{j^{\prime}}|\geq c|u|^{2}
. In particular, this implies that
|\psi_{k}|\geq c|u|
for all
k\in\mathcal{P}_{j^{\prime}}
, and hence by Claim
8.3

\{\psi_{k}\}
must change signs somewhere along the arithmetic progression
\mathcal{P}_{j^{\prime}}
. This is a contradiction to the fact that
|m_{j^{\prime}}|\geq 1
, and hence
m_{1}=\ldots=m_{p}=0
, as desired.
∎

We are ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

The proof splits into two cases. In case
\#\big\{k\in[0,d]\cap\mathbb{Z}\,:\|\psi_{k}\|\geq d^{-\tau}\big\}>d^{3\tau}
, we clearly have

\sum_{k=0}^{d}\|\psi_{k}\|^{2}\geq d^{-2\tau}d^{3\tau}=d^{\tau}.

The other case is when
\#\big\{k\in[0,d]\cap\mathbb{Z}\,:\|\psi_{k}\|\geq d^{-\tau}\big\}\leq d^{3\tau}
, which by Claim
8.4
implies there exists an interval
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,d]
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\geq cd^{1-5\tau}|u|^{2}\geq cd^{1-5\tau}\,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sup_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|\psi_{k}|\leq d^{-\tau}\,.

As
\tau>0
is sufficiently small, we get that

\sum_{k=0}^{d}\|\psi_{k}\|^{2}\geq\sum_{k\in\mathcal{J}}|\psi_{k}|^{2}\geq cd^{1-5\tau}\geq d^{\tau}

for all
d
large enough, and we are done.
∎

9
Gaussian comparison for smooth separated tuples

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
3.12
, that is, to show that for smooth, separated tuples of points in
\Omega_{K}
the probabilities of small-ball events at scale
\gtrsim n^{-M}
(where
M>0
is arbitrary but fixed), are the same as in the case of Gaussian coefficients. Our derivation here is largely inspired by
[
CN21
]
, which proved a similar result (see in particular
[
CN21
, Theorem 3.2]
) for net points exactly on the unit circle (which corresponds to taking
K=0
, instead of large but fixed constant) and considering the polynomial and its first derivative, whereas in our derivation we need also to consider the second derivative. However, these extensions are rather of technical nature, and the core of the proof remains the same.

Recall that for a fixed
m\geq 1
and
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
we consider the random vector
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6m}
as given by (
32
). First, we derive a convenient “random walk” representation of this random vector. For a tuple
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
and
0\leq k\leq n
we set

\mathbf{z}^{k}=\big(z_{1}^{k},\ldots,z_{m}^{k}\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{m}\,,

and

w_{k}=w_{k}(\mathbf{z})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(\mathbf{z}^{k},\frac{k}{n}\mathbf{z}^{k},\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\mathbf{z}^{k}\Big)\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}.

(112)

Let
W_{n}=W_{n}(\mathbf{z})
be a
(n+1)\times(3m)
matrix with rows
w_{k}
for
0\leq k\leq n
. We have

S_{n}(\mathbf{z})=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\xi_{k}w_{k}(\mathbf{z})=W_{n}^{{\sf T}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}

(113)

where
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{n})
is the vector of random coefficients. We note that the covariance matrix of
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
is given by
W_{n}^{{\sf T}}W_{n}
, as shown clearly from the representation (
113
).

9.1
Non-singularity of covariance matrix for tuples

As a first step towards the proof of Theorem
3.12
we need to prove that
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6m}
is genuinely
6m
-dimensional. This amounts to showing that the minimal eigenvalue of the covariance matrix
W_{n}^{{\sf T}}W_{n}
is uniformly bounded away from zero, under the necessary assumption that the tuple
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
is spread, in the sense of Definition
3.10
.

Lemma 9.1
.

For any
m\geq 1
,
\kappa>0
and for any tuple
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
of
n^{\kappa}
-smooth and
\gamma
-spread points we have

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}\\[0.81949pt]
\|u_{1}\|^{2}+\|u_{2}\|^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\|W_{n}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}+\overline{W_{n}(\mathbf{z})}u_{2}\|^{2}\gtrsim_{m}\min\{\gamma,1\}^{8m-4}

For the proof of Lemma
9.1
, it will be slightly more convenient to work on a scale on which the points
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
are of order one separation. The next claim shows that such a rescaling is possible.

Claim 9.2
.

Let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\in\Omega_{K}^{n}
be
\gamma
-spread for some
\gamma>0
, and let
\{a_{n}\}
be any positive sequence of integers with
a_{n}=o(n)
as
n\to\infty
. Then for all
n
large there exists an integer
L\asymp n/a_{n}
so that

\qquad\qquad|z_{j}^{L}-z_{j^{\prime}}^{L}|\gtrsim_{m}\gamma/a_{n}\,,\qquad\forall 1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m\,.

Proof.

To prove this claim, it will be more convenient to parameterize
z_{j}=e(w_{j}/n)
, where
w_{j}=x_{j}+iy_{j}
and
x_{j}\in[0,n\pi]
and
y_{j}\in[-K,K]
. Our assumption that
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
is
\gamma
-spread implies that for each
j<j^{\prime}
we have

|x_{j}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\gamma/2\qquad\text{or }\qquad|y_{j}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\gamma/2\,.

We call a pair
(j,j^{\prime})\in[m]^{2}

good
if
|x_{j}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\gamma/2
, and otherwise we say that the pair
(j,j^{\prime})
is
bad
. By
[
CN21
, Lemma 2.8]
there exists
L\asymp n/a_{n}
so that

\Big|\frac{L(x_{j}-x_{j^{\prime}})}{n}\Big|\gtrsim_{m}\gamma/a_{n}

for all good pairs
(j,j^{\prime})
. In particular, for these pairs we have

\displaystyle|z_{j}^{L}-z_{j^{\prime}}^{L}|

\displaystyle=|e(w_{j}L/n)-e(w_{j^{\prime}}L/n)|

\displaystyle\geq\min\big\{e^{-y_{j}L/n},e^{-y_{j^{\prime}}L/n}\big\}\,\big|e(x_{j}L/n)-e(x_{j^{\prime}}L/n)\big|\geq e^{-K/a_{n}}\Big|\frac{L(x_{j}-x_{j^{\prime}})}{n}\Big|\gtrsim_{m}\gamma/a_{n}\,.

To conclude the claim, we note that for all bad pairs
(j,j^{\prime})
we must have
|y_{j}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\gamma/2
, which in turn implies the lower bound

|z_{j}^{L}-z_{j^{\prime}}^{L}|\geq\big||z_{j}|^{L}-|z_{j^{\prime}}|^{L}\big|\geq L\frac{|y_{j}-y_{j^{\prime}}|}{n}\gtrsim\gamma/a_{n}

and we are done.
∎

Proof of Lemma
9.1
.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Fix
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
and write
u_{j}=(u_{j,1},\ldots,u_{j,3m})
for
j=1,2
. The
k
’th entry of
W_{n}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}
is given by

\big(W_{n}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}\big)_{k}=\langle w_{k}(\mathbf{z}),u_{1}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}z_{j}^{k}\Big(\overline{u}_{1,j}+\frac{k}{n}\overline{u}_{1,j+m}+\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\overline{u}_{1,j+2m}\Big)\,.

Therefore, we are after the lower bound

\|W_{n}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}+\overline{W_{n}(\mathbf{z})}u_{2}\|^{2}\\
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{m}z_{j}^{k}\Big(\overline{u}_{1,j}+\frac{k}{n}\overline{u}_{1,j+m}+\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\overline{u}_{1,j+2m}\Big)+\overline{z}_{j}^{k}\Big(\overline{u}_{2,j}+\frac{k}{n}\overline{u}_{2,j+m}+\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\overline{u}_{2,j+2m}\Big)\bigg|^{2}\\
\gtrsim\gamma^{8m-4}\,,

(114)

uniformly over all possible choices of
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
which satisfy
|u_{1}|^{2}+|u_{2}|^{2}=1
.
By Claim
9.2
(applied to the
\gamma
-spread tuple
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m},+1,-1)
), there exists
L=c(m)n
with
c(m)>0
being a small constant so that

\qquad\qquad|z_{j}^{L}-z_{j^{\prime}}^{L}|\gtrsim_{m}\gamma\,,\qquad\forall 1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m\,,

(115)

and

\qquad\qquad\min\{|z_{j}^{L}+1|,|z_{j}^{L}-1|\}\gtrsim_{m}\gamma\,,\qquad\forall 1\leq j\leq m\,.

We denote by
P
the intersection of
[0,n]
with the arithmetic progression
\{jL\,:\,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, and note that
|P|\asymp_{m}1
. With a slight abuse of notation, let
W_{P}
be the sub-matrix of
W_{n}
with rows indexed by
P
. Towards proving (
114
), we first show that for all admissible choices
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
we have

\|W_{P}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}+\overline{W_{P}(\mathbf{z})}u_{2}\|^{2}\gtrsim_{m}\frac{\gamma^{6m-3}}{n}\,.

(116)

The method of proof is similar to that of the proof of Lemma
7.1
. Indeed, for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
we introduce the third-order twisted discrete differential operator

(D_{\zeta}f)(k)=\sum_{t=0}^{3}\binom{3}{t}(-1)^{t}\zeta^{-tL}f(k+tL)\,,

(117)

(not to be confused with the second-order difference given by (
94
)) acting on sequences
f:P\to\mathbb{C}
. For the sequences

a_{z}(k)=z^{k}\,,\qquad b_{z}(k)=\frac{k}{n}z^{k}\,,\qquad c_{z}(k)=\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}z^{k}\,,

a simple computation shows that

\displaystyle(D_{\zeta}a_{z})(k)

\displaystyle=\big(1-(z/\zeta)^{L}\big)^{3}a_{z}(k)\,,

\displaystyle(D_{\zeta}b_{z})(k)

\displaystyle=\big(1-(z/\zeta)^{L}\big)^{3}\Big[b_{z}(k)+a_{z}(k)\alpha_{L}(z/\zeta)\Big]\,,

(118)

\displaystyle(D_{\zeta}c_{z})(k)

\displaystyle=\big(1-(z/\zeta)^{L}\big)^{3}\Big[c_{z}(k)+2b_{z}(k)\alpha_{L}(z/\zeta)+a_{z}(k)\Big(\beta_{L}(z/\zeta)-\frac{\alpha_{L}(z/\zeta)}{n}\Big)\Big]\,,

where

\alpha_{L}(s)=-3\frac{L}{n}\frac{s^{L}}{1-s^{L}}\qquad\text{and }\qquad\beta_{L}(s)=3s^{L}\frac{L^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{3s^{L}-1}{(1-s^{L})^{2}}\,.

From (
9.1
), we see that for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and for all
k\in[0,n]\cap\mathbb{Z}
we have

(D_{z}a_{z})(k)=(D_{z}b_{z})(k)=(D_{z}c_{z})(k)=0\,.

The key point about the factors
(1-(\cdot)^{L})^{3}
,
\alpha_{L}(\cdot)
and
\beta_{L}(\cdot)
is that they do not depend on
k
, and hence commute with the difference operator
D_{\zeta}
.

To prove the lower bound (
116
), we cover the complex sphere in
\mathbb{C}^{6m}
by
2m
pieces as

\Big\{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{6m}\,:\,|u_{j,r}|^{2}+|u_{j,r+m}|^{2}+|u_{j,r+2m}|^{2}\geq 1/2m\Big\}\,,\qquad j\in\{1,2\},\ 1\leq r\leq m\,,

and prove the desired lower-bound on each piece separately. We will assume from now on that

|u_{1,m}|^{2}+|u_{1,2m}|^{2}+|u_{1,3m}|^{2}\geq 1/2m\,.

as all other cases will follow from the same argument. Denote by
D:\mathbb{C}^{|P|}\to\mathbb{C}^{|P|}
the matrix associated to the linear operator

D=D_{z_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{z_{m-1}}\circ D_{\overline{z}_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{\overline{z}_{m-1}}\circ D_{\overline{z}_{m}}\,.

By (
9.1
), for all
k\in P
and for all
1\leq r\leq m-1
we have that

(Da_{z_{r}})(k)=(Db_{z_{r}})(k)=(Dc_{z_{r}})(k)=(Da_{\overline{z}_{r}})(k)=(Db_{\overline{z}_{r}})(k)=(Dc_{\overline{z}_{r}})(k)=0\,.

For
r=m
, (
9.1
) implies that

(Da_{\overline{z}_{m}})(k)=(Db_{\overline{z}_{m}})(k)=(Dc_{\overline{z}_{m}})(k)=0\,,

and

\displaystyle(Da_{z_{m}})(k)

\displaystyle=G\cdot a_{z_{m}}(k)\,,

\displaystyle(Db_{z_{m}})(k)

\displaystyle=G\cdot\big[b_{z_{m}}(k)+A\cdot a_{z_{m}}(k)\big]\,,

\displaystyle(Dc_{z_{m}})(k)

\displaystyle=G\cdot\big[c_{z_{m}}(k)+2A\cdot b_{z_{m}}(k)+(B-A/n)\cdot a_{z_{m}}(k)\big]\,,

where

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\big(1-(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{m})^{L}\big)^{3}\prod_{r=1}^{m-1}\Big[\big(1-(z_{m}/z_{r})^{L}\big)^{3}\big(1-(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{r})^{L}\big)^{3}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\alpha_{L}(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{m})+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\big[\alpha_{L}(z_{m}/z_{r})+\alpha_{L}(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{r})\big]\,,

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\beta_{L}(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{m})+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\big[\beta_{L}(z_{m}/z_{r})+\beta_{L}(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{r})\big]\,.

We now want to use the above computations to lower bound the minimal singular value of the linear operator
D\circ(W_{P}+\overline{W}_{P}):\mathbb{C}^{6m}\to\mathbb{C}^{|P|}
. For
k\in P
such that
k+3mL\in P
, we have

\displaystyle(DW_{P}

\displaystyle u_{1}+D\overline{W}_{P}u_{2})_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{G}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\Big(a_{z_{m}}(k)\overline{u}_{1,m}+\big(b_{z_{m}}(k)+Aa_{z_{m}}(k)\big)\overline{u}_{2m}+\big(c_{z_{m}}(k)+2Ab_{z_{m}}(k)+(B-A/n)a_{z_{m}}(k)\big)\overline{u}_{3m}\Big)

\displaystyle=z_{m}^{k}\,\frac{G}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\Big(\overline{u}_{m}+\frac{k}{n}\overline{u}_{2m}+\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\overline{u}_{3m}+A\big(\overline{u}_{2m}+\frac{2k-1}{n}\overline{u}_{3m}\big)+B\overline{u}_{3m}\Big)\,.

Taking the modulus on both sides and summing, while using the fact that
z_{m}\in\Omega_{K}
, we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P\\
k+3mL\in P\end{subarray}}|(DW_{P}

\displaystyle u_{1}+D\overline{W}_{P}u_{2})_{k}|^{2}

(119)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-K}\frac{|G|^{2}}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P\\
k+3mL\in P\end{subarray}}\Big|\overline{u}_{m}+\frac{k}{n}\overline{u}_{2m}+\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\overline{u}_{3m}+A\big(\overline{u}_{2m}+\frac{2k-1}{n}\overline{u}_{3m}\big)+B\overline{u}_{3m}\Big|^{2}\,.

Since
z_{m}
is
n^{\kappa}
-smooth, it is separated from the real axis and our choice of
L\asymp_{m}n
given by (
115
) implies that
|G|^{2}\gtrsim_{m}\gamma^{6m-3}
, and furthermore that

\max\big\{|A|,|B|\big\}\lesssim m/\gamma\,.

Since
|u_{m}|^{2}+|u_{2m}|^{2}+|u_{3m}|^{2}\geq 1/2m
, similar considerations as in the proof of Claim
7.3
in the proof of Lemma
7.1
imply that the sum on the right-hand side of (
119
) is of size at least
|P|\asymp_{m}1
, and hence

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in P\\
k+3mL\in P\end{subarray}}|(DW_{P}u_{1}+D\overline{W}_{P}u_{2})_{k}|^{2}\gtrsim_{m}\frac{\gamma^{6m-3}}{n}\,.

On the other hand, the operator norm of
D:\ell_{2}(P)\to\ell_{2}(P)
is
O(m)
, so the left-hand side of the above is bounded by
\lesssim_{m}\|W_{P}u_{1}+\overline{W}_{P}u_{2}\|^{2}
, and (
116
) is obtained.

It remains to conclude (
114
). Again, the argument is similar to the one presented in the proof of Lemma
7.1
, so we will keep it brief. Consider the sub-matrices
W_{P},W_{1+P},\ldots,W_{n_{0}+P}
composed of rows indexed by the shifted arithmetic progressions
P,1+P,\ldots,n_{0}+P
. If
n_{0}\leq L
, then these matrices are all disjoint. Letting
F
be the
3m
-dimensional diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m},z_{1},\ldots,z_{m},z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}
, we note that
W_{k+P}
and
W_{P}F^{k}
differ by a matrix of norm
\lesssim_{m}k/n^{3/2}
(as they only differ by a dilation by
k/n
and
k(k-1)/n^{2}
in some of the columns, and there is a factor
n^{-1/2}
which multiplies everything). Since
F
is of full rank, a simple application of the min-max principle shows that

\sigma_{3m}(W_{P}F^{k})\geq\sigma_{3m}(F^{k})\cdot\sigma_{3m}(W_{P})

where
\sigma_{3m}(\cdot)
denotes here the minimal singular value. Since
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\in\Omega_{K}
, we have
\sigma_{3m}(F^{k})\gtrsim\exp(-K)
, and (
116
) gives that

\sigma_{3m}(W_{P}F^{k})\gtrsim\frac{\gamma^{3m-3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

Take
n_{0}=c_{m}\gamma^{2m-1}n
for some
c_{m}>0
sufficiently small, and note that the triangle inequality implies that
\sigma_{3m}(W_{k+P})\gtrsim\gamma^{3m-3/2}/\sqrt{n}
for all
1\leq k\leq n_{0}
. The exact same argument also applies to
\overline{W_{k+P}}
, and in fact also to the
6m
block matrix

\begin{pmatrix}W_{k+P}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\overline{W}_{k+P}\end{pmatrix}\,.

Since these progressions are all disjoint, we see that

\|W_{n}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}+\overline{W_{n}(\mathbf{z})}u_{2}\|^{2}\geq\sum_{k=0}^{n_{0}}\|W_{k+P}(\mathbf{z})u_{1}+\overline{W_{k+P}(\mathbf{z})}u_{2}\|^{2}\gtrsim_{m}n_{0}\frac{\gamma^{6m-3}}{n}\gtrsim_{m}\gamma^{8m-4}\,,

which proves (
114
) and we are done.
∎

9.2
Optimal small-ball bounds for tuples

The next thing on our agenda is to prove Proposition
3.13
. For that, the key input is Theorem
9.3
below, which is analogous to Lemma
8.1
from the
m=1
case, and shows that the characteristic function of
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
decays very fast for
\mathbf{z}
which is smooth and separated. This observation will also be key in the proof of Theorem
3.12
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
be a tuple of
n^{\kappa}
-smooth and
\gamma
-spread for some
\kappa\in(0,1)
and
\gamma\geq n^{-{1/30m}}
. Then for any
K_{\ast}>0
and
u\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
with
n^{1/3}\leq\|u\|\leq n^{K_{\ast}}
we have

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\text{Re}(S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|\leq\exp\big(-\log^{2}n\big)

for all sufficiently large
n
depending on
m,\kappa,K_{\ast}
and the distribution of
\xi
.

The proof of Theorem
9.3
, which only requires minor modifications from the analogous result
[
CN21
, Theorem 3.1]
, is provided in Appendix
A
below.

Proof of Proposition
3.13
.

By the Esseen inequality (
25
), we have that

\sup_{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}}\mathbb{P}\Big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in B(\zeta,\delta)\Big]\lesssim\delta^{6m}\int_{|u|\leq\delta^{-1}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\text{Re}(S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|\,{\rm d}m(u)\,.

(120)

We need to bound the integral on the right-hand side of (
120
). For that, we note that for
|u|\leq c\sqrt{n}
with
c>0
sufficiently small we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\text{Re}(S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{n}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\xi_{k}\text{Re}(w_{k}\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{n}\Big|\phi\Big(\text{Re}(w_{k}\,\overline{u})\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(-c\sum_{k=0}^{n}\big|\text{Re}(w_{k}\,\overline{u})\big|^{2}\Big)\leq\exp\Big(-c\sum_{k=0}^{n}\big|w_{k}\,\overline{u}+\overline{w}_{k}u\big|^{2}\Big)\,.

Therefore, by Lemma
9.1
, we have that

\int_{|u|\leq c\sqrt{n}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\text{Re}(S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|\,{\rm d}m(u)\lesssim\int_{|u|\leq c\sqrt{n}}\exp\Big(-c|u|^{2}\gamma^{8m-4}\Big){\rm d}m(u)\lesssim\gamma^{12m-24m^{2}}\,.

On the other hand, by Theorem
9.3
we have

\int_{c\sqrt{n}\leq|u|\leq\delta^{-1}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\text{Re}(S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|\,{\rm d}m(u)\lesssim n^{O(1)}\exp\big(-\log^{2}n\big)=o(1)\,,

which, in view of (
120
), completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

9.3
Gaussian asymptotic for small ball probabilities

Here we will finally give the proof of Theorem
3.12
. For that, we will fix throughout the section a tuple
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
of
1
-spread and
n^{\kappa}
-smooth points, and a rectangle
Q\subset\mathbb{C}^{3m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6m}
as in the statement of Theorem
3.12
, which we systematically write as

Q=[a_{1},b_{1}]\times\cdots\times[a_{3m},b_{3m}]\,.

(121)

We will always assume that the rectangle
Q
is contained in some fixed compact set. Our goal is to show that the probability that
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
falls within the rectangle
Q
, is asymptotically the same as the probability that a Gaussian vector
\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})
, with the same covariance structure, falls in
Q
. We will show that by following an idea from
[
Kon94
]
(see also
[
KS99
]
), which suggests to compare the characteristic functions of
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
and
\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})
in the relevant range. Indeed, we already established such a comparison (given by Lemma
7.4
above), so we only need to show that all that remains in frequency space is negligible, which we do in what follows.

Proof of Theorem
3.12
.

Since the tuple
\mathbf{z}
is kept fixed throughout the proof, we will write for short
S_{n}=S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
and
\Gamma_{n}=\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})
. The proof will follow once we show that for all
n
large enough

\bigg|\int_{Q}{\rm d}S_{n}-\int_{Q}{\rm d}\Gamma_{n}\bigg|\lesssim\frac{m(Q)}{\sqrt{n}}\,,

(122)

where, for a random vector
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{6m}
, we denote by
{\rm d}\mathbf{X}
its law.
Throughout the proof we denote by
{\varepsilon}=n^{-K_{\ast}}
, where
K_{\ast}=M+2
. For
1\leq j\leq 6m
, let
\phi_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a
C^{\infty}
-smooth function with the following properties
2
2

2

To show the existence of such function, simply take
\phi_{j}=\mathbf{1}_{[a_{j}-{\varepsilon}/2,b_{j}+{\varepsilon}/2]}\ast\psi_{\varepsilon}
, where
\psi_{\varepsilon}
is a
C^{\infty}
bump function supported in
[-{\varepsilon}/4,{\varepsilon}/4]
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\varepsilon}=1
.
:

(i)

\phi_{j}(s)=1
for all
s\in[a_{j},b_{j}]
;

(ii)

\phi_{j}(s)=0
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[a_{j}-{\varepsilon},b_{j}+{\varepsilon}]
;

(iii)

|\phi_{j}^{(k)}(s)|\lesssim_{k}{\varepsilon}^{-k}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
k\geq 1
(uniformly in
j
).

Setting
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{6m}\to[0,1]
as

\Phi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{6m})=\prod_{j=1}^{6m}\phi_{j}(x_{j})\,,

we see that
\Phi
is a smooth function such that
\Phi\equiv 1
on
Q
and
\text{supp}(\Phi)\subset Q^{+{\varepsilon}}
. Let
\widehat{\Phi}
be the Fourier transform of
\Phi
, normalized as

\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi(x)\,\overline{e(\langle\xi,x\rangle)}\,{\rm d}m(x)\,.

Then, by combining item (iii) with integration by parts, we have

\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{6m}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)|\lesssim_{k,m}{\varepsilon}^{-k}(1+|\xi|^{k})^{-1}

(123)

for all
k\geq 1
. To prove (
122
), it will by sufficient to show that

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}S_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}\Gamma_{n}\bigg|\lesssim\frac{m(Q)}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

(124)

Indeed, let
\mathcal{M}
be a covering of
Q^{+{\varepsilon}}\setminus Q
with balls of radius
{\varepsilon}
. Then by Proposition
3.13
we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}S_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\mathbf{1}_{Q}\,{\rm d}S_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\text{supp}(\Phi)\setminus Q\big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in B\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{B\in\mathcal{M}}m(B)\lesssim m\big(Q^{+{\varepsilon}}\setminus Q\big)\lesssim{\varepsilon}\max_{1\leq j\leq 6m}|b_{j}-a_{j}|\lesssim n^{-1/2}m(Q)\,.

A similar bound holds when
S_{n}
is replaced by
\Gamma_{n}
, so indeed (
124
) implies (
122
).

To prove (
124
) we use the Parseval formula and see that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}S_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}\Gamma_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)\Big(\widehat{S}_{n}(\xi)-\widehat{\Gamma}_{n}(\xi)\Big)\,{\rm d}m(\xi)

where
\widehat{S}_{n},\widehat{{\Gamma}}_{n}
denote the characteristic functions of
S_{n}
and
{\Gamma}_{n}
. Since
\displaystyle|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\leq 2m(Q)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{6m}
, we have that

\displaystyle\bigg|

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}S_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}\Gamma_{n}\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)|\,\big|\widehat{S}_{n}(\xi)-\widehat{\Gamma}_{n}(\xi)\big|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)

(125)

\displaystyle\lesssim m(Q)\int_{|\xi|\leq n^{1/3}}\big|\widehat{S}_{n}(\xi)-\widehat{\Gamma}_{n}(\xi)\big|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)+\int_{|\xi|\geq n^{1/3}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)||\widehat{S}_{n}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)+\int_{|\xi|\geq n^{1/3}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)||\widehat{\Gamma}_{n}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\,.

By Lemma
9.1
, we know that the covariance matrix of
S_{n}
is uniformly non-singular (as we assume that the tuple
\mathbf{z}
is
1
-spread). Thus, we can apply Lemma
7.4
and get that

\int_{|\xi|\leq n^{1/3}}\big|\widehat{S}_{n}(\xi)-\widehat{\Gamma}_{n}(\xi)\big|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{|\xi|\leq n^{1/3}}e^{-c|\xi|^{2}}|\xi|^{3}{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

Furthermore, by Theorem
9.3
we have

\int_{|\xi|\geq n^{1/3}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)||\widehat{S}_{n}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim e^{-\log^{2}n}\int_{|\xi|\geq n^{1/3}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bound_on_decay_of_fourier_transform_of_Phi_depnding_of_eps}}}{{\lesssim}}e^{-\log^{2}n}{\varepsilon}^{-O_{m}(1)}\,,

and similarly

\int_{|\xi|\geq n^{1/3}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)||\widehat{\Gamma}_{n}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim e^{-cn^{2/3}}\int_{|\xi|\geq n^{1/3}}|\widehat{\Phi}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}m(\xi)\lesssim e^{-cn^{2/3}}{\varepsilon}^{-O_{m}(1)}\,.

Plugging the above bounds into (
9.3
) gives

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}S_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{6m}}\Phi\,{\rm d}\Gamma_{n}\bigg|\lesssim\frac{m(Q)}{\sqrt{n}}+e^{-\log^{2}n}n^{O_{m,K_{\ast}}(1)}

which shows that (
124
) holds for all
n
large enough, and we are done.
∎

9.4
Approximating the event
A_{z}(U)

We conclude this section with the proof of Lemma
6.1
from Section
6
. Recall that the events
A_{z}(U)
and
A_{z}^{\pm}(U)
are given by (
6
). We want to show that for a tuples
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in(\Omega_{K})^{m}
which is
n^{7\tau}
-smooth and
n^{\beta}
-spread the probability
\mathbb{P}\big[\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}A_{z_{j}}(U)\big]
asymptotically scales like the corresponding product of Gaussian probabilities. In view of Theorem
3.12
and Proposition
3.13
we just proved, this will follow once we show the event
A_{z}(U)
can be well approximated by disjoint union of small rectangles, in which we have a local CLT for
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

For simplicity we will only consider the case
A_{z}(U)
; the proof for the events
A_{z}^{+}(U)
and
A_{z}^{-}(U)
follows the exact same steps with minor modifications. For the tuple
\mathbf{z}
and the finite union of intervals
U\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we let
\mathbb{A}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6m}
be the domain such that the event

\Big(\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}A_{z_{j}}(U)\Big)\bigcap\Big(\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z_{j})|\leq n^{5/2}\log^{2}n\}\Big)

occurs if and only if
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{A}
, where
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
is given by (
32
). Then
\mathbb{A}
is a compact Lipschitz domain, and we already observed in Section
6
(see the proof of Lemma
6.2
, and also the computations in Section
10
below) that

\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\lesssim m_{6m}(\mathbb{A})\lesssim\Big(\frac{1}{n^{3/2+2\beta}}\Big)^{m}\log^{O(1)}n

where here
m_{6m}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{6m}
. Therefore, there exists
M\geq 1
large enough so that we can cover
\mathbb{A}
with cubes from
n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}
and have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\cap\partial\mathbb{A}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}m_{6m}(Q)\lesssim n^{-1}m_{6m}(\mathbb{A}).

The triangle inequality shows that

\Big|\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{A}\big]-\mathbb{P}\big[{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{A}\big]\Big|=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{A}}\big({\rm d}S_{n}(\mathbf{z})-{\rm d}\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{z})\big)\Big|\\
\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\subset\mathbb{A}\end{subarray}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]-\mathbb{P}\big[{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]\Big|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\cap\partial\mathbb{A}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]+\mathbb{P}\big[{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]\Big\}\,.

By Theorem
3.12
, we know that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\subset\mathbb{A}\end{subarray}}\Big|\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]-\mathbb{P}\big[{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]\Big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\subset\mathbb{A}\end{subarray}}m_{6m}(Q)\leq\frac{m_{6m}(\mathbb{A})}{\sqrt{n}}\,,

whereas by Proposition
3.13
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\cap\partial\mathbb{A}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big[S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]+\mathbb{P}\big[{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathbf{z})\in Q\big]\Big\}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in n^{-M}\mathbb{Z}^{6m}\\
Q\cap\partial\mathbb{A}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}m_{6m}(Q)\lesssim n^{-1}m_{6m}(\mathbb{A})\,.

Since we also have that

\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\{|f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z_{j})|\leq n^{5/2}\log^{2}n\}\Big)^{c}\Big]\leq\exp\Big(-c\log^{2}n\Big)

(this follows formally from Lemma
3.3
, but can also be derived directly from the sub-Gaussian assumption on the coefficients
\{\xi_{k}\}
), we conclude that

\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z_{1}}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}(U)\big]=\big(1+o(1)\big)\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z_{1}}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}(U)\big]\,.

(126)

To finish the proof of the lemma, we need to further show that

\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z_{1}}(U)\cap\ldots\cap A_{z_{m}}(U)\big]=\big(1+o(1)\big)\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}_{{\sf G}}\big[A_{z_{j}}(U)\big]

(127)

as
n\to\infty
. Indeed, (
127
) follows from a standard argument for decorrelation of Gaussian fields. For every
n^{\beta}
-separated tuple
\mathbf{z}
, recall the matrix
W_{n}=W_{n}(\mathbf{z})
given by (
112
) and that
W_{n}^{{\sf T}}W_{n}
is the covariance matrix of
{\Gamma}_{n}(\mathbf{z})
. Lemma
9.1
implies that

W_{n}^{{\sf T}}W_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma(z_{1})&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\Sigma(z_{2})&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\Sigma(z_{m})\end{pmatrix}+R

(128)

where
\Sigma(z)
, which is given explicitly by (
135
) below, is the limiting covariance matrix of
{\Gamma}_{n}(z)
for
z\in\Omega_{K}
, and
R
is a matrix with all entries
O(n^{-\beta})
. Since the matrices
W_{n}^{{\sf T}}W_{n}
and
\{\Sigma(z_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}
are uniformly non-singular as
n\to\infty
, the equality (
128
) implies that

\big(W_{n}^{{\sf T}}W_{n}\big)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma(z_{1})^{-1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\Sigma(z_{2})^{-1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\Sigma(z_{m})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}+\widetilde{R}\,,

where all entries of
\widetilde{R}
are
O(n^{-\beta})
. The asymptotic equality (
127
) now follows from a routine Gaussian comparison for the densities, see for instance
[
Coo+23
, Lemma 3.5]
. In view of (
126
), the proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

10
Computing the limiting intensity

In this section we will assume that the random coefficients of
f_{n}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables, without repeating this assumption throughout. Recall that by
Z\sim N_{\mathbb{C}}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
with
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
and
\sigma^{2}>0
we mean that
Z
is a complex-valued random variable with the density

\frac{1}{\pi\sigma^{2}}\exp\big(-|z-\mu|^{2}/\sigma^{2}\big)\,{\rm d}m(z)\,.

(129)

The proof of Lemma
6.4
consists of a Gaussian small-ball computation, which involves
f_{n}(z),f_{n}^{\prime}(z),f_{n}^{\prime\prime}(z)
evaluated at a point
z\in\Omega_{K}
. We start by recording some simple computations associated with the complex Gaussian density (
129
) that will be helpful throughout. By the Lebesgue differentiation theorem, we have

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{P}\big[Z\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}\big]}{m(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon})}=\frac{1}{\pi\sigma^{2}}e^{-|\zeta-\mu|^{2}/\sigma^{2}}

(130)

for any family of sets
\{\mathcal{V}_{{\varepsilon}}\}
that shrink nicely to a point
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
as
{\varepsilon}\downarrow 0
, see for instance
[
Fol99
, Theorem 3.21]
. We will also use the fact that, for a mean-zero complex Gaussian
Z
and for all
c>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\big[|Z|^{4}e^{-c|Z|^{2}}\big]=\frac{1}{\pi\sigma^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|z|^{4}\,e^{-|z|^{2}(\sigma^{-2}+c)}\,{\rm d}m(z)=\frac{2\sigma^{4}}{(1+c\sigma^{2})^{3}}\,.

(131)

Throughout this section, it will be convenient to apply the change of variables
z=e^{w/n}
and define

F_{n}(w)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}f_{n}(e^{w/n}).

(132)

As
z\in\Omega_{K}
, the variable
w
now parameterizes the rectangle

\widetilde{\Omega}_{K}=\Big[n\log\Big(1-\frac{K}{n}\Big),n\log\Big(1+\frac{K}{n}\Big)\Big]\times\big[0,n\pi\big]\subset\mathbb{C}\,.

The event
A_{z}(U)
given by (
50
), for which we want to compute its probability, can be written in the new coordinate system as

A_{z}(u)=\Big\{\frac{F_{n}(w)}{F_{n}^{\prime}(w)}-ne^{w/n}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{w}\ ,\ \frac{2|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|}{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|}\in n^{-1/4}U\,,\ |F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\geq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}\log n}\Big\}

(133)

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{w}
is the rectangle
[-\frac{K}{M_{1}},\frac{K}{M_{1}}]\times[-\frac{n}{2M_{2}},\frac{n}{2M_{2}}]
around the point
ne^{w/n}
.

10.1
Conditional Gaussian random variables

Given
w\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{K}
which we write as
w=x+iy
the random vector

\big(F_{n}(w),F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\big)

(134)

is a mean-zero Gaussian random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{6}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}
. We first recall some basic properties of the multivariate complex Gaussian distribution, as presented in
[
Pic96
]
. A mean-zero complex Gaussian random vector
\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{X}+i\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
can be described via 2 parameters:

\Sigma=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{Z}\cdot\mathbf{Z}^{H}\big]\qquad\text{and }\qquad R=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{Z}\cdot\mathbf{Z}^{T}\big]

where
\mathbf{Z}^{T}
is the matrix transpose of
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{H}
denotes the conjugate transpose. The matrix
\Sigma
is Hermitian and non-negative is referred to as the
complex covariance matrix
, while the symmetric matrix
R
is referred to as the
relation matrix
. For the complex vector (
134
), the complex covariance matrix is given as

\Sigma=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}e^{2x\tfrac{k}{n}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{k}{n}&\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}}\\
\frac{k}{n}&\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}}&\frac{k^{3}}{n^{3}}\\
\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}}&\frac{k^{3}}{n^{3}}&\frac{k^{4}}{n^{4}}\end{pmatrix}\\
=\int_{0}^{1}e^{2xt}\begin{pmatrix}1&t&t^{2}\\
t&t^{2}&t^{3}\\
t^{2}&t^{3}&t^{4}\end{pmatrix}{\rm d}t+O(n^{-1})\,.

(135)

Recall the definition (
75
) of the functions
a_{j}(x)
from Section
6
, given as

a_{j}=a_{j}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}t^{j}e^{2xt}{\rm d}t\,.

The above takes the form of

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&a_{1}&a_{2}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{4}\end{pmatrix}+O(n^{-1})

uniformly for
w\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{K}
. On the other hand, the relation matrix for the complex vector (
134
) tends to zero as
n\to\infty
, uniformly for
z=e^{w/n}
as in the assumptions of Lemma
6.4
. Indeed, the later remark follows from the simple relation

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{k^{\ell}}{n^{\ell}}\,e^{2\tfrac{k}{n}w}=o(1)\,,\qquad\ell\in\{0,\ldots,4\}

which in turn follows from our assumption that
\arg(z)\in[n^{-1+4\tau},n^{-1+4\tau}]
. To compute the probability of the event (
133
) we will need to first state two Gaussian conditional laws, as described below. As always, to compute these Gaussian conditional laws we use the Schur complement of the covariance and relation matrices, see
[
Pic96
, Section IV]
for the relevant background.

(A)
F_{n}^{\prime}(w)
conditional on
F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)
is a complex Gaussian random variable given by the density (
129
) with mean

\mu=\frac{a_{3}}{a_{4}}F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\,,

and variance

\sigma^{2}=\Big(a_{2}-\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}}\Big)\big(1+o(1)\big)\,.

(B)
F_{n}(w)
conditional on
\{F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\}
is a complex Gaussian random variable with mean

\mu=\frac{1}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{4}&-a_{3}\\
-a_{3}&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}F_{n}^{\prime}(w)\\
F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\end{pmatrix}\,,

and variance

\sigma^{2}=a_{0}-\frac{1}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{4}&-a_{3}\\
-a_{3}&a_{2}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\end{pmatrix}+o(1)=\sigma_{1}^{2}+o(1)\,,

where

\sigma_{1}^{2}=a_{0}-\frac{a_{1}^{2}a_{4}-2a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}+a_{2}^{3}}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}\,.

10.2
The intensity function
\mathfrak{F}

We wish to compute the leading order asymptotic for the small-ball event (
133
). Denote by

\mathcal{E}_{\ast}=\Big\{\max_{w\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{K}}|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|\leq\log^{3}(n)\Big\}\,,

and note that by Lemma
3.3
(applied both for
F_{n}(w)
and
F_{n}(w^{-1})
) we have
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}_{\ast}^{c}\big]\leq\exp(-c\log^{2}n)
, uniformly for
K>0
in a compact.

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

In view of the above, it will be enough to compute
\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\big]
, which we start with computing the conditional probability

\mathbb{P}\Big[\frac{F_{n}(w)}{F_{n}^{\prime}(w)}-ne^{w/n}\in\widetilde{\mathsf{R}}_{w}\mid\{F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\}\Big]\\
=\mathbb{P}\bigg[F_{n}(w)\in F_{n}^{\prime}(w)\cdot\Big[-\frac{K}{M_{1}},\frac{K}{M_{1}}\Big]\times\Big[-\frac{n}{2M_{2}},\frac{n}{2M_{2}}\Big]\,\mid\{F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\}\bigg]\,.

Recall that
M_{1}=\lceil 4K/(\delta n)\rceil
and
M_{2}=\lceil 4/\delta\rceil
, where
\delta=n^{-5/4-\beta}
. On the event
\big\{\frac{2F_{n}^{\prime}(w)}{F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)}\in n^{-1/4}U\big\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\ast}
, we have
|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\lesssim n^{-1/4}\log^{3}(n)
for all
w\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{K}
, and hence

\max\Big\{|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|M_{1}^{-1},n|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|M_{2}^{-1}\Big\}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0\,.

We also note that by item (B) from the previous section the conditional mean of
F_{n}(w)
given
\{F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\}
is

F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\frac{a_{2}^{2}-a_{1}a_{3}}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}+o(1)\,.

Denoting by
\mu_{1}=\frac{a_{2}^{2}-a_{1}a_{3}}{a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}}
, we see from (
130
) that

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\ast}}\mathbb{P}\bigg[F_{n}(w)\in F_{n}^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big[-\frac{K}{M_{1}},\frac{K}{M_{1}}\Big]\times\Big[-\frac{n}{2M_{2}},\frac{n}{2M_{2}}\Big]\,\mid\{F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\}\bigg]

\displaystyle=2n\cdot\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\,|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\frac{1}{\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}}\,\Big)\big(1+o(1)\big)\,.

(136)

Next, we want to compute the leading order asymptotic of the conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}\Big[|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\in\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|}{2}\,n^{-1/4}U\}}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\geq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}\log n}\}}\mid F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\Big]

as
n\to\infty
. Indeed, by item (A) from the previous section we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[

\displaystyle|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\in\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|}{2}\,n^{-1/4}U\}}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\geq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}\log n}\}}\mid F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{a_{4}}{\pi\big(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}\big)}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2})}\Big)\cdot\Big(\int_{\{|z|\in\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|}{2}\,n^{-1/4}U\}}|z|^{2}\,{\rm d}m(z)\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{4}}{2^{4}n}\cdot\frac{a_{4}}{\pi\big(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}\big)}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2})}\Big)\cdot\Big(\int_{\{|z^{\prime}|\in U\}}|z^{\prime}|^{2}\,{\rm d}m(z^{\prime})\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{4}}{2^{3}n}\cdot\frac{a_{4}}{\big(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}\big)}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2})}\Big)\cdot\Big(\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)\,,

where in the second equality we applied the change of variables
z=\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|}{2}\,n^{-1/4}z^{\prime}
. Combining this computation with (
10.2
), we see that the law of total expectation yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\ast}}\mid F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\Big]

(137)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\ast}}\mid\big\{F_{n}^{\prime}(w),F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\big\}\big]\mid F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\Big]

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{2nK}{M_{1}M_{2}}\frac{1}{\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}}\,\Big)\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|F_{n}^{\prime}(w)|\in\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|}{2}\,n^{-1/4}U\}}\mid F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\Big]\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\ast}}

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\frac{|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{4}}{4\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}}\frac{a_{4}}{\big(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}\big)}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\Big\{\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}}+\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2})}\Big\}\Big)\cdot\Big(\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\ast}}\,.

Finally, we apply (
131
) with

c=\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}}+\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2})}

to get that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{4}\exp\Big(-|F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)|^{2}\Big\{\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}}+\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2})}\Big\}\Big)\bigg]=\frac{2a_{4}^{4}}{(1+ca_{4}^{2})^{3}}\,.

It remains to recall that
\mathfrak{F}
is given by (
78
) and note the relations

\mu_{1}^{2}=\frac{\Delta_{1}^{2}}{\Delta_{2}^{2}}\,,\quad\sigma_{1}^{2}=\frac{\eta}{\Delta_{2}}\,,\quad c=\frac{1}{\Delta_{2}}\Big(\frac{\Delta_{1}^{2}}{\eta}+\frac{a_{3}^{2}}{a_{4}}\Big)\,,

where
\Delta_{1}=a_{1}a_{3}-a_{2}^{2}
,
\Delta_{2}=a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{3}
and
\eta
is given by (
77
). In fact, the formula for
\sigma_{1}^{2}
can also be seen from (
80
) above. Now, some simple algebra yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\ast}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{A_{z}(U)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\ast}}\mid F_{n}^{\prime\prime}(w)\big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}}\frac{1}{\big(a_{2}a_{4}-a_{3}^{2}\big)}\frac{a_{4}^{5}}{(1+ca_{4}^{2})^{3}}\,\Big(\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{K}{M_{1}M_{2}}\,\mathfrak{F}(x)\,\Big(\int_{U}t^{3}{\rm d}t\Big)\,,

and we are done.
∎

Appendix A
Decay of characteristic function for smooth tuples

The goal of this appendix is to prove Theorem
9.3
, which states that the characteristic function on
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
decays very fast for tuples
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
which are smooth and separated. The idea of the proof is similar to the one used to prove Lemma
3.9
, only that here our situation is technically more complicated as we need to deal with
m
points and another derivative. For
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
we denote by

\psi(k)=\psi(k;\mathbf{z},\eta)=\text{Re}\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{m}z_{j}^{k}\Big(\eta_{j}+\frac{k}{n}\eta_{j+m}+\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\eta_{j+2m}\Big)\Big]\,.

(138)

The following proposition, which is analogous to Lemma
8.1
, is the key result of this appendix.

Proposition A.1
.

For an
n^{\kappa}
-smooth and
\gamma
-spread tuple
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega_{K}^{m}
, with
\gamma\geq n^{-{1/30m}}
and for all
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
with
n^{-1/6}\leq|\eta|\leq n^{K_{\ast}}
we have

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|\psi(k)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\geq\log^{3}n

for all
n\geq n_{0}(K_{\ast},K,m,\kappa)
large enough.

We first show how Proposition
A.1
implies Theorem
9.3
.

Proof of Theorem
9.3
.

We recall a basic inequality for the characteristic function of a sum of independent random vectors (see, for instance
[
CN21
, Eq. (9.2)]
):

\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[e\big(\text{Re}(S_{n}(\mathbf{z})\,\overline{u})\big)\Big]\Big|\leq\exp\bigg(-c\inf_{a_{1}\leq a\leq a_{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|\psi(k;\mathbf{z},au/\sqrt{n})\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\bigg)

(139)

In fact, we already used this inequality in the course of the proof of Lemma
3.9
. There, we were dealing with the symmetric random coefficients law
\eta_{0}
instead of the present
\xi_{0}
, so to prove (
139
) we need to first symmetrize the random coefficients and then use the non-degeneracy and apply (
102
). The extra symmetrization needed here only changes the value of the numerical constant
c>0
in (
139
). As the constants
a_{1},a_{2}\asymp 1
depend only on the distribution of
\xi_{0}
, we can apply Proposition
A.1
with
\eta=au/\sqrt{n}
, which is in the correct range. By (
139
), this implies the desired bound on the characteristic function of
S_{n}(\mathbf{z})
.
∎

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving Proposition
A.1
.
Throughout this section we will denote by
T=\log^{3}n
and suppose towards a contradiction that

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|\psi(k)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}<T\,.

(140)

By Markov’s inequality, (
140
) implies that

\#\Big\{k\in[0,n]\cap\mathbb{Z}\,:\,\|\psi(k)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\geq T^{-1}\Big\}\leq T^{3}\,.

Hence, by the pigeonhole principle, there exists an interval
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,n]
of length at least
n/T^{4}
so that

\sup_{k\in\mathcal{J}}\|\psi(k)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq T^{-1}\,.

(141)

The basic idea is that (
141
) combined with (
140
) implies that the sequence
\{\psi(k)\}
and its finite differences are all close to integers, which is impossible for smooth
\mathbf{z}
. By the Dirichlet theorem on simultaneous Diophantine approximations (see for instance
[
Cas57
, Chapter 1, Theorem VI]
), there exists
q\in[1,n^{\kappa}]\cap\mathbb{Z}
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
so that

q\,\text{arg}(z_{r})/\pi-s_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(142)

and

\max_{1\leq r\leq m}|s_{r}|\leq\sqrt{m}n^{-\kappa/m}\,.

Thus, for each
1\leq r\leq m
we have

|1-z_{r}^{q}|\leq|1-|z_{r}|^{q}|+|z_{r}|^{q}|1-e(q\,\text{arg}(z_{r}))|\lesssim\frac{K}{n^{1-\kappa}}+2\pi|s_{r}|\lesssim\sqrt{m}\,n^{-\kappa/m}\,.

A.1
Finite differences revisited

For a sequence
g:[n]\to\mathbb{C}
and non-negative integers
h,q
we define the discrete differential of order
h
with step size
q
as before (
105
), that is

(\Delta_{q}^{h}g)(k)=\sum_{t=0}^{h}\binom{h}{t}(-1)^{t}g(k+tq)\,.

Recall the definition of the sequences from Section
9
given by

a_{z}(k)=z^{k}\,,\quad b_{z}(k)=\frac{k}{n}z^{k}\,,\quad c_{z}(k)=\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}z^{k}\,.

A simple computation of some geometric sums and their derivatives shows that

\displaystyle(\Delta_{q}^{h}a_{z})(k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{h}\binom{h}{t}(-1)^{t}z^{k+tq}=(1-z^{q})^{h}z^{k}\,,

\displaystyle(\Delta_{q}^{h}b_{z})(k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{h}\binom{h}{t}(-1)^{t}\frac{k+tq}{n}z^{k+tq}=\frac{z}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\big[(1-z^{q})^{h}z^{k}\big]\,,

\displaystyle(\Delta_{q}^{h}c_{z})(k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{h}\binom{h}{t}(-1)^{t}\frac{(k+tq)(k+tq-1)}{n^{2}}z^{k+tq}=\frac{z^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\big[(1-z^{q})^{h}z^{k}\big]\,.

Denoting by

f_{z,\ell}(k)=(1-z^{q\ell})^{h}z^{k}

for
\ell\geq 1
and
q\in[1,n^{\kappa}]
given by (
142
), we can combine to above identities with (
138
) to get

(\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)=\text{Re}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{z_{j},\ell}(k)\,\eta_{j}+\frac{z_{j}}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\Big[f_{z_{j},\ell}(k)\Big]\,\eta_{j+m}+\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{j}^{2}}\Big[f_{z_{j},\ell}(k)\Big]\,\eta_{j+2m}\bigg]\,,

(143)

for all
\ell,h\geq 1
.

Lemma A.2
.

There exists
h=O_{m,\kappa,K_{\ast}}(1)
such that for all
\ell\geq 1
and for any
k
with
[k,k+h\ell q]\subset J
we have

\Big|(\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)\Big|\lesssim_{m,\kappa}\sum_{s=0}^{h}\|\psi(k+s\ell q)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,.

Proof.

We start with the case
\ell=1
and then use that to conclude the lemma for general
\ell\geq 1
. Our choice of
q
given by (
142
) implies that for all
j=1,\ldots,m
we have

|f_{z_{j},1}(k)|=|z_{j}|^{k}|1-z_{j}^{q}|^{h}\leq e^{K}m^{h/2}n^{-h\kappa/m}\lesssim n^{-h\kappa/m}\,.

Furthermore, Cauchy’s estimates (
13
) gives that

\max\Big\{\frac{z_{j}}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}f_{z_{j},\ell}(k)\,,\,\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{j}^{2}}f_{z_{j},\ell}(k)\Big\}\lesssim n^{-h\kappa/m}\,,

and hence

\Big|(\Delta_{q}^{h}\psi)(k)\Big|\lesssim n^{-h\kappa/m}\sum_{j=1}^{3m}|\eta_{j}|\lesssim n^{K_{\ast}-h\kappa/m}\,.

Letting
m(k)
be the closest integer to
\psi(k)
, the triangle inequality together with (
141
) implies that

\Big|(\Delta_{q}^{h}m)(k)\Big|\lesssim n^{K_{\ast}-h\kappa/m}+\Big|(\Delta_{q}^{h}(m-\psi))(k)\Big|\leq n^{K_{\ast}-h\kappa/m}+2^{h}\,T^{-1}\,,

as long as
[k,k+hq]\subset\mathcal{J}
. Taking
h=\lfloor K_{\ast}m/\kappa\rfloor+2
, we see that for
n
large enough the right-hand side is smaller than
1
, and it follows that

(\Delta_{q}^{h}m)(k)=0\,.

This proves the desired bound for
\ell=1
, as

\Big|(\Delta_{q}^{h}\psi)(k)\Big|=\Big|(\Delta_{q}^{h}\psi)(k)-(\Delta_{q}^{h}m)(k)\Big|\lesssim 2^{h}\sum_{s=0}^{h}\|\psi(k+sq)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,.

For
\ell\geq 2
, repeated application of the above for
k
running through progressions starting from
k_{0},k_{0}+q,k_{0}+2q,\ldots,
gives that for any
k\in\mathcal{J}
such that
[k,k+hq]\subset\mathcal{J}
there exists a polynomial
Q_{k}
of degree at most
h-1
so that

m(k+tq)=Q_{k}(t)\,,

for all
t\geq 0
such that
[k,k+tq]\subset\mathcal{J}
. Thus, we also have
(\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}m)(k)=0
for all
\ell\geq 1
and for all
k
such that
[k,k+\ell hq]\subset\mathcal{J}
. The triangle inequality implies that for all such
k
’s we have

\Big|(\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)\Big|=\Big|(\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)-(\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}m)(k)\Big|\lesssim 2^{h}\sum_{s=0}^{h}\|\psi(k+s\ell q)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,,

as desired.
∎

A.2
Canceling out variables

Recall our assumption that
|\eta|\geq n^{-1/6}
, where
\eta=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{3m})\in\mathbb{C}^{3m}
. The proof of Proposition
A.1
splits into three cases:

1.

There exists
j\in\{2m+1,\ldots,3m\}
such that
|\eta_{j}|\geq n^{-1/4}
; or

2.

For all
j\in\{2m+1,\ldots,3m\}
we have
|\eta_{j}|<n^{-1/4}
, and there exists
j^{\prime}\in\{m+1,\ldots,2m\}
such that
|\eta_{j^{\prime}}|\geq n^{-1/5}
; or

3.

For all
j\in\{m+1,\ldots,3m\}
we have
|\eta_{j}|<n^{-1/5}
, which implies that for some
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
we have
|\eta_{j^{\prime}}|\gtrsim_{m}n^{-1/6}
.

In what follows, we shall deal mostly with the first case (which is the most technical), and comment in the end of the section what are the necessary adaptations for the other two cases, see Remark
A.4
. From here on now, we shall assume without loss of generality that

|\eta_{3m}|\geq n^{-1/4}\,.

Lemma A.3
.

For any positive integers
k,\ell,L,L^{\prime}
such that
[k,k+h\ell q+3(m-1)L+2L^{\prime}]\subset\mathcal{J}
with
h\geq 1
as in Lemma
A.2
, we have

\frac{(L^{\prime})^{2}}{n^{2}}\bigg|\eta_{3m}\big(1-z_{m}^{\ell q}\big)^{h}\big(1-(z_{m}/|z_{m}|)^{2L^{\prime}}\big)^{3}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big(1-\Big(\frac{z_{m}}{z_{r}}\Big)^{L}\Big)^{3}\Big(1-\Big(\frac{z_{m}}{\overline{z}_{r}}\Big)^{L}\Big)^{3}\bigg|\\
\lesssim_{\kappa,m,K_{\ast}}\sum_{s=0}^{h}\sum_{a=0}^{6(m-1)}\sum_{b=0}^{4}\|\psi(k+s\ell q+aL+bL^{\prime})\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,.

(144)

Proof.

The method of proof is similar to the one applied in the proof of Lemma
9.1
, namely, we will use repeated applications of the twisted differences to cancel out terms from (
143
). Recall that for
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
the third-order discrete differential operator with step size
L\geq 1
is given by (
117
), i.e.

(D_{\zeta}f)(k)=\sum_{t=0}^{3}\binom{3}{t}(-1)^{t}\zeta^{-tL}f(k+tL)\,.

Recalling that
f_{z,\ell}(k)=(1-z^{q\ell})^{h}z^{k}
, the computation (
9.1
) shows that

(D_{\zeta}f_{z,\ell})(k)=\big(1-(z/\zeta)^{L}\big)^{3}f_{z,\ell}(k)\,.

(145)

As in the proof of Lemma
9.1
, denote by

D=D_{z_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{z_{m-1}}\circ D_{\overline{z}_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{\overline{z}_{m-1}}\circ D_{\overline{z}_{m}}\,.

As derivative and sum commute, we see from (
143
) that

(D\circ\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)=\text{Re}\bigg[(Df_{z_{m},\ell})(k)\,\eta_{m}+\frac{z_{m}}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{m}}\Big[(Df_{z_{j},\ell})(k)\Big]\,\eta_{2m}+\frac{z_{m}^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{m}^{2}}\Big[(Df_{z_{m},\ell})(k)\Big]\,\eta_{3m}\bigg]\,.

(146)

With the aid of the linear operator
D
, we have turned a sum of
3m
terms for
\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi
into a sum of
3
term. Now we want to get rid of the real part. Indeed, let
\delta_{L}(s)=(1-s^{L})
and note that (
145
) implies that

(Df_{z_{m},\ell})(k)=\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big[\delta_{L}(z_{m}/z_{r})\delta_{L}(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{r})\Big]^{3}\,\bigg)\cdot f_{z_{m},\ell}(k)\,.

Let
L^{\prime}\geq 1
be some integer and denote by
\widetilde{D}_{\overline{z}_{m}}
the twisted differential of order
3
with “twist”
\overline{z}_{m}
and step size
L^{\prime}
instead of
L
. As before, we have

(\widetilde{D}_{\overline{z}_{m}}f_{\overline{z}_{m},\ell})(k)=0\,,

and

(\widetilde{D}_{\overline{z}_{m}}\circ Df_{\overline{z}_{m},\ell})(k)=\delta_{2L^{\prime}}(z_{m}/|z_{m}|)^{3}\cdot\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big[\delta_{L}(z_{m}/z_{r})\delta_{L}(z_{m}/\overline{z}_{r})\Big]^{3}\,\bigg)\cdot f_{z_{m},\ell}(k)\,.

Hence, we obtain from (
146
) that

(\widetilde{D}_{\overline{z}_{m}}\circ D\circ\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)\\
=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[H(z_{m})f_{z_{m},\ell}(k)\,\eta_{m}+\frac{z_{m}}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{m}}\Big[H(z_{m})f_{z_{m},\ell}(k)\Big]\,\eta_{2m}+\frac{z_{m}^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{m}^{2}}\Big[H(z_{m})f_{z_{m},\ell}(k)\Big]\,\eta_{3m}\bigg]\,,

(147)

where

H(z)=\delta_{2L^{\prime}}(z/|z|)^{3}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big[\delta_{L}(z/z_{r})\delta_{L}(z/\overline{z}_{r})\Big]^{3}\,.

Note that
H
is a smooth function in a neighborhood of
z_{m}
. The goal now is to take suitable differences from (
147
) to isolate the variable
\eta_{3m}
. Denote by

\widetilde{H}(z)=(1-z^{\ell q})^{h}H(z)\,,

which, as would be important in what follows, does not depend on
k
, and by

F(k)=2\cdot(\widetilde{D}_{\overline{z}_{m}}\circ D\circ\Delta_{\ell q}^{h}\psi)(k)\,.

Then (
147
) reads

\displaystyle F(k)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}(z_{m})z_{m}^{k}\eta_{m}+\frac{z_{m}}{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{m}}\Big[\widetilde{H}(z_{m})z_{m}^{k}\Big]\,\eta_{2m}+\frac{z_{m}^{2}}{n^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{m}^{2}}\Big[\widetilde{H}(z_{m})z_{m}^{k}\Big]\,\eta_{3m}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}(z_{m})z_{m}^{k}\bigg(\frac{k(k-1)}{n^{2}}\eta_{3m}+\frac{k}{n}\mathsf{F}_{1}+\mathsf{F}_{2}\bigg)\,.

Here
\mathsf{F}_{i}
,
i=1,2
are functions that may depend on the variables
\ell,q,n,\eta,\mathbf{z},h
but not on
k
. This representation implies that

F(k)-2z_{m}^{-L^{\prime}}F(k+L^{\prime})+z_{m}^{-2L^{\prime}}F(k+2L^{\prime})=\frac{(L^{\prime})^{2}}{n^{2}}\widetilde{H}(z_{m})z_{m}^{k}\eta_{3m}\\
=\frac{(L^{\prime})^{2}}{n^{2}}H(z_{m})(1-z_{m}^{\ell q})^{h}z_{m}^{k}\eta_{3m}\,.

(148)

Taking the absolute value on both sides of (
148
) and plugging in the formula for
H
, we identify the right-hand side exactly as the left-hand side of (
144
). The proof of the lemma now follows by applying the triangle inequality to the left-hand side of (
148
) and applying Lemma
A.2
to each term in the sum generated by the difference operator
\widetilde{D}_{\overline{z}_{m}}\circ D
.
∎

With Lemma
A.3
at our disposal, we are ready to prove Proposition
A.1
and conclude this section.

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

Recall our assumption that
|\eta_{3m}|\geq n^{-1/4}
and that
\mathcal{J}\subset[0,n]
is an interval of length at least
n/T^{4}
. Suppose that
\ell\geq 1
is an integer such that
\ell hq\leq|\mathcal{J}|/2
, where
h
is as in Lemma
A.2
and
q
is given by (
142
). By Claim
9.2
, we can choose
L\asymp n/T^{6}=o(|\mathcal{J}|)
such that

\qquad\qquad|z_{j}^{L}-z_{j^{\prime}}^{L}|\geq\gamma/T^{6}\,,\qquad\forall 1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq m\,.

Furthermore, since
z_{m}
is
n^{\kappa}
-smooth, we can choose
L^{\prime}\asymp n/T^{7}
such that

\big|1-(z_{m}/|z_{m}|)^{2L^{\prime}}\big|>\gamma^{2}\geq n^{-1/15m}\,.

With these choices of
\ell,L,L^{\prime}
, we have that the left-hand side of (
144
) is at least

\gtrsim_{m}n^{-1/4}T^{-O_{m}(1)}|1-z_{m}^{\ell q}|^{m}\gamma^{6m}\gtrsim n^{-9/20}T^{-O_{m}(1)}|1-z_{m}^{\ell q}|^{m}\,.

On the other hand, since we assume by contradiction that (
140
) holds, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies that

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\|\psi(k)\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq\sqrt{nT}\,,

and by pigeonholing we can find
k\in\mathcal{J}
so that
[k,k+\ell hq+3(m-1)L+2L^{\prime}]\subset\mathcal{J}
so that the right-hand side of (
144
) is
\lesssim_{m,\kappa}n^{-1/2}T^{O(1)}
. Combining, both observations, Lemma
A.3
implies that

\big|1-z_{m}^{\ell q}\big|\leq T^{O_{m}(1)}n^{-1/10m}\,.

Since
q\in[1,n^{\kappa}]
and since
z_{m}\in\Omega_{K}
, we get that for all
n
large enough

\|\ell q\text{arg}(z_{m})/\pi\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq\big|1-e\big(\ell q\text{arg}(z_{m})\big)\big|\leq n^{-1/20m}\,,

(149)

for all integers
\ell=1,\ldots,\lfloor|\mathcal{J}|/qh\rfloor
. It remains to note that for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\|\theta\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq\tfrac{1}{100}
, we have

\|2\theta\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq 2\|\theta\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,.

Iterating this inequality with the observation (
149
) implies that

\|q\text{arg}(z_{m})/\pi\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\lesssim qh|\mathcal{J}|^{-1}n^{-1/20m}=o(n^{\kappa-1})

and we get a contradiction to the fact that
z_{m}
is
n^{\kappa}
-smooth, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark A.4
.

We now explain how to handle the case where
|\eta_{j}|<n^{-1/4}
for all
j\in\{2m+1,\ldots,3m\}
but without loss of generality we have
|\eta_{2m}|\geq n^{-1/5}
. Then, we can show the following simpler analogue of Lemma
A.3
:

Lemma A.5
.

For any positive integers
k,\ell,L,L^{\prime}
such that
[k,k+h\ell q+3(m-1)L+2L^{\prime}]\subset\mathcal{J}
with
h\geq 1
as in Lemma
A.2
, we have

\frac{L^{\prime}}{n}\,\bigg|\eta_{2m}\big(1-z_{m}^{\ell q}\big)^{h}\big(1-(z_{m}/|z_{m}|)^{2L^{\prime}}\big)^{3}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big(1-\Big(\frac{z_{m}}{z_{r}}\Big)^{L}\Big)^{3}\Big(1-\Big(\frac{z_{m}}{\overline{z}_{r}}\Big)^{L}\Big)^{3}\bigg|\\
\lesssim_{\kappa,m,K_{\ast}}\big(1+o(n^{-1/20})\big)\sum_{s=0}^{h}\sum_{a=0}^{6(m-1)}\sum_{b=0}^{4}\|\psi(k+s\ell q+aL+bL^{\prime})\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,.

Here the additional
\big(1+o(n^{-1/20})\big)
term on the right-hand side is caused by estimating from above the contribution of the second-order differential in (
147
) and showing it is negligible compared to the contribution from the first differential. The proof then proceeds the same, just that in (
148
) we only need to take the different of
F(k)
and
z_{m}^{-L^{\prime}}F(k+L^{\prime})
as we want to isolate the term multiplying
k/n
(in particular, on the left-hand side we get the term
L^{\prime}/n
instead of
(L^{\prime})^{2}/n^{2}
, as we did in Lemma
A.3
). Once Lemma
A.5
is established, the proof of Proposition
A.1
proceeds in the exact same way.

Finally, we need to deal with the case where
|\eta_{m}|\gtrsim_{m}n^{-1/6}
, while for all
j\in\{m+1,\ldots,3m\}
we have
|\eta_{j}|\leq n^{-1/5}
. This is the simplest case of the three, as we don’t need to take the extra differences to cancel out unnecessary variables from (
147
). We state the lemma in this case without further comment.

Lemma A.6
.

For any positive integers
k,\ell,L,L^{\prime}
such that
[k,k+h\ell q+3(m-1)L+2L^{\prime}]\subset\mathcal{J}
with
h\geq 1
as in Lemma
A.2
, we have

\bigg|\eta_{m}\big(1-z_{m}^{\ell q}\big)^{h}\big(1-(z_{m}/|z_{m}|)^{2L^{\prime}}\big)^{3}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\Big(1-\Big(\frac{z_{m}}{z_{r}}\Big)^{L}\Big)^{3}\Big(1-\Big(\frac{z_{m}}{\overline{z}_{r}}\Big)^{L}\Big)^{3}\bigg|\\
\lesssim_{\kappa,m,K_{\ast}}\big(1+o(n^{-1/30})\big)\sum_{s=0}^{h}\sum_{a=0}^{6(m-1)}\sum_{b=0}^{4}\|\psi(k+s\ell q+aL+bL^{\prime})\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\,.
